Psychiatric Disorders, Chapter 2

Final Textbook 3, Psychiatric Disorders, full-length edition: chapter 2 of 11. The book goes up chapter by chapter; the A4 colour and black and white PDFs come once all 11 chapters are in.

Chapter 2 Descriptive Psychopathology: Signs and Symptoms

In this chapter
  1. 2.1 What Descriptive Psychopathology Is: Jaspers, Form and Content: explain what descriptive psychopathology is and how Karl Jaspers' phenomenological method works, and classify any symptom using the distinctions between form and content, primary and secondary, sign and symptom, and ego-syntonic and ego-dystonic.
  2. 2.2 Disorders of Consciousness, Attention and Orientation: describe the lowering and the qualitative changes of consciousness from clouding to coma, including delirium, twilight and oneiroid states and stupor, and recognise the disorders of attention and the patterns of disorientation that point to an organic or a dissociative cause.
  3. 2.3 Disorders of Perception: Distortions, Illusions and Hallucinations: distinguish sensory distortions, illusions, hallucinations and pseudohallucinations, and link each hallucination, by sense and by special type, to the disorders it marks, including Charles Bonnet syndrome and the hallucinations of healthy people.
  4. 2.4 Disorders of Thought and Speech: Stream, Form and Possession: classify the disorders of the stream, form and possession of thought and the disorders of speech, telling apart flight of ideas, derailment, tangentiality, circumstantiality and word salad, and thought insertion, withdrawal and broadcasting.
  5. 2.5 Delusions and the Other Disorders of Thought Content: define a delusion, explain primary and secondary delusions and the terms used to describe delusions, link each delusional theme to its disorders, and tell delusions apart from overvalued ideas, obsessions and phobias.
  6. 2.6 The Named Syndromes: Misidentification, Cotard, Othello and the Other Eponyms: recognise the Capgras, Fregoli and other misidentification syndromes and the Cotard, Othello, de Clérambault, Ekbom, Couvade and Diogenes syndromes, with who named them, where they occur and how they are explained.
  7. 2.7 Disorders of Memory: Amnesia and the Paramnesias: describe the forms of amnesia and separate the distortions of recall (confabulation, pseudologia fantastica, retrospective falsification, cryptomnesia) from the distortions of recognition (déjà vu, déjà entendu, jamais vu), with the disorders each marks.
  8. 2.8 Disorders of Emotion: Mood, Affect and Their Vocabulary: separate mood from affect, describe affect along its dimensions, grade elevated mood by severity, and define anhedonia, alexithymia, ambivalence, lability, pseudobulbar affect and the other emotional signs.
  9. 2.9 Disorders of Movement and Will: describe psychomotor change, the catatonic signs and the other abnormal movements, tell mannerisms, stereotypies, tics and compulsions apart, and define avolition, abulia and the other disorders of will.
  10. 2.10 The Self, the Body, Insight and Judgement: describe the disorders of self-experience including depersonalisation and derealisation, the disorders of body image, and the grades of insight and kinds of judgement, with the disorders in which each is lost.

Weightage. 45 of the 314 questions mapped in this book (14.3%) test this chapter, 18 of them from real past papers. 3 of its 10 sections are High yield.

On a June day in 1918 a woman walked into a police station in Paris with an urgent report. A gang of bandits, she said, had kidnapped a great many people, most of them children, and was holding them prisoner in the cellar of her house. She could hear them whimpering and calling for their mother, and there were other prisoners held underground all over the city. She asked for two police constables to come with her and set them free. The police did not send constables. They sent her to the Infirmerie Spéciale, the emergency post to which the Paris police took people they thought mentally disturbed. Two days later she was admitted to the Sainte-Anne asylum, and in the spring of 1919 she was transferred to the asylum of Maison-Blanche, where she came under the care of the psychiatrist Joseph Capgras (1873 to 1950). The medical literature knows her only as Madame M. By the time Capgras presented her case, in January 1923, she was 53, married, and had been ill for about ten years.

What she told Capgras was stranger than the story of the cellars. Her husband and her daughter, she said, had disappeared, and in their places were sosies, the French word for exact look-alikes (it comes from Sosia, the servant in the old comedy of Amphitryon whose shape the god Mercury borrows). The doubles multiplied. Her children had been stolen and replaced, and her little daughter had been replaced again and again by one look-alike girl after another: "I have had more than two thousand in five years," she said. Her real husband had been murdered, and the men who came to visit her were doubles, at least eighty of them by her count. The concierge of her building had doubles, and the other tenants had all been replaced by doubles; in the hospital nearly everyone had them, including the doctors ("don't tell me there is only one of them, I know at least fifteen") and the nurses. She had even gone to law: she wanted a divorce from "this double" and had asked the courts for a separation.

Now notice what she did not say. She did not say that the man who visited her was a stranger with a different face. A double, she explained, is simply a person who resembles another, and the proofs she offered were trifles: a little mark on an ear, a thinner face, a longer moustache. Her eyes worked. Her memory of her husband's face worked. She perceived the visitor correctly and remembered her husband correctly, and yet the conclusion she drew from the two was impossible. In 1923 Capgras and his intern Jean Reboul-Lachaux published her case as L'illusion des "sosies" dans un délire systématisé chronique ("The illusion of doubles in a chronic systematised delusion") in the Bulletin de la Société clinique de médecine mentale. They noted that nothing was wrong with her sensations or with her memory images; what had failed, they thought, was the act of identifying a familiar person. In 1929 the psychiatrist Lévy-Valensi proposed that the condition be called Capgras syndrome, and the name has stayed.

So what kind of disorder was it? A disorder of perception, of recognition, of memory, of feeling or of belief? Capgras and Reboul-Lachaux called it an illusion, a word that today means a misperception of something real, though they added that it was not, strictly, an illusion of the senses but "the conclusion of an emotional judgement". Modern psychopathology files it as a delusion, a disorder of belief, and in 1990 the British psychologists Hadyn Ellis and Andrew Young proposed that what Madame M. had lost was not the face but the feeling of familiarity that normally comes with a known face (Section 2.6 takes up their theory). Whatever the answer, look at how Capgras reached the question. He first described, patiently and in her own words, exactly what she experienced, and only then asked which mental function had gone wrong. That is the method of this chapter, applied to every sign and symptom in psychiatry. Remember Madame M.; you will return to her at the end of the chapter.

2.1 What Descriptive Psychopathology Is: Jaspers, Form and ContentLower yield · 1 Q

It is a busy Monday in the psychiatry outpatient department of a district hospital in north Karnataka, and three people, seen one after another, say almost the same sentence: "My neighbour wants to harm me." Kamala, a 34-year-old tailor, says that every night she hears the neighbour's voice through the bedroom wall, as plainly as she hears the doctor now: "Tonight we finish her." Her husband, lying beside her, hears nothing. Arvind, a 41-year-old bank clerk, has heard no voice and seen nothing, but he is certain. He knew it, he says, on the morning the neighbour parked his car facing Arvind's gate, and nothing his brother says moves him an inch. Fatima, a 27-year-old schoolteacher, is tormented several times an hour by the thought "What if he poisons our water tank?" She knows that the neighbour is a kindly old man, she calls the thought "my stupid thought", and she still climbs to the roof ten times a day to check the lid of the tank.

Three people, one theme. Yet the doctor writes three quite different things in the notes, and the three are heading towards different diagnoses. Kamala is describing a perception that has no source outside her, an auditory hallucination. Arvind is describing a belief held with a certainty that evidence cannot touch, a delusion. Fatima is describing a thought she recognises as her own, knows to be senseless and fights against, an obsession (and her checking is a compulsion). What separates them is not what the experience is about. It is what kind of experience it is. That difference is called the form of the experience, and learning to see it is the single skill on which this whole chapter depends. Before you can see it, though, you need to know where the idea came from and why psychiatry needed it.

The science of describing what the patient lives through

The word psychopathology joins three Greek roots: psyche (mind), pathos (suffering) and logos (study). Clinicians use it in two senses. It can mean the abnormal phenomena themselves ("his psychopathology included voices and grandiose ideas"), or the discipline that studies them. That discipline has several branches, and the one this chapter teaches is descriptive psychopathology: the precise description, definition and classification of abnormal experiences, beliefs and behaviour, as the person lives them and as the clinician observes them, without commitment to any theory of what causes them.

Two parts of that definition deserve a second look. The first is "as the person lives them". Most of what matters in psychiatry cannot be seen. You cannot see a voice, a conviction or a feeling that the world has become unreal; you can only hear the patient's account of it and watch how he behaves. Descriptive psychopathology takes the patient's inner experience as its main material and treats behaviour as the second source. The second is "without commitment to any theory". Whether Kamala's voice comes from too much dopamine, from a buried conflict, from a faulty way of monitoring her own inner speech or from black magic, the description is the same: a voice heard in outer space, in the absence of any speaker. Because the description does not depend on the theory, clinicians who disagree about causes can still agree about what they are looking at.

What descriptive psychopathology produces is a vocabulary, and you already know a good deal of it: hallucination, delusion, obsession, flight of ideas, flat affect, thought insertion, confabulation, waxy flexibility. Each of these words has a precise meaning, fixed by long use. Every heading of the mental status examination (MSE) is written in this vocabulary, every criterion in DSM-5-TR and ICD-11 uses it, and every question at the end of this chapter tests whether you know it. When a DSM-5-TR criterion asks for "delusions" or "disorganized speech", it assumes that the clinician already knows exactly what counts as one.

Why not simply use ordinary language? Because ordinary words for distress are wide, and the distinctions that matter clinically fall inside them. Think of how people use the word "obsessed". A cricket fan who never misses a match and knows every score since 1983 calls himself obsessed with cricket. He loves it, it is part of who he is, and he would not give it up for anything. Fatima's thought about poison is the opposite in every respect: she hates it, it feels senseless and foreign, and she would give anything to be rid of it. In psychopathology only Fatima has an obsession. The same is true of "depressed" (a student is "depressed" after a bad result and cheerful again by the weekend, which is not the mood state of Chapter 5), of "hysterical" (in everyday speech, over-emotional; in the history of psychiatry, the conversion and dissociative states of Chapter 7) and of "hearing things" (which covers the hallucinations of Section 2.3, the half-dreams of falling asleep and simply mishearing a word in a noisy bus). If one clinician writes "obsessed" and means the cricket fan while another means Fatima, their notes cannot be compared, their diagnoses will not agree, and research that pools their patients is meaningless.

That last point is the reliability problem. A diagnosis is reliable when two clinicians who examine the same patient independently reach the same conclusion. Reliability is impossible unless the words that describe the patient mean the same thing to everyone who uses them. Book 2 tells the story of how psychiatry discovered this the hard way: the US-UK Diagnostic Project showed that much of the apparent difference between schizophrenia in New York and in London lay in the psychiatrists rather than in the patients (Book 2, Section 2.1.3), and Book 2, Section 2.10.5 shows how agreement between raters is measured with Cohen's kappa. A shared, precise language of signs and symptoms is the first condition for any reliable diagnosis.

Psychiatry before Jaspers: courses, brains and hidden meanings

At the beginning of the twentieth century psychiatry spoke three languages, and none of them was mainly about the patient's experience. The first was the language of clinical course. Emil Kraepelin (1856 to 1926) followed thousands of patients over years and sorted them by how their illnesses began, progressed and ended, separating dementia praecox, with its downhill course, from manic-depressive insanity, with its recoveries (Book 2, Section 1.3.7; Section 4.1.3). Kraepelin described symptoms carefully, but his unit of analysis was the whole illness over time rather than the single experience at one moment.

The second language was the language of the brain. The German psychiatrist Wilhelm Griesinger (1817 to 1868), whose position is usually summed up in the slogan that mental diseases are brain diseases, had made psychiatry a branch of medicine. His successors, among them the Viennese neuroanatomist Theodor Meynert (1833 to 1892) and the neuropsychiatrist Carl Wernicke (1848 to 1905), built ambitious theories in which each form of madness was explained by a particular disturbance of brain pathways. The trouble was that nobody had seen these disturbances; they were inferred from the symptoms they were meant to explain. Jaspers later called this kind of speculation brain mythology (in German, Hirnmythologie). He was not denying that the brain matters. He was objecting to theories that ran far ahead of anything anyone had observed, and to the habit of describing a patient in the language of imagined brain lesions instead of in the language of what the patient actually experienced.

The third language, newest of the three, was the language of hidden meaning. Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939) and his followers read symptoms as disguised expressions of unconscious wishes and conflicts (Book 2, Section 1.7.3). Psychoanalysis took the patient's inner life very seriously, but it moved quickly from what the patient said to what the analyst believed it meant. Between the brain theorists who described patients in terms of lesions nobody had seen, and the analysts who described them in terms of meanings the patient did not recognise, the plain question "what is this person actually experiencing?" was in danger of going unanswered. That is the gap a young doctor at Heidelberg set out to fill.

Exam trap

Descriptive psychopathology has two defining features: it describes experience as the patient lives it, and it assumes nothing about cause. An option such as "the study of the unconscious causes of symptoms" describes dynamic psychopathology (Section 2.1.10), and "classifying disorders by their brain lesions" describes the brain psychiatry that Jaspers criticised. "Mental diseases are brain diseases" is the slogan associated with Griesinger; "brain mythology" is Jaspers' criticism of speculative brain theories such as Meynert's and Wernicke's, not a view he held.

Karl Jaspers and General Psychopathology

Karl Jaspers (1883 to 1969) was born in Oldenburg in north Germany into a well-to-do family. He began by studying law, gave it up after three semesters, and in 1902 switched to medicine, which he studied in Berlin, Göttingen and Heidelberg. On 1 July 1909 Franz Nissl (1860 to 1919), the director of the Heidelberg psychiatric clinic and a famous neuropathologist (the Nissl stain for nerve cells is his), took him on as a voluntary, unpaid assistant. Jaspers had a chronic lung disease, bronchiectasis, that left him easily exhausted, and within weeks he asked to be spared ordinary ward duties. Nissl agreed. Jaspers spent much of his time in the clinic library and with patients he chose to study in depth, and that unusual freedom shaped what he wrote.

In 1913, at about the age of thirty, he published Allgemeine Psychopathologie (General Psychopathology). The same year he qualified as a university lecturer in psychology in Heidelberg's faculty of philosophy, and from then on his career lay in psychology and philosophy rather than at the bedside. He held the chair of philosophy at Heidelberg from 1922, was forced out of his post by the Nazi government in 1937 (his wife, Gertrud, was Jewish), moved to the University of Basel in Switzerland in 1948 and died there in 1969. Philosophy students know him as one of the founders of existential philosophy, the thinker of "boundary situations" such as death, suffering, struggle and guilt. Psychiatry students know him for the book he wrote before he was thirty-one. The book grew with each edition; the seventh edition of 1959 ran to about 750 pages, and it was translated into English by J. Hoenig and M. W. Hamilton and published by Manchester University Press in 1963. That translation is the "Jaspers (1963)" you will see cited in textbooks such as Barlow and Durand's.

Key study: how General Psychopathology came to be written (1910 to 1913)

In the autumn of 1910 the Berlin publisher Ferdinand Springer invited Karl Wilmanns, a senior psychiatrist at the Heidelberg clinic, to write an introduction to psychiatry. Wilmanns declined, and the invitation passed to his young, unpaid and often ill colleague. Jaspers took it on with enthusiasm. He saw his task as bringing order, at least, into the facts: before anyone argued about causes, the experiences of mentally ill people should be described so clearly that every psychiatrist could recognise the same thing under the same name. He drew on his own patients, on the case reports and textbooks in the clinic library, and on patients' written accounts of their own experiences, which he prized. The first edition of 1913 had 332 pages.

The historian of psychiatry Germán Berrios has pointed out that the book was not an instant success; early reviewers treated it as one more introduction to psychiatry. Its influence grew slowly, through Jaspers' Heidelberg colleagues and above all through his student Kurt Schneider (1887 to 1967), whose short Clinical Psychopathology (1950) distilled Jaspers' ideas for working clinicians and gave the world the first-rank symptoms (Section 4.1.6). Through Schneider and the British textbooks described in Section 2.1.10, Jaspers' categories became the language of the MSE.

What was the method? Jaspers called it phenomenology. He took the word from the philosopher Edmund Husserl (1859 to 1938), whose early descriptive psychology he admired, but he insisted that he was using it in a limited, practical sense: as an empirical method for a clinician, not as a philosophical system. In Jaspers' sense, phenomenology is the attempt to make present to oneself, as vividly and exactly as possible, what the patient actually experiences, to mark off each experience precisely from its neighbours, and to give it a fixed name. Jaspers called this grasp of what an experience is like static understanding: "static" because it captures the experience as it is at one moment, like a single frame, without yet asking where it came from.

How can one person know what another experiences? Jaspers named three sources. The first is careful, patient questioning: asking the person to describe the experience again and again from different angles until its shape is clear. The second is the patient's own written self-descriptions, which Jaspers valued because they are composed at leisure, in the patient's own words, without the interviewer's questions shaping them. The third is observation of behaviour: expression, gesture, posture and speech, which show what the patient may not be able to put into words. Throughout, the clinician must set aside theories and prejudices, both the brain theories and the psychoanalytic ones, and attend only to what is given.

The method also demands an attitude. The clinician is curious, because the aim is to find out what the experience is really like. He is neutral, because the aim is to describe, not to approve or condemn. He is empathic, because only by imaginatively entering the patient's position can he grasp the experience from inside. And he never argues. Telling Arvind that his belief is absurd will not shift it, and it will stop him describing it; asking him "How did you first come to know?" and "How sure are you, today?" opens the door to exactly the information that shows what kind of belief it is.

Clinical link

The three neighbours in the clinic were told apart by a handful of neutral questions about form, never about whether the neighbour really is dangerous. Do you hear it with your ears, as clearly as you hear me? Where does the voice seem to come from? Could anyone else hear it? How did you come to know? Could you be mistaken? Is the thought your own? Do you try to push it away? How to ask these questions at interview, and how to record the answers, is taught in Book 2, Sections 2.5.4 to 2.5.7; this chapter supplies the definitions the answers are sorted into.

Exam trap

Three similar titles: Karl Jaspers wrote General Psychopathology (1913); Kurt Schneider wrote Clinical Psychopathology (1950); Frank Fish wrote Clinical Psychopathology (1967), the British textbook. Descriptive psychopathology and the phenomenological method in psychiatry go back to Jaspers, not to Kraepelin, Freud or Schneider. "Phenomenology" also has other meanings: Husserl's philosophy, the humanistic therapists' focus on the client's subjective world (Book 2), and the loose American usage in which "the phenomenology of depression" simply means its symptom picture. Jaspers appears in exams twice, once as the founder of descriptive psychopathology and once as an existential philosopher (boundary situations).

Understanding, explaining and the limit of understanding

Jaspers is most often remembered for a pair of ideas. The first, understanding (Verstehen), is grasping, by empathy, how one mental state grows meaningfully out of another. The second, explaining (Erklären), is the natural scientist's way of tracing a mental state to causes that have no meaning in themselves, such as a tumour, a fever or a toxin. Jaspers took the contrast from the philosopher Wilhelm Dilthey (1833 to 1911), who had used it to separate the human sciences from the natural sciences, and turned it into a working tool for the clinic. Section 4.1.5 shows how the pair shaped the idea of schizophrenia; here it matters because it supplies several terms that run through the rest of this chapter.

Static understanding, described above, grasps what an experience is. What Jaspers called genetic understanding grasps how it came about. The word "genetic" here has nothing to do with genes; it comes from genesis, origin. You understand a mental event genetically when you can see how it follows, meaningfully, from what went before. A woman who found loving messages from another woman on her husband's phone becomes watchful and suspicious whenever he comes home late. A father whose son was killed on the highway sinks into despair and blames himself for having bought the motorcycle. A clerk humiliated by his manager in front of the whole office stays angry for days. In each case you do not need a theory to see the connection; Jaspers said that such connections carry their own sense of being self-evident. Notice what genetic understanding is not. It is not causal proof. You cannot test whether the messages on the husband's phone really produced the wife's suspicion in the way you can test whether alcohol produced a man's tremor, and an examiner can feel that he understands a connection that was not, in fact, how things happened. Jaspers knew this, and he never claimed that understanding replaces explanation; the two answer different questions.

Now return to Arvind. He has had no quarrel with his neighbour, no loss, no humiliation. He saw a car parked facing his gate and knew, at once and for certain, that the neighbour meant to kill him. You can try as hard as you like to put yourself in his place, and you will not find a path from the parked car to the certainty. Here, Jaspers said, understanding reaches its limit. An experience that cannot be understood from the person's life, personality and situation is a clue that something has broken into the person's mental life from outside the realm of meaning, something that must be explained rather than understood. This is the root of Jaspers' idea of the primary delusion, which Section 2.5 teaches with its four forms.

Jaspers used the limit of understanding to sort the ways in which a mental life can change. A personality development is the gradual, understandable unfolding of a personality over time. Picture a touchy, mistrustful man who has always felt slighted, whose suspicion of his colleagues grows over twenty years through a series of real setbacks and petty injustices: each step can be followed, and the end state, though extreme, is still recognisably him. A reaction is an understandable response to a definite event, whose content reflects the event and which tends to fade as the event recedes, as when a shopkeeper who has been robbed at knifepoint is fearful and suspicious of strangers for some months. (Jaspers grouped such abnormal psychogenic reactions with the neuroses and the abnormal personalities, as variations of human nature rather than diseases.) A process, in Jaspers' sense, is a pathological change that cannot be understood from what went before, that breaks into the life at a particular point and alters the person lastingly. Jaspers regarded schizophrenia as the model of a process: after its onset the person's mental life no longer develops out of his earlier self but runs along new lines.

Key study: Jaspers on delusional jealousy (1910)

One of Jaspers' earliest papers, published in 1910 in the Zeitschrift für die gesamte Neurologie und Psychiatrie, asked its question in its title: Eifersuchtswahn. Ein Beitrag zur Frage: "Entwicklung einer Persönlichkeit" oder "Prozess"? (Delusional jealousy: a contribution to the question "development of a personality" or "process"?). Working through a few detailed case histories of jealous patients, he argued that the same content, the conviction that one's partner is unfaithful, can arise in two quite different ways: as the understandable outgrowth of a suspicious personality and a troubled marriage, or as something new that appears at a definite point and transforms the person. What decides is not what the belief is about but how it arose and how it is held. The paper is the seed of the whole of descriptive psychopathology: look past the content to the form and the genesis.

Criticism: understandability depends on the examiner. A clinician who does not share the patient's culture, language or experience may find "not understandable" what another would grasp at once (Book 2, Section 2.2.9), and cognitive researchers have since argued that many delusions are more understandable than Jaspers allowed (Section 4.9.2). Failing to understand an experience is a clue, never a proof, that a disease process is at work. Jealousy itself, and the Othello syndrome, are taught in Section 2.6.

Exam trap

Understanding is Verstehen (meaningful connections grasped by empathy); explaining is Erklären (causal connections, as in natural science). Static understanding grasps what an experience is like; genetic understanding grasps how one experience grows out of another, and "genetic" here means "to do with origin", not heredity. An experience that cannot be understood points, for Jaspers, to a process; an understandable change is a reaction or a personality development.

Form and content: the central distinction

Everything so far leads to the distinction that Jaspers placed at the centre of psychopathology and that you met with the three neighbours. The form of an experience is its structure: what kind of mental event it is. Is it a perception or an image? A belief or a passing thought? A memory or a fantasy? Is the thought experienced as one's own, or as put there by someone else? Is it welcomed or resisted? The content of an experience is what it is about: the neighbour, poison, God, money, sex, cricket, sin, the Prime Minister. Every abnormal experience has both, and they come from different places.

Figure 2.1 takes a single content, "my neighbour wants to harm me", and shows it in four forms. Kamala's version is a hallucination, a perception without a stimulus (Section 2.3). Arvind's is a delusion, a false belief held with a certainty that no argument shifts (Section 2.5). Fatima's is an obsession, an unwanted thought recognised as her own, known to be absurd and resisted (Section 2.5). The fourth panel adds a man the three neighbours did not include: a retired schoolmaster who has fought his neighbour over a real boundary wall for twenty years, through the courts and in letters to the district collector, until the grievance has swallowed his life. His belief that the neighbour is out to ruin him is not impossible, and it is understandable from what has actually happened, but it dominates his thinking and feeling far beyond its importance. That form is an overvalued idea (Section 2.5).

Where does content come from? From the person's life, culture and era. Delusions of being influenced by a machine are a good example, because the machine keeps changing. In 1810 the London tea merchant James Tilly Matthews believed that a gang worked an "Air Loom" against him, a contraption of the industrial age (Section 4.1.1). Patients of the mid-twentieth century were tormented by radio waves and television sets; patients today speak of mobile towers, Wi-Fi, CCTV cameras, chips implanted during an operation and messages hidden in WhatsApp forwards. In 2012 the psychiatrist Joel Gold and the philosopher Ian Gold described a series of patients who believed that their lives were being secretly filmed and broadcast as a reality show, and named it the "Truman Show" delusion after the 1998 film. Their conclusion is a modern statement of Jaspers' rule: the specific content of delusions is shaped by culture, while the broad categories stay the same across times and cultures. In India, persecutors work through black magic (jadu-tona), an angry planet, a spirit or a relative's envy as often as through technology; a guilty patient speaks of sin, karma or having done something haram according to his faith; a grandiose one is no longer Napoleon but a film star, a cricket captain or the secret adviser of the Prime Minister. The forms (persecution, guilt, grandeur, passivity) are recognisable everywhere.

The practical rule follows. The content tells you about the person: his fears, his history, his culture, the things that matter to him. The form tells you about the disorder. Two patients whose voices say completely different things may have the same illness; two patients with the same worry may have entirely different illnesses, as Kamala, Arvind and Fatima do. That is why diagnosis rests mainly on form. Book 2, Section 2.4.1 states the rule in a paragraph; the sections that follow in this chapter are, in effect, a catalogue of forms.

The rule has limits, and a good clinician respects them. Content is not irrelevant. It guides the assessment of risk: a voice commanding the patient to harm his child, or a delusion that one's wife is unfaithful, changes what must be done today, whatever the form (Book 2, Section 2.7.5). Some content is characteristic of particular disorders: guilt, ruin and nihilism go with severe depression, and grandeur with mania, so that "mood-congruent" content helps diagnosis (Section 2.5). Content is what matters to the patient, and it is the material of psychotherapy. Finally, culture enters the judgement of form itself. A belief in black magic that the whole village shares is not a delusion at all, because a delusion is, among other things, out of keeping with the person's cultural background; telling the two apart is one of the hardest tasks in Indian practice (Section 2.5; Book 2, Sections 1.6.8 and 2.2.9).

Exam trap

Form is the type or structure of an experience (hallucination, delusion, obsession); content is what it is about (the neighbour, poison, God). Culture shapes content; form carries the diagnosis. The same idea runs through the disorders of thought: delusions, obsessions and overvalued ideas are disorders of thought content, whereas derailment, tangentiality, circumstantiality and word salad are disorders of the form of thought, the way ideas are linked (Section 2.4 answers those questions, and explains why some British texts file flight of ideas and circumstantiality under the "stream" of thought). A hallucination is a disorder of perception whatever its content.

Primary and secondary: three meanings of one pair of words

Few words in psychopathology cause more confusion than "primary" and "secondary", because three authorities used them for three different things, and exam questions do not always say which one they mean.

The first meaning is Jaspers'. A primary symptom, in his sense, is one that cannot be traced psychologically to anything else: it is the end of the line of understanding, as Arvind's sudden certainty is. A secondary symptom is one that can be understood as arising from another experience, such as a mood, a hallucination or another belief. Suppose that Kamala, after months of hearing the neighbour's voice through the wall, concludes that he has hidden a microphone and a speaker in the wall to torment her. That conclusion is a delusion, but a secondary one: given the voices, it makes sense. A severely depressed man who becomes convinced that he has ruined his family has a secondary delusion too, understandable from his mood. Section 2.5 applies this meaning to delusions in full.

The second meaning is Eugen Bleuler's (1857 to 1939). In his 1911 book on the group of schizophrenias Bleuler ranked symptoms in two ways: fundamental symptoms, present in every case (the "four As"), versus accessory symptoms such as hallucinations and delusions, which came and went and also occurred in other illnesses; and primary symptoms, the direct product of the disease process, versus secondary ones, the person's psychological reactions to it (Section 4.1.4). For Bleuler, then, voices were secondary, which is almost the reverse of how most people picture schizophrenia.

The third meaning is the everyday clinical one: a symptom produced directly by the illness versus one produced by the person's reaction to the illness or to its consequences. Kamala's sleeplessness is secondary in this sense, because she lies awake listening for the voice; treat the voice and she sleeps. A man with a long, disabling illness who becomes demoralised and hopeless has secondary depression. In schizophrenia, apathy and withdrawal that are really caused by depression, by the side effects of antipsychotic drugs, by frightening positive symptoms or by years in an understimulating ward are called secondary negative symptoms, to separate them from the primary negative symptoms of the illness itself (Section 4.3.3). The distinction matters for treatment, because secondary symptoms often lift when their source is dealt with.

Classification adds a fourth twist. DSM-IV used "primary mental disorder" for a disorder that is not caused by a substance or a medical condition. DSM-5 replaced "primary" with "independent", and DSM-5-TR explains why: "primary" had too many other meanings, including the disorder that appeared first when a person has several. ICD-11, meanwhile, uses "secondary" for mental syndromes caused by a medical condition, such as a secondary psychotic syndrome after a brain injury. So "secondary psychosis" in ICD-11 means a psychosis due to a medical disease, not a psychosis that is understandable from a mood.

Exam trap

Ask which sense is meant. Jaspers: primary means not understandable from anything else; secondary means understandable from another experience (a mood, a hallucination). Bleuler: fundamental versus accessory symptoms, and primary (disease process) versus secondary (psychological reaction). Clinical usage: produced by the illness versus a reaction to it. "Primary" never means "most important" or "most common", and a secondary delusion is no less a delusion.

Signs, symptoms, syndromes and disorders

Medicine has always distinguished what the doctor finds from what the patient feels, and psychiatry keeps the distinction. A sign is objective: something the clinician observes, which another observer could confirm. A tremor, a flat and unchanging facial expression, waxy flexibility of the limbs, pressured speech that cannot be interrupted, tears, a dishevelled appearance and sudden stops in mid-sentence are signs. A symptom is subjective: something the patient reports and only the patient can know directly. Low mood, hearing voices, feeling that the world has become unreal, a sense of dread and a belief that one's thoughts are being taken away are symptoms.

Many phenomena have both an observed side and a reported side, and it pays to keep them apart. Thought block is observed: the patient stops abruptly in the middle of a sentence and cannot pick up the thread. Thought withdrawal is reported: the patient says that the thought was taken out of his head (Section 2.4). The two often go together, but one is a sign and the other a symptom, and the patient who shows the first may or may not report the second. In the same way, hallucinatory behaviour is a sign (the patient pauses, tilts his head, mutters a reply to no one), while the hallucination itself is a symptom (Book 2, Section 2.4.2). Signs have the advantage that they can be checked; they are recorded even when the patient is mute, denies everything or lacks the words to describe his experience. Symptoms carry information no observer can reach, but they depend on the patient's willingness and ability to tell, which can be limited by suspicion, shame, a language barrier, a child's vocabulary or a difficulty in naming feelings (alexithymia, Section 2.8). Book 2, Section 2.4.1 shows how the MSE records both.

Signs and symptoms that tend to occur together form a syndrome, from the Greek for "running together". The catatonic syndrome, the Korsakoff syndrome and the Capgras syndrome are examples. A syndrome is a description, not an explanation: the delirium syndrome of Section 2.2, for instance, can be caused by an infection, a drug, alcohol withdrawal or a failing liver. A mental disorder is a syndrome that has been given defined criteria, with rules about duration, course, distress or impairment and exclusions, so that it can be diagnosed reliably (Chapter 1 discusses what qualifies). Most psychiatric diagnoses are still syndromes in this sense; their causes are not known well enough for them to be called diseases in the strict medical sense of a known pathology.

A sign is pathognomonic when it occurs in one disorder only, so that its presence alone makes the diagnosis. Medicine has a few: the small white Koplik spots inside the cheek are pathognomonic of measles. Psychiatry has almost none. Kurt Schneider hoped that his first-rank symptoms would come close, but they turned out to occur in mania, in other psychoses and in some organic states as well as in schizophrenia (Section 4.1.6). This is one reason why psychiatric diagnosis rests on patterns of signs and symptoms, on course and on exclusions, rather than on any single finding.

One more pair belongs here, though another chapter owns its history. In this pair, positive symptoms are experiences or behaviours added to normal mental life: hallucinations, delusions, disorganised speech. By contrast, negative symptoms are losses or reductions of normal function: flattened expression of emotion, poverty of speech, loss of will and drive, loss of pleasure, social withdrawal. The idea goes back to the nineteenth-century neurologists John Russell Reynolds and John Hughlings Jackson and entered modern psychiatry through the study of schizophrenia (Section 4.3.1). Do not confuse the pair with "good" and "bad": negative symptoms are usually the more disabling.

Exam trap

A sign is observed by the clinician (objective); a symptom is reported by the patient (subjective). Thought block is a sign, thought withdrawal a symptom; hallucinatory behaviour is a sign, the hallucination a symptom. A syndrome is a cluster of signs and symptoms that occur together; it does not imply a known cause. "Pathognomonic" means diagnostic of one disorder only: no first-rank symptom is pathognomonic of schizophrenia.

Continuity with normal life, and the old divide between organic and functional

Is every hallucination a sign of illness, every odd belief a delusion? No, and this is one of the first things a descriptive psychopathologist learns. Many abnormal phenomena have close relatives in healthy life. A self-conscious student who walks late into a lecture hall and hears laughter from the back row may be sure, for a moment, that it is about him: a fleeting idea of reference (Section 2.5). Many healthy people hear their name called, or see a face, as they drift off to sleep (Section 2.3). A doctor at the end of a thirty-hour duty may feel strangely detached, as if watching herself work from a distance (Section 2.10). Most people have felt the eerie certainty that a new scene has happened before, the déjà vu of Section 2.7. In the months after a death, many bereaved people briefly hear, see or sense the person they have lost. Other phenomena have no normal counterpart at all: no healthy person experiences thoughts being placed in the mind by an outside agency, which is why thought insertion carries so much diagnostic weight (Section 2.4).

Two consequences follow. First, the mere presence of a phenomenon rarely settles a diagnosis. What counts is its exact form, the setting in which it occurs, how long it lasts, and the distress and dysfunction it brings, which is the logic of the four Ds of Book 2, Section 1.6.5. Second, the boundary between normal and abnormal is often a matter of degree, and much of this chapter is about where the lines are drawn and why.

The second old division is between organic and functional. Psychiatry once sorted mental disorders into organic disorders, in which a demonstrable disease of the brain or body, a drug or a toxin produced the mental change (delirium, dementia, the effects of alcohol), and functional disorders, in which no such cause could be found (schizophrenia, the mood disorders and the neuroses). Jaspers' own rough grouping of disorders followed this line. The division survives at the bedside because certain descriptive findings really do point to a bodily cause, and each is taught in its own section: a clouded or fluctuating level of consciousness and disorientation (Section 2.2); hallucinations in vision, touch and smell, more than in hearing (Section 2.3); loss of memory for recent events, and confabulation (Section 2.7); and the first appearance of a psychiatric illness late in life, or in close relation to a physical illness, a head injury or a new medicine. When an Indian clinician writes "rule out organicity", this is what he means.

As a theory, however, the division is now thought misleading. It implied that "functional" disorders have no biological basis, and that "organic" ones have no psychological side, and neither is true: schizophrenia and bipolar disorder have well-documented brain correlates, while delirium and dementia have psychological and social dimensions that shape how they are experienced. DSM used the terms "organic" and "nonorganic" up to DSM-III-R. DSM-IV (1994) eliminated them, dividing the old organic disorders into those caused by a substance and those caused by a medical condition; DSM-5-TR keeps this language ("due to another medical condition", "substance/medication-induced"). ICD-10 kept a block called "Organic, including symptomatic, mental disorders" (F00 to F09); ICD-11 speaks instead of neurocognitive disorders and of "secondary" mental or behavioural syndromes associated with other diseases. Chapter 10 teaches these disorders.

Clinical link

A retired teacher of 66, never before unwell, begins to see small children sitting in the corners of her room at night, is muddled about the date and calls her son by her late husband's name. A trainee notes "visual hallucinations" and starts to think of a psychosis. A descriptive psychopathologist notes the late first onset, the visual modality, the evening worsening and the disorientation, every one of them a pointer to a bodily cause, and asks first for a medical examination, blood tests and a review of her medicines. Description is not a formality; it changes what happens next.

Exam trap

"Organic" meant caused by a demonstrable brain or bodily disease, a drug or a toxin; "functional" meant that no such cause was found. DSM-IV (1994) dropped both words because they wrongly implied that functional disorders have no biological basis. Do not confuse the old "functional psychoses" (schizophrenia, mood disorders) with today's functional neurological symptom disorder (conversion disorder, Section 7.8.4), where "functional" means a disturbance of how the nervous system works without structural damage. Visual, tactile and olfactory hallucinations, clouded consciousness, disorientation and recent memory loss suggest an organic cause.

Ego-syntonic and ego-dystonic

Go back to the cricket fan and Fatima. Both, in everyday speech, are "obsessed". The difference between them is not the amount of time they spend on the subject; the fan probably spends more. It is how each of them stands towards the experience. The fan embraces his enthusiasm: it is part of who he is. Fatima rejects her thought: it feels like an intruder in her own mind. Psychopathology has a pair of words for exactly this difference. An experience, impulse, trait or symptom is ego-syntonic when it is experienced as consistent with the person's values, goals and self-image: acceptable, natural, "me". It is ego-dystonic when it is experienced as intrusive, unwanted and alien, inconsistent with the person's values and self-image: "not me", something to be fought or got rid of.

The words come from psychoanalysis, in which the ego is the organised, conscious, reality-facing part of the personality that has to come to terms with impulses, conscience and the outside world (Book 1, Section 2.4; Book 2, Section 3.2; Freud's structural model itself belongs to the Personality book). "Syntonic" means in tune with; "dystonic", out of tune with. In modern descriptive use the terms have lost most of their theoretical baggage. You do not need to accept any part of psychoanalysis to ask a patient, "Does this feel like part of you, or like something foreign that you want to stop?"

Which phenomena fall on which side? On the ego-dystonic side are obsessions, which are the textbook case: a careful driver tormented by the thought that he may have knocked someone down without noticing, though he knows he did not; Fatima and her water tank. Intrusive images and urges of the same kind belong here, as do most phobias (a man who knows perfectly well that the lift is safe but cannot make himself enter it), tics, which DSM-5-TR describes as usually ego-dystonic, and the hair-pulling that a person with trichotillomania struggles against. On the ego-syntonic side are most personality traits, including the traits of personality disorders: the suspicious man who regards himself as rightly careful, the perfectionism of obsessive-compulsive personality disorder (OCPD), which feels to its owner like the only sensible way to work (Section 6.5.4; Chapter 11). Here too belong the pursuit of thinness in anorexia nervosa, which often feels at first like an achievement rather than an illness (Chapter 8); most overvalued ideas, such as the schoolmaster's grievance, which he defends with passion (Section 2.5); and, very often, the expansive grandiosity of mania, which feels wonderful (Section 2.8). DSM-5-TR uses the same contrast to separate the stereotypies of children, which are ego-syntonic ("children enjoy them"), from tics (Section 2.9).

Three points prevent the commonest misunderstandings. First, ego-dystonic is not the same as distressing. A persecutory delusion is terrifying, but Arvind does not regard his belief as senseless or as something he wants to stop believing; he regards the danger as real and wants protection from it. A person with a delusion usually accepts it as true rather than fighting it, so delusions are not ego-dystonic in the way obsessions are. Second, ego-dystonic is not the same as alien in origin. Fatima knows that her thought is her own, however much she hates it; a patient with thought insertion experiences the thought as not his own at all, put there by someone else (Section 2.4). The obsession is "my thought that I reject"; the inserted thought is "not my thought". Third, ego-dystonic symptoms are not, as a rule, psychotic. Most of them belong to the anxiety, obsessive-compulsive and related disorders, in which reality testing is preserved; that is why preserved insight and ego-dystonic symptoms were the classic marks of the neuroses (Section 6.1.3).

Why does the distinction matter so much? Because it predicts who comes for help and how they engage. People with ego-dystonic problems usually seek treatment themselves: the symptom is the enemy, and they want it gone. People with ego-syntonic problems often do not, because they do not experience anything as wrong with them; they are brought by a worried family, an employer or a court, and they tend to locate the problem in other people. Ego-syntonic problems therefore go with poorer insight (Section 2.10), and treatment has to begin by building motivation rather than by attacking the symptom. Syntonicity can also change over time. A young woman whose weight loss once felt like a triumph may come to feel it as a prison, and the moment a symptom turns from syntonic to dystonic is often the moment a person first asks for help.

The history of the terms contains a warning about how they can be misused. In 1973 the American Psychiatric Association removed homosexuality from DSM-II, replacing it with a category for people who were disturbed by, or wished to change, their sexual orientation. DSM-III (1980) renamed this "ego-dystonic homosexuality", and DSM-III-R (1987) removed it. ICD-10 kept a category of "egodystonic sexual orientation" (F66.1), and ICD-11 dropped it altogether. Critics had argued that the distress such people felt usually came from stigma and rejection, not from the orientation, which is not a disorder; a diagnosis built on ego-dystonia alone risked turning social disapproval into psychopathology. Chapter 9 tells the full story.

Exam trap

Ego-dystonic symptoms are experienced as intrusive, unwanted and inconsistent with one's self-image and values (obsessions are the classic example). "Experienced as consistent with one's self-image and values" is the definition of ego-syntonic, the opposite (personality traits, the perfectionism of obsessive-compulsive personality disorder). Ego-dystonic symptoms are not "always psychotic": most are non-psychotic, and a person with a delusion usually accepts it as true rather than resisting it. Nothing in the definition concerns sleep, so "occur only during sleep" is simply wrong (the sleep-related phenomena are hypnagogic and hypnopompic experiences, Section 2.3). In everyday speech "obsessed" often describes an ego-syntonic passion; in psychopathology an obsession is always ego-dystonic.

Descriptive and dynamic psychopathology, and the traditions behind the textbooks

Descriptive psychopathology is not the only way of studying abnormal experience. The psychoanalytic tradition of dynamic psychopathology asks a different question: not "what is this experience?" but "what does it mean, and what conflict or defence does it express?" Take Fatima once more. A descriptive account says that she has an obsession, recognised as her own, experienced as senseless and resisted, with checking compulsions. A classical psychoanalytic account might add that a thought of being poisoned by a neighbour could express hostility she cannot acknowledge, kept at bay by defences such as isolation and undoing (Sections 6.8.8 to 6.8.10; Book 2, Section 3.2). The two accounts are answers to different questions and need not contradict each other. But only the descriptive account can be agreed by clinicians of every school, which is why classification is built on it. Jaspers was as wary of psychoanalysis claiming to decode every symptom as he was of brain mythology.

Two traditions of descriptive teaching reached India, and you will meet both in examinations. The British tradition comes directly from Jaspers and Schneider. Its classic text is Frank Fish's Clinical Psychopathology: Signs and Symptoms in Psychiatry, first published in 1967, revised by Max Hamilton (of the Hamilton rating scales) in 1974 and reissued as a second edition in 1985, and later revised by Patricia Casey and Brendan Kelly (2007 and after). Its companion is Andrew Sims' Symptoms in the Mind, first published in 1988 and continued in later editions by Femi Oyebode. These books use the British headings for thought (stream, form, content and possession), keep Jaspers' distinctions (form and content, primary and secondary delusions, the pseudohallucination) and supply much of the vocabulary of Indian postgraduate teaching. The American tradition appears in the glossary of signs and symptoms in Kaplan and Sadock's Synopsis of Psychiatry and in the "Glossary of Technical Terms" at the back of DSM-5-TR; it tends to speak of thought "process" and thought "content" rather than four headings. Indian question papers sometimes follow one tradition and sometimes the other, which is why a keyed answer may file circumstantiality under "form" in one paper and a British text may file it under "stream" (Section 2.4 sorts this out).

Descriptive psychopathology was also turned into tools. In London, John Wing and his colleagues built the Present State Examination, a semi-structured interview with a glossary that defined every symptom precisely enough for trained interviewers in different countries to rate it the same way; it was used in the World Health Organization's international studies of schizophrenia (Book 2, Section 2.1.4). And in 1980 DSM-III built Jaspers' first principle, describe before you explain, into the official classification: diagnosis was to rest on described signs and symptoms, set out as explicit criteria, and the manual was to stay neutral about causes that were not yet known (Book 2, Section 2.10.3; Chapter 1). Table 2.1 collects the pairs of descriptive terms taught in this section.

Table 2.1 The pairs of descriptive terms, with an example of each.
PairFirst termSecond termExample

Exam trap

Descriptive psychopathology describes and classifies experiences without a theory of cause; dynamic psychopathology interprets their meaning. The British texts are Fish's Clinical Psychopathology (1967) and Sims' Symptoms in the Mind (1988); Kaplan and Sadock and the DSM-5-TR glossary represent the American usage. When an option set mixes "stream", "form", "content" and "possession", the paper is using the British four-heading scheme; check which heading the keyed answer assumes before you choose.

How this chapter is organised

The rest of the chapter takes the mind apart function by function, in roughly the order in which the MSE examines it, and asks of each function the same questions: what is normal, how can it go wrong, what is each abnormality called, who named it, and in which disorders is it found? Figure 2.2 draws the plan as a ring of functions around the person, with this section's method at the centre, and Table 2.2 gives the same map in detail, with the place in Book 2 where each sign is elicited at interview.

How does this chapter differ from Book 2, Sections 2.4 to 2.6, which covered much of the same ground? Those sections taught the clinician's task: how to observe, ask about and test each heading of the MSE at interview, and how to write the findings down. The headings of the MSE in their usual order, with the ASEPTIC mnemonic, are in Book 2, Section 2.4.1 (Table 2.4), and a complete written MSE is in Book 2, Section 2.6.11. This chapter is the reference behind that task. For each phenomenon it gives the definition, the history and who named it, the forms it takes, the disorders in which it occurs and the traps that examiners set, so that when a patient tells you something strange you can name it exactly, as Capgras named what Madame M. told him. We begin with the function on which every other depends, because a patient who is not fully conscious cannot reliably perceive, think or remember: consciousness itself.

Scan Your Brain

  5. Kamala's voice and Arvind's certainty have the same content but differ in their , which is what mainly guides diagnosis.
