Answers: (1) descriptive, (2) 1913, (3) static; genetic, (4) process, (5) form, (6) secondary, (7) accessory, (8) sign; symptom, (9) pathognomonic, (10) ego-dystonic; ego-syntonic.

2.2 Disorders of Consciousness, Attention and OrientationLower yield · not asked yet

Why should a chapter on signs and symptoms begin with consciousness? Because every other finding in it is read through consciousness. A voice heard by a man who is wide awake and clear in his mind means something quite different from the same voice heard by a man who is feverish, drowsy and unsure whether it is day or night. A gap in memory says one thing about a patient who was attending to you and another about a patient who kept drifting off in the middle of your questions. The first questions a clinician asks of any mental state are therefore the most basic ones. Is this person awake? Is he aware of what is going on around him? Can he attend to it? Does he know where he is, when it is and who he is? This section describes what can go wrong with each of those functions, and what each failure tells you about its cause.

What consciousness means in psychopathology

In descriptive psychopathology, consciousness means the state of awareness of oneself and one's surroundings. The deeper questions of what consciousness is and how sleep, dreaming, hypnosis and meditation alter it belong to general psychology (Book 1, Sections 5.5.1 and 5.5.5). The clinician needs something narrower and more practical: a way of describing how much awareness a person has at this moment and what kind of awareness it is.

Two different things are packed into the word, and keeping them apart makes the whole subject easier. The first is the level of consciousness: how awake the person is, his wakefulness or arousal. The second is the content of consciousness, sometimes called its clarity or its field: the range and the quality of what the person is aware of, and how well that awareness is organised. A torch makes a useful picture. The level is how brightly the torch is shining; the content is how wide the beam is and how sharply it shows what it falls on. A torch can grow dim (the level falls, as in drowsiness and coma). It can also stay bright while its beam narrows to a single spot, or while it lights up a scene that is not really there (the content changes, as in the twilight and oneiroid states described below). Disorders of consciousness are therefore of two broad kinds: quantitative changes, in which the level is lowered, and qualitative changes, in which the field is narrowed, distorted or filled with dream-like material. Delirium, the most important of all, is both at once.

DSM-5-TR adopts the same continuum. In its account of delirium it says that normal attention and arousal, delirium and coma lie on a continuum, and it describes delirium as an impaired state of consciousness "in the setting of an aroused cortex": the brain is awake enough to respond, but the awareness it produces is disordered. Coma, at the other end, is a state of unconsciousness with no cognition, no sleep-wake cycle and no meaningful response to voice or touch.

How these states are examined at the bedside has already been taught, and this section builds on it rather than repeating it. Book 2, Section 2.6.2 and its table of levels give the clinical levels from alert to coma, the Glasgow Coma Scale (eye opening scored 1 to 4, verbal response 1 to 5 and motor response 1 to 6, so that the total runs from 3 to 15, with 8 or less taken as coma) and the two meanings of the word stupor. Book 2, Section 2.6.4 gives the tests of attention and concentration (digit span, serial sevens, serial threes, the months or days backwards and the letter-tapping test of vigilance), and Book 2, Section 2.6.3 the questions for orientation and the need to calibrate the questions about time to the person's culture and schooling. What follows describes the states themselves: what they are like for the person in them, what they look like from outside, and which disorders produce them.

The lowering of consciousness: a continuum seen from the inside

Picture the level of consciousness as a staircase. Normal, alert wakefulness is near the top, and each step down takes the person further from the world, until at the bottom he cannot be reached at all. One step sits above normal, and it is worth starting there, because it is the step that students forget.

Hypervigilance is a state of heightened arousal in which the person is constantly on the alert, scanning the surroundings for danger. Every creak of a door and every footstep in the corridor registers; the person startles easily and cannot settle. It is not a clearer consciousness, though it can feel like one. Attention in hypervigilance is locked onto possible threat, and flexible, sustained attention to anything else is actually poorer. DSM-5-TR lists hypervigilance among the arousal symptoms of post-traumatic stress disorder and among the behavioural changes of stimulant intoxication; it is also seen in severe anxiety, in alcohol withdrawal and in paranoid states, where the person watches the street from behind a curtain for the people he believes are following him.

Below normal wakefulness lies clouding of consciousness, the mildest degree of lowered consciousness and the one most easily missed. The person is awake, may be sitting up and may even chat politely, so that a hurried visitor notices nothing wrong. From the inside, clouding is like trying to think through fog. Thinking is slow and effortful and keeps losing its thread. What is seen and heard is taken in imperfectly and is often misread: a coat on a chair becomes a person, the hiss of oxygen becomes whispering. The person grasps only part of what is happening and cannot hold the whole situation in mind. Because new information is registered poorly, memory for the period is patchy afterwards, with islands of recollection in a sea of blankness. People recovering from a clouded state often say they knew something was wrong but could not work out what, or that everything seemed to be happening too fast and too far away.

Further down, drowsiness (somnolence) is a state in which the person falls asleep unless stimulated but can be roused by a voice and then answers before drifting off again. In obtundation, a step lower in many texts, alertness is blunted: responses are slow, interest in the surroundings has drained away, the person sleeps much of the time and needs a louder voice or a shake to respond. In stupor, in its neurological sense, only vigorous or painful stimulation produces a response, and the response is brief and often only a groan or a withdrawal of a limb; DSM-5-TR describes stuporous individuals as having a reduced level of brain arousal, but not the complete unconsciousness of coma. In coma the person cannot be roused at all and makes no meaningful response even to pain.

These steps are not just a list to be memorised. In a medical ward you can watch a single patient walk down them. Consider a 54-year-old man with cirrhosis of the liver admitted to a city hospital after a bleed from the gut. On the first evening he is irritable and cannot sleep. By the next morning he is muddled: he calls the nurse by his daughter's name, picks at the bedsheet and cannot say why he is in hospital, though he answers greetings normally (clouding). By afternoon he dozes through the ward round and must be called twice before he opens his eyes (drowsiness, then obtundation). That night he responds only with a groan when the doctor presses hard on his breastbone (stupor). The cause is the build-up of toxins that his damaged liver can no longer clear, and with treatment he climbs back up the same staircase over the next few days. A psychologist asked to see him on the first evening for "irritability and poor sleep" would have been seeing the top step of a slide into coma.

The practical rule follows directly. Every other finding in the examination must be read in the light of the level of consciousness. A low memory score, a muddled answer to a proverb or a failure on serial sevens obtained from a clouded or drowsy patient measures the clouding, not the person's memory, intelligence or reasoning. That is why consciousness is the first heading of the cognitive examination, and why it is recorded in plain words before anything else.

Exam trap

Clouding of consciousness is the mildest lowering of consciousness and the core of delirium; it is not drowsiness and not coma. Hypervigilance is a raised level of arousal (PTSD, stimulant intoxication, alcohol withdrawal, paranoid states), not a lowered one. The order downwards is clouding, drowsiness, obtundation, stupor, coma. The level of consciousness is arousal; it is not the form of thought, so tangentiality, derailment and loosening of associations are never "disturbances of the level of consciousness" (they are disorders of the form of thought, Section 2.4).

Confusion and delirium

Few words in a hospital are used as loosely as "confused". In a referral note it can mean forgetful, disoriented, deluded, hallucinating, drowsy, angry or simply difficult to understand, and the psychologist who reads "elderly lady, confused, please see" knows almost nothing. In descriptive psychopathology confusion has a narrower meaning: muddled, poorly connected thinking with disorientation and a failure to grasp the situation one is in, usually as a result of impaired consciousness. In that sense "acute confusional state" is an older name for delirium. The clinician's job is to translate the vague word in the referral into precise terms: is the level of consciousness lowered, is attention impaired, is the person disoriented, is there a memory gap, is there a delusion? Each answer points somewhere different.

The word also sits close to a word it should never be confused with. Confusion is a disturbance of consciousness and thinking; confabulation is a falsification of memory, in which gaps are filled with invented but sincerely believed accounts, classically in Korsakoff syndrome (Section 2.7). A patient can be confused without confabulating (a delirious woman who cannot follow the conversation), and confabulate without being confused (a man with Korsakoff syndrome, fully alert, telling you calmly about the market he visited this morning, although he has not left the ward in a month).

Delirium is the syndrome of acutely disturbed consciousness. DSM-5-TR calls its essential feature "an acute impairment of consciousness", and its criteria are worth reading slowly, because each clause excludes something.

  • A disturbance of attention and awareness. The person has a reduced ability to direct, focus, sustain and shift attention, together with reduced awareness of the environment. In practice, questions have to be repeated because attention wanders; the person may give the answer to the previous question instead of shifting to the new one; and he is easily pulled away by irrelevant things, a trolley in the corridor or a voice at the next bed. Attention and awareness are the two core features of normal consciousness, which is why their disturbance comes first.
  • An acute onset and a fluctuating course. The disturbance develops over a short period, usually hours to a few days, represents a change from the person's usual state, and tends to fluctuate in severity during the day. Acute onset is what separates delirium from dementia, which creeps on over months or years; "a change from baseline" matters because a person who already has dementia can develop a delirium on top of it, and then it is the sudden worsening that gives it away.
  • A further disturbance of cognition. There is a change in at least one more area: memory (particularly recent memory), orientation (particularly to time and place), language (particularly the understanding of what is said), visuospatial ability or perception. The perceptual disturbances range from misinterpretations and illusions to hallucinations, which are typically visual but can occur in any sense (Section 2.3).
  • Not better explained by an existing neurocognitive disorder, and not in coma. Cognition can only be assessed if the cortex is awake enough to respond to speech; a person who can barely be roused has a disorder of arousal, stupor or coma, not a delirium, although delirium often follows as such a person wakes.
  • A physical cause. There must be evidence that the state is the direct physiological result of a medical condition, a substance taken or withdrawn, a medication, a toxin, or several of these together. Delirium is always a sign that the body or brain is under physical attack.

Around this core DSM-5-TR describes several associated features that complete the picture. The sleep-wake cycle is almost always disturbed: the person sleeps by day and is awake and agitated at night, and in some cases the cycle is completely reversed; this disturbance is so common that it has been proposed as a core criterion. Emotions are unstable, with fear, anxiety, irritability, anger, euphoria or apathy and rapid, unpredictable shifts from one to another, and the person may call out, scream, curse, moan or mutter, especially at night. Symptoms often worsen in the evening and at night, when the external cues that keep a person oriented (daylight, visitors, the noise of the ward) fall away. Nurses call this evening worsening sundowning.

DSM-5-TR also asks the clinician to specify the level of activity. In hyperactive delirium the person is agitated, restless and emotionally labile, pulls out drips and catheters, tries to climb out of bed and refuses care. In hypoactive delirium he is slowed, sluggish and lethargic, sometimes approaching stupor, lying quietly with half-closed eyes. In the mixed form activity is normal although attention and awareness are disturbed, or it swings rapidly between the two. The hyperactive form is the one everyone notices. The hypoactive form, DSM-5-TR warns, may be the more frequent in older adults and is often unrecognised in emergency departments and hospitals; it is commoner in women, whereas men more often show agitation and lability.

A case shows how hypoactive delirium hides. Lalita, a 78-year-old retired schoolteacher, was admitted to a general medical ward of a large hospital in Pune with fever and a burning pain on passing urine. On her third day the ward team asked the psychology service to see her for depression. She had stopped eating, lay facing the wall, answered in a whisper and, her son said, had been "sad ever since Father passed away last year". The trainee psychologist found her lying still with her eyes half closed. She did answer questions, but slowly, and twice she stopped in the middle of a sentence and seemed to lose it. She could repeat only three digits. Asked the time, at four in the afternoon, she said it was early morning and that she was waiting for her tea. The night nurse's notes, when the trainee read them, told a different story from the quiet woman in front of her: at two in the morning Lalita had pulled out her drip and called out for her sister, who had died years before. This was not a depression. It was a hypoactive delirium caused by a urinary infection, with inattention, disorientation, an acute onset, fluctuation across the day and a sleep-wake cycle turned upside down. With antibiotics and fluids she cleared over the following week, and afterwards she remembered almost nothing of the days she had spent in hospital.

Lalita's case also shows why fluctuation is itself a sign. A delirious patient can have a lucid interval, a period of near-normal clarity, and if the only examination takes place during it, the delirium is missed. The night nurse's report, the family's account of the evening before and a second visit at a different time of day are therefore part of the assessment. (Neurosurgeons use "lucid interval" in a related sense: after some head injuries, especially bleeding outside the brain's covering, the person is clear for a time and then deteriorates as the blood collects, which is why a lucid patient after a head injury is still watched.)

The size of the problem is easy to underestimate. DSM-5-TR gives figures from several countries: delirium is present in 18 to 35 percent of people at admission to hospital, occurs in up to 81 percent of those in intensive care, and as many as 38 to 41 percent of hospitalised people with delirium die within a year of the diagnosis. The causes of delirium, its alcohol-withdrawal form delirium tremens, its treatment and its separation from dementia are taught in Chapter 10. Its hallucinations are described in Section 2.3 and its memory in Section 2.7.

Clinical link

Psychologists in general hospitals are often asked to see "depression" that is really hypoactive delirium. Four questions sort them out. Was the onset sudden, over hours or days? Does the state fluctuate, especially worse at night? Is attention impaired on simple tests such as digit span or the months backwards? Is there a medical illness, a new drug or a withdrawal? Three yes answers mean the patient goes back to the physicians, urgently, before anyone prescribes an antidepressant. A delirium treated as a depression leaves its physical cause untreated.

Exam trap

Delirium is a disturbance of attention and awareness that develops over hours to days, fluctuates (often worse at night) and has a physical cause; its hallucinations are usually visual. Confusion and delirium are disturbances of consciousness, so they are the wrong answers when a question describes filling memory gaps with invented stories: that is confabulation (Section 2.7), and deliberate faking for gain is malingering. The form most often missed is hypoactive delirium, mistaken for depression.

Qualitative changes: twilight, oneiroid, narrowed and heightened states

In the quantitative changes the torch grows dim. In the qualitative changes it may stay quite bright while its beam changes shape. The person is awake, sometimes active, but the field of awareness has narrowed to a few ideas or actions, or has filled with a dream-like world of its own.

The twilight state (the German psychiatric term is Dämmerzustand) is a disturbance of consciousness in which the field of awareness is narrowed while the person remains able to act. It begins and ends abruptly and lasts from minutes to hours, occasionally days. During it the person can carry out relatively ordered and apparently purposeful acts, sometimes quite complex ones: walking through streets, boarding a bus, buying something, answering simple questions. Onlookers may notice nothing more than that he seems absent, slow or odd. But his grasp of the wider situation is gone, his behaviour can suddenly become bizarre, and outbursts of fear, anger or violence can occur, particularly if someone tries to restrain him. Afterwards there is partial or complete amnesia for the episode. Twilight states occur after epileptic seizures and in some prolonged epileptic states, after head injury and in other brain disease, with alcohol and drug misuse, and as a psychogenic, dissociative reaction to severe stress, where they shade into dissociative fugue (Section 7.5.6). Older texts also describe a so-called pathological intoxication, in which a small amount of alcohol is followed by a sudden outburst of senseless, often aggressive behaviour and then amnesia, but the existence of this as a separate condition has been disputed. Twilight states matter in forensic psychiatry, because acts done in them, occasionally violent acts, raise the question of whether the person was responsible for what he did.

Manoj, a 32-year-old bank clerk in Nagpur with temporal lobe epilepsy that was poorly controlled because he kept forgetting his tablets, had a cluster of seizures one Sunday morning. An hour later his wife found that he had gone. He was found three hours afterwards at the bus stand on the other side of the city, sitting on a bench with a ticket to a town he had no reason to visit. He answered the policeman's questions slowly, gave his name correctly but could not say how he had got there, and became frightened and struck out when the policeman took his arm. By evening he was himself again, with no memory of the bus, the ticket or the policeman. He had been in a post-ictal twilight state: awake, able to make a journey, but with a narrowed and clouded field of awareness and amnesia afterwards.

The oneiroid state (from the Greek oneiros, dream) is a dream-like state in which the person is immersed in vivid, scenic, often fantastic experiences, as though living inside a dream: journeys through space, battles, the end of the world, scenes in which he himself is an actor. Outwardly he may be quiet, immobile or even stuporous, and he is usually disoriented. When it is over he may remember the fantastic inner drama better than the real events around him. The classic description is a monograph of 1924 by the German psychiatrist Wilhelm Mayer-Gross (1889 to 1961), then working at the Heidelberg psychiatric clinic, with the title Selbstschilderungen der Verwirrtheit: Die oneiroide Erlebnisform ("Self-descriptions of confusion: the oneiroid form of experience"). As its title says, it was built from patients' own accounts of what they had lived through, which is the phenomenological method at work. Mayer-Gross later left Germany for Britain, where he was the senior author of the textbook Clinical Psychiatry (1954) with Eliot Slater and Martin Roth, one of the books through which the European descriptive tradition reached British and Indian psychiatry. Oneiroid states are described mainly in acute psychoses, especially catatonic states (the European literature speaks of oneiroid catatonia), and in some organic and toxic states. The term is not used in DSM-5-TR. What separates the oneiroid state from delirium is its coherence: delirium produces fragmentary, fleeting perceptions in a mind that cannot hold its attention; the oneiroid state produces a connected dream-world in which the person is absorbed.

Narrowing of consciousness also happens without any brain disease. In intense emotion, terror or rage, the field of awareness shrinks to the object of the emotion: the frightened person sees only the knife, the furious one only the face of his enemy, and both may later remember little else. In hypnosis and in dissociative states such as trance and possession, consciousness is narrowed in a more organised way, with awareness of the surroundings reduced and attention held by a single focus (Chapter 7, Section 7.6.7).

At the other end, people describe heightened or expanded awareness: in mania, when colours seem brighter, every event seems significant and the mind seems to take in everything at once; with hallucinogens such as LSD and psilocybin; and in meditative and mystical states, where the boundary between self and world may seem to dissolve (Book 1, Sections 5.5.5 and 5.11). This is a change in the quality of awareness, not a higher level of arousal, and the manic person's sense of seeing everything goes with marked distractibility, as the section on attention below explains. Table 2.3 brings all these states together, and Figure 2.3 shows how the quantitative and qualitative changes relate.

Table 2.3 The states of consciousness: what the person experiences, what the clinician sees and the usual causes.
StateWhat the person experiencesWhat the clinician seesUsual causes
HypervigilanceOn edge, scanning for danger, cannot settleStartles easily, watchful, restlessPTSD, anxiety, stimulants, alcohol withdrawal, paranoid states
CloudingThinking in a fog; things half grasped, misreadAwake but slow, muddled; misses parts of questionsEarly delirium of any cause
Drowsiness, obtundationHeavy, sleepy, little interest in anythingFalls asleep unless roused; slow responsesMetabolic illness, drugs, raised intracranial pressure
Stupor (neurological)Little or nothing recalledResponds only briefly to vigorous or painful stimuliSevere brain or metabolic disease, overdose
ComaNoneUnrousable; no meaningful responseSevere brain injury, overdose, metabolic failure
DeliriumFog, fear, misperceptions, visual hallucinationsInattention, disorientation, fluctuation, worse at nightInfection, drugs, withdrawal, metabolic and brain disease
Twilight stateNarrowed field; later amnesiaOrdered acts, sudden outbursts, abrupt onset and endEpilepsy, head injury, alcohol, dissociation
Oneiroid stateImmersed in vivid, dream-like scenesQuiet or immobile, disorientedAcute psychoses, catatonia, some organic states
NarrowingAware only of one object or ideaAbsorbed, unresponsive to the restIntense emotion, hypnosis, trance, dissociation
Exam trap

A twilight state is a narrowed consciousness in which the person carries out ordered, apparently purposeful acts and is amnesic afterwards; its classic setting is epilepsy (after seizures), with head injury, alcohol and dissociation as the others. Do not confuse it with delirium, in which inattention and disorganisation are obvious, or with the oneiroid state, which is a vivid, dream-like inner drama (Mayer-Gross, 1924) in acute psychoses and catatonia. Heightened awareness in mania is a change in the quality of consciousness, not a higher level.

Stupor and its neurological mimics

Stupor is the one word in this section with two meanings, and Book 2, Section 2.6.2 has already set them out: a deep level of impaired consciousness in neurology, and in psychiatry a state of mutism and immobility in which consciousness may be fully preserved. This subsection takes the psychiatric meaning further, because the patient who lies silent and motionless in a bed is one of the most testing problems in the whole of descriptive psychopathology. The sign is the same in every case; the causes could hardly be more different.

In stupor in the psychiatric sense, the person does not speak (mutism), makes few or no voluntary movements (akinesia) and responds little or not at all to what goes on around him; he may not eat or drink. Five kinds are usually distinguished.

  • Catatonic stupor comes with the other catatonic signs: posturing, negativism, rigidity or waxy flexibility, staring, and sometimes a sudden switch into purposeless excitement. The signs are taught in Section 2.9 and catatonia across the disorders in Section 4.5.7.
  • Depressive stupor is the extreme of psychomotor retardation in severe melancholic depression. The face is fixed in misery, the person lies still, refuses food and fluids and may become dangerously dehydrated, which makes it a medical emergency; electroconvulsive therapy is often the treatment of choice (Section 5.7.7).
  • Manic stupor is rare. Emil Kraepelin described it among the mixed states of manic-depressive illness, the form in which the mournful mood of a depressive stupor is replaced by a cheerful one: the patient lies quietly in bed, inaccessible and giving no answers, but smiles without any recognisable cause, may break out suddenly into laughter and mischief and sink back again, and afterwards often remembers the period accurately.
  • Dissociative stupor follows severe stress or conflict. Voluntary movement and responsiveness to light, noise and touch are profoundly reduced, but muscle tone, posture and breathing are normal, and the person may open his eyes and look about (Chapter 7).
  • Organic stupor is caused by brain disease or a disturbance of the body's chemistry: encephalitis, a tumour or a cyst near the third ventricle, raised pressure inside the skull, metabolic failure or drugs. Here, unlike the other four, the level of consciousness really is lowered.

How does a clinician tell at the bedside whether consciousness is preserved? Several clues help. The eyes are often open and follow movement around the room, and the person may blink when a hand moves suddenly towards his face. If the examiner tries to open closed eyelids, they may be screwed tightly shut against him, which is an active, conscious act. Pupils and reflexes are normal, and the electroencephalogram is usually normal in catatonic and dissociative stupor, whereas it is slowed in most organic states. And patients who recover from a catatonic or dissociative stupor can often repeat what was said at their bedside while they lay apparently unaware, so nothing should ever be said in front of a stuporous patient that one would not say to his face.

Two neurological states imitate stupor and must be known by name. In akinetic mutism the person lies with eyes open, apparently awake, following the examiner with his eyes, but makes almost no spontaneous movement or speech, though he is not paralysed; what is missing is the drive to act. The term came into use after a report in 1941 by the Oxford neurosurgeon Hugh Cairns (1896 to 1952) and his colleagues, who described a 14-year-old girl with an epidermoid cyst of the third ventricle. Akinetic mutism follows damage to the medial frontal lobes (including the anterior cingulate cortex) or to deep midline structures, and it is the extreme of the loss of will called abulia (Section 2.9). In the locked-in syndrome the situation is the reverse: consciousness is entirely intact, but a lesion in the ventral part of the pons, usually a stroke, has paralysed every voluntary muscle except those that move the eyes vertically and the eyelids. The person is awake, aware and thinking, yet looks unresponsive and can communicate only by blinking or looking up and down. The term was introduced by the American neurologists Fred Plum and Jerome Posner in 1966, in their book The Diagnosis of Stupor and Coma. The French magazine editor Jean-Dominique Bauby, locked in after a stroke, dictated a whole book, The Diving Bell and the Butterfly (1997), letter by letter, by blinking his left eyelid. The anatomy of both states belongs to the Physiological book; what matters here is that neither is a psychiatric stupor, and that a locked-in patient wrongly assumed to be unconscious is a person being discussed in front of as if he were absent. Table 2.4 sets the states side by side.

Table 2.4 Stupor and its mimics: is the person conscious?
StateConsciousnessClues at the bedsideTypical setting
Catatonic stuporUsually preservedPosturing, negativism, rigidity, staring; later recallMood disorders, schizophrenia, medical illness
Depressive stuporPreservedFixed misery, refuses food and fluidsSevere melancholic depression
Dissociative stuporPreservedNormal tone and breathing; eyes may openAfter severe stress
Organic stuporLoweredSlowed EEG, neurological signsEncephalitis, tumour, metabolic failure, drugs
Akinetic mutismAwake, little driveEyes follow; almost no speech or movement; not paralysedMedial frontal or deep midline lesions
Locked-in syndromeFully intactParalysed except vertical eye movements and blinkingVentral pontine stroke
Exam trap

In psychiatry, stupor means mutism and immobility, and consciousness is often preserved (catatonic, depressive, manic, dissociative stupor); only organic stupor lowers the level of consciousness. Akinetic mutism (Cairns and colleagues, 1941) is a loss of drive with eyes that follow; the locked-in syndrome (Plum and Posner, 1966) is full consciousness in a paralysed body, communicating by vertical eye movements and blinking. A locked-in patient is not in a coma.

Disorders of attention

Attention is the gatekeeper of consciousness: of all the things that reach the senses, it decides which will be taken in. The general psychology of attention, its theories and experiments, is in Book 1, Sections 5.1 to 5.4, and Book 2, Section 2.6.4 draws the clinical distinction between attention, the ability to turn the mind towards something, and concentration, the ability to keep it there. Here the interest is in the ways in which attention goes wrong and in what each way points to.

Distractibility is the failure to keep attention on the matter in hand because it is captured by trivial things outside. DSM-5-TR's mania criterion puts it precisely: attention too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli. A man in a manic episode, asked about his sleep, starts to answer, breaks off to admire the clinician's pen, comments on a crow on the window ledge, notices the colour of the file and asks where it was bought, and never returns to his sleep. Every stimulus wins, and none holds. Distractibility is the attentional sign of mania; it is also prominent in delirium, which DSM-5-TR describes as a state in which the individual is easily distracted by irrelevant stimuli, and in attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (Chapter 3).

Inattention, the inability to direct and hold attention at all, is something more basic. The delirious patient is not drawn to the crow on the window ledge; he cannot keep the instruction in mind long enough to begin the task. That is why a disturbance of attention is Criterion A of delirium, and why even a simple test such as repeating five digits can expose it.

Attention can also be intact in amount but biased in direction. An attentional bias is a tendency for attention to be drawn preferentially to one kind of material. In anxiety disorders attention is captured by threat: the anxious student scanning a crowded exam hall notices first the stern face of the invigilator and the boy who looks as if he is cheating, and the experiments that measure this bias are described in Section 6.8.4. In depression attention lingers on loss, failure and criticism. These biases are not failures of attention but skews in where it goes, and they help to keep the disorders going.

Finally, poor concentration is a symptom in its own right in many disorders. DSM-5-TR lists a "diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness, nearly every day" among the criteria for a major depressive episode, "difficulty concentrating or mind going blank" among those of generalised anxiety disorder, and "problems with concentration" among the arousal symptoms of post-traumatic stress disorder. In depression the complaint is often larger than the deficit the tests show: the person says his mind has stopped working, then performs better than he expected. When the deficit is large, the picture can resemble dementia, the pseudodementia of Book 2, Section 2.6.5.

Older textbooks and medical dictionaries add three terms that are still met in some notes. Hyperprosexia is an excessive fixity of attention on one object or idea to the exclusion of others, as when a man with health anxiety cannot take his attention off his own heartbeat. Hypoprosexia is a reduced ability to fix attention, as in fatigue, depression and clouded states, and aprosexia, its extreme, a complete inability to do so. They are Greek-rooted names (from prosexis, attention) for phenomena already described above in plain words, and a question that uses them is testing only whether you can translate them.

Exam trap

Distractibility (attention captured by trivial outside stimuli) is the attentional sign of mania; inattention is the core of delirium. The ability to do serial subtraction tests concentration; it has nothing to do with insight, which is the person's awareness and understanding of his own illness (Section 2.10). Difficulty concentrating is a cognitive symptom of depression and anxiety; it is not anhedonia, which is the loss of pleasure and interest (Section 2.8).

Disorders of orientation

Orientation is the person's awareness of where he stands in time, in place and among people: knowing what day and time it is, where he is and who he and the people around him are. It is worth noticing what kind of function this is. Orientation is not a faculty of its own; it is the product of other functions working together. To know the date you must have attended to it and kept it in memory, and you must update it every day. So disorientation, the loss of this awareness, is usually a secondary sign: it appears when consciousness, attention or recent memory fail, and that is exactly why it is such a good marker of brain dysfunction.

The order in which orientation is lost follows from the same point. Time, which needs constant updating, is lost first; then place; then the identity of other people; and one's own identity last of all; as the person recovers, orientation returns in the reverse order. Book 2, Section 2.6.3 and its diagram of the order of loss explain the reason and give the questions, and only the conclusions are needed here.

The first conclusion is that marked disorientation is the hallmark of organic states: delirium (DSM-5-TR notes disorientation particularly to time and place), dementia, intoxication and the confusion that follows a head injury. Orientation is usually preserved in schizophrenia and in depression, even severe ones, and an apparently wrong answer from such a patient is usually something else. A severely depressed man may say "I don't know" because answering seems pointless; a psychotic woman may refuse to answer because she suspects the question; an acutely disturbed man may simply not attend. None of these is disorientation, and a clinician who records it as such has pointed the diagnosis in the wrong direction.

Some patterns of wrong answers are more interesting than a simple loss. In Korsakoff syndrome the patient may give a false orientation with complete confidence: sitting in a medical ward, he tells you that he is in the office of the mill where he worked twenty years ago, that it is a Monday morning and that he is waiting for the manager, filling the gap in his memory with a plausible story (confabulation, Section 2.7). In chronic schizophrenia a patient may show double orientation, giving the correct place and, in the same breath, a delusional one, a coexistence Eugen Bleuler called "double bookkeeping" (Section 4.1.4). A patient who insists that the hospital is a prison run by her enemies is not disoriented either; she knows where she is, but gives it a delusional meaning (Section 2.5). And a patient who insists that the hospital has been moved to her home town, or that there are two identical hospitals, has reduplicative paramnesia (Section 2.6).

The second conclusion is that a pattern which breaks the usual order is a clue in itself. A person who has lost his own name and history while knowing perfectly well the date and the name of the town has an isolated loss of personal identity, and that points away from brain disease to a dissociative cause: dissociative amnesia, or a fugue in which the person may travel and take on a new identity (Sections 7.5.5 and 7.5.6). Table 2.5 sums up these patterns and what each points to.

Indian case records often sum up the first three headings of the cognitive examination in one phrase: "conscious, oriented, sensorium clear". The sensorium is an old word for the faculties of perception and awareness taken together, and a clear sensorium means that the person is alert, attentive and oriented. The phrase matters because it is the condition under which other findings carry their full weight: DSM-5-TR requires that hallucinations, to count as a symptom of psychosis, occur in the context of a clear sensorium.

Orientation for time, knowing the date, is different from the experience of time, how time feels as it passes. The experience of time can be altered while orientation is perfect. In depression time drags. A meta-analysis by Sven Thönes and Daniel Oberfeld (2015), pooling 16 studies with 433 depressed patients and 485 controls, found that depressed people judged time to pass more slowly than controls did, a moderate effect, although their judgements of how long an interval lasted were basically unaffected. DSM-5-TR lists a subjectively altered sense of time, too fast or too slow, among the associated features of depersonalisation-derealisation disorder (Section 2.10). Cannabis and hallucinogens can stretch minutes into what feel like hours. None of these people is disoriented; ask them the date and they will tell you.

Table 2.5 Patterns of disorientation and what they point to.
PatternWhat is foundPoints to
Time, then place, then person lostWrong date first; own name kept longestDelirium, dementia, intoxication, head injury
Fluctuating disorientationOriented in the morning, lost at nightDelirium
False, confident orientationPlausible wrong answers filling memory gapsKorsakoff syndrome (confabulation)
Double orientationCorrect place and a delusional one togetherChronic schizophrenia
Own identity lost, time and place intactCannot give name or history; knows date and townDissociative amnesia or fugue
Apparent errors without true loss"Don't know", refusal, inattentionDepression, psychosis, non-cooperation
Altered experience of time, orientation intactTime drags or rushes; date correctDepression, depersonalisation, drugs
Exam trap

Orientation is lost time first, then place, then person, and one's own identity last; it returns in reverse. Marked disorientation means think organic (delirium, dementia, intoxication, head injury), not schizophrenia or depression, in which orientation is usually preserved. Lost personal identity with intact time and place points to a dissociative cause. Orientation is a cognitive function; it is not insight, which is the trap answer when a question asks what insight means (Section 2.10).

2.3 Disorders of Perception: Distortions, Illusions and HallucinationsMedium yield · 5 Qs

To perceive is to become aware, through the senses, of the world outside and of one's own body. Sensation supplies the raw signal; perception organises it into objects, voices, smells and touches that mean something (Book 1, Sections 3.1 and 4.1 draw the line between the two). Most of the time the process is so smooth that we never notice it. Psychopathology notices it when it goes wrong, and it can go wrong in three quite different ways. A real object can be perceived but perceived as changed. A real object can be mistaken for something else. Or something can be perceived when there is nothing there at all. Each of these has a name, each points to different disorders, and examiners return to them every year, because the words are easy to confuse and the distinctions are exactly the ones a clinician must make at the bedside.

Some of this ground has been covered elsewhere, and this section points back rather than repeating it. How to ask a person about hallucinations, with a normalising question, and what to record about them is set out in Book 2, Section 2.5.7. Book 1, Section 4.5.6 has already introduced Andrew Sims' division of perceptual disorders into distortions and deceptions and the three kinds of illusion. And hallucinations as they occur in schizophrenia, their frequency, the evidence that visual hallucinations are commoner in schizophrenia than once taught, the brain imaging of voices and the community surveys of voice-hearing, are the subject of Section 4.2.3. This section is the reference for every perceptual phenomenon in every disorder.

Two families: sensory distortions and sensory deceptions

The first question to ask of any abnormal perception is whether the person is perceiving a real object differently or perceiving something new. In a sensory distortion a real object is perceived, and perceived as itself, but it is altered in intensity, in quality, or in size and shape. In a sensory deception a new perception arises, which may be a misreading of something real (an illusion) or may have no external stimulus at all (a hallucination). Distortions are therefore the more modest disturbance: the world is still the world, only changed.

Distortions of intensity. In hyperaesthesia sensations in general are felt more intensely than normal, and in hyperacusis sounds in particular seem painfully loud. The morning after a long wedding, a man with a bad hangover winces at the clatter of steel plates in the kitchen and the ringing of a phone; a woman in the middle of a migraine cannot bear the light from the window or the sound of the pressure cooker. Hyperacusis and heightened sensitivity to light also occur in anxiety, in an overactive thyroid and with hallucinogens, and in mania colours can seem more brilliant and sounds sharper than ever before. The opposite is a dulling of perception. People in a severe depression often describe a world drained of colour and flavour: food tastes like cardboard, the colours of Holi look grey, music has gone flat. That dulling is felt, not imagined; it is part of how depression changes experience.

Distortions of quality. Here the character of a sensation changes. The best-known example is xanthopsia, yellow vision, a relatively specific sign of toxicity from digoxin, the heart drug made from the foxglove; digoxin can also tint the world green (chloropsia), and erythropsia is the corresponding term for red vision. A popular theory holds that the yellows of Vincent van Gogh's paintings came from treatment with foxglove, but most critics point out that he painted in yellow for most of his working life, long before any such treatment, so the story is best treated as speculation. Hallucinogens such as LSD transform colours, making them more intense and making them shimmer and flow.

Distortions of size, shape and distance. In micropsia objects look smaller than they really are, and in macropsia (megalopsia) larger; the general term for distortions of size is dysmegalopsia (also called dysmetropsia). A girl in the middle of a migraine attack says that her mother's face across the room has shrunk to the size of a coin, though she knows that her mother has not changed. Related distortions concern distance: in teleopsia (sometimes called porropsia) objects seem much farther away than they are, as though receding down a tunnel, and in pelopsia they seem nearer. In metamorphopsia the shape of things is distorted: straight lines look wavy or bent, as the door frame does to an older man with disease of the macula of his retina, and the same distortion occurs with lesions of the visual cortex. The British psychiatrist John Todd, in a paper of 1955 in the Canadian Medical Association Journal, collected these experiences of altered size, shape and body image under the name Alice in Wonderland syndrome, after the heroine who grows and shrinks in Lewis Carroll's book, and suggested that Carroll, who had migraine, may have known these experiences himself. The syndrome occurs mainly in migraine, in epilepsy and with infections, especially in children; in a Spanish series of 20 children, infection was the commonest cause (nine children, five of them with Epstein-Barr virus, the cause of glandular fever), followed by migraine (eight), and most of the children never had another episode. Table 2.6 gathers the sensory distortions in one place.

Table 2.6 The sensory distortions: a real object perceived as changed.
DistortionWhat changesExampleUsual causes
Hyperaesthesia, hyperacusisIntensity raisedOrdinary sounds painfully loudMigraine, hangover, anxiety, hyperthyroidism, mania, hallucinogens
Dulled perceptionIntensity loweredColours grey, food tastelessDepression
Xanthopsia, chloropsia, erythropsiaColour (yellow, green, red)White walls look yellowDigoxin toxicity (yellow, green); hallucinogens
Micropsia, macropsiaSizeA face shrunk to a coinMigraine, epilepsy, infections, retinal disease
Teleopsia, pelopsiaDistanceThe room seems to recedeMigraine, epilepsy, Alice in Wonderland syndrome
MetamorphopsiaShapeStraight lines look wavyMacular disease, occipital lesions
Exam trap

"A real perceptual object perceived in a distorted way" is a sensory distortion; "a new perception" is a sensory deception (illusion or hallucination). Do not confuse micropsia, in which real objects look smaller, with Lilliputian hallucinations, in which tiny people or animals are seen that are not there at all (Section 2.3.5). Xanthopsia (yellow vision) points to digoxin. Alice in Wonderland syndrome was named by John Todd in 1955; its usual causes are migraine, epilepsy and infection, not psychosis.

Illusions

An illusion is a misperception of a real external stimulus: something is really there, but it is perceived as something it is not. Descriptive psychopathology recognises three kinds, and they differ in what drives the mistake and in what happens when the person looks again.

A completion illusion arises because perception fills in what is incomplete. You read a shop sign that says "HOSPTAL" as HOSPITAL and do not notice the missing letter; you hear the whole of a familiar film song from a neighbour's radio even though half the words are drowned by traffic. Completion illusions depend on inattention, and they disappear as soon as you look or listen carefully. An affect illusion is driven by a strong emotion, usually fear or longing. A student walking home at night past the cremation ground sees a white plastic sheet flapping on a bush and, for a terrifying moment, sees a figure; a mother waiting up for her son, late on his motorcycle, hears his engine in every vehicle that passes. Affect illusions too vanish when the person attends closely, though the emotion may linger. Pareidolia is the effortless seeing of vivid, meaningful images in vague or random material: faces in the pattern of a mosaic floor, an elephant in the damp patches of a ceiling after the monsoon, the hare that Indian tradition sees in the markings of the moon. Pareidolia behaves differently from the other two. It does not disappear when you look more closely; it persists and often grows richer the longer you attend, and it coexists with full knowledge of what the stimulus really is. Nobody thinks there is an elephant on the ceiling. Pareidolia is entirely normal.

Illusions multiply whenever perception is degraded or the mind is primed to misread. They are common in dim light and darkness, in fatigue and fear, with hallucinogens, and in people whose eyesight or hearing is failing. Above all they multiply in delirium, where DSM-5-TR lists misinterpretations and illusions among the perceptual disturbances: the drip stand by the bed and the folds of a curtain become people, and the frightened, inattentive patient reacts to them. This is why good lighting at night, a familiar face and the patient's own spectacles and hearing aid are part of the nursing care of delirium; they take away the raw material from which illusions are made.

One distinction belongs here because students so often get it wrong. In a delusional perception, a symptom taught in Section 2.5, the real object is perceived correctly, and it is its meaning that is false: a man sees a red car parked outside his house, sees it accurately as a red car, and knows at once that it means he has been chosen to be killed. That is not an illusion, because nothing in the perception itself is mistaken; it is a disorder of belief attached to a normal perception.

Exam trap

Illusions are distortions or misinterpretations of existing stimuli. "The same as hallucinations" is wrong by definition. "Not based on external reality" describes a hallucination, which has no external stimulus. "The result of innate mechanisms" is not a definition and is wrong as a description: illusions arise from normal perceptual processes that learning shapes, as the cross-cultural studies of illusions show (Book 1, Section 4.5.6). Completion and affect illusions vanish with attention; pareidolia persists. A delusional perception is a correct perception with a false meaning, not an illusion.

What makes a hallucination

The French psychiatrist Jean-Étienne Dominique Esquirol (1772 to 1840), working in Paris in the early nineteenth century, was the first to separate the two kinds of deception clearly: in an illusion something is there and is misperceived, in a hallucination nothing is there at all. The modern definitions keep his core and add to it. DSM-5-TR describes hallucinations as perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus; they are vivid and clear, with the full force and impact of normal perceptions, and not under voluntary control. The British psychologists Peter Slade and Richard Bentall, in their book Sensory Deception (1988), gave a definition with three criteria that has become standard in psychological research, and each criterion separates the hallucination from one of its neighbours:

  1. It is a percept-like experience that occurs in the absence of an appropriate stimulus. This separates it from an illusion, which has a stimulus.
  2. It has the full force or impact of a real perception. This separates it from ordinary imagery, which we know at once to be our own and which is fainter than seeing or hearing.
  3. It is not amenable to direct and voluntary control by the person. This separates it from daydreams and fantasies, which we can call up, change and dismiss at will.

Descriptive psychopathology adds two marks of a true hallucination, drawn from Jaspers. It is located in external, objective space, out there in the world, coming from the window or the ceiling or the next room. And it is experienced alongside real perceptions, not instead of them: the patient hears the voice from the ceiling and the clinician's question at the same time, and may turn from one to the other.

Hallucinations are also classified by how organised they are. An elementary hallucination is unformed: flashes of light, zigzag lines, bangs, buzzing, whistling, a single tone. A complex hallucination is formed: a voice speaking words, a face, a person, an animal, a whole scene, a piece of music. The distinction matters because elementary hallucinations usually point to a disturbance early in the sensory pathway or in the primary sensory cortex (the flashes of a migraine aura, the noises of ear disease), while complex ones involve the higher levels of perception and occur both in organic states and in the psychoses.

One idea runs through all that follows and answers a whole family of exam questions. A hallucination is a disorder of perception. It is not a disorder of thinking, of judgement or of intelligence. What the person believes about the hallucination is a separate matter. An old man with failing eyesight who sees children on his bed may know perfectly well that they are not real, and his insight is intact (Section 2.3.8). A woman with schizophrenia who hears a voice may be certain that it is her neighbour speaking through the wall, and may go on to build a delusion of persecution on it, a secondary delusion in the sense of Section 2.5. The perception is disordered in both; only in the second has the belief about it become delusional. That is why hallucinations and delusions are taught as different phenomena even though they so often travel together in psychosis.

Exam trap

"Hallucination is a disorder of": perception, not thinking, judgement or intelligence. A delusion is the disorder of thought content (Section 2.5). A hallucination has no external stimulus, is vivid, is located in outer space and is not under voluntary control (DSM-5-TR; Slade and Bentall, 1988). Whether the person believes it is real is a separate question, which is why insight can be preserved in someone who hallucinates.

The neighbours of the hallucination: imagery, presence and the pseudohallucination

Several experiences lie close to the hallucination and are regularly confused with it. Ordinary mental imagery is the most familiar: you can picture your mother's face or hear a tune in your head. Imagery is experienced in inner, subjective space ("in my mind's eye"), it is willed (you call it up and dismiss it), and it is less vivid than seeing or hearing. People vary greatly in how vivid their imagery is, and vivid imagery is not a symptom.

Eidetic imagery is a rarer and stranger normal experience. After looking at a picture for a short time, the person continues to "see" it, in full detail, as if it were still projected in front of him after the picture has been taken away, and he can scan it with his eyes and read off details he had not consciously noticed. Eidetic imagery is found in a small minority of children and very rarely in adults. After-images, the coloured patch that floats in front of the eyes after looking at a bright light, are ordinary sensory events whose explanation belongs to general psychology.

The sensed presence (or felt presence) is the vivid awareness that someone is nearby, without seeing or hearing him. It is common in the early months of bereavement, during sleep paralysis, and in people exhausted and in danger: the explorer Ernest Shackleton wrote that during his march across the mountains of South Georgia in 1916 it often seemed to him that his party was four, not three. A sensed presence is not, strictly, a perception in any one sense, and most people who have one know that no one is really there.

The term that causes most trouble is the pseudohallucination, and the trouble comes from its having two meanings, which Book 2, Section 2.5.7 has already set out and Section 4.2.3 takes up again. The word is usually credited to the German psychiatrist Friedrich Wilhelm Hagen in 1868. In the European tradition of the Russian psychiatrist Victor Kandinsky (1849 to 1889) and of Jaspers, a pseudohallucination is a vivid, involuntary perception-like experience located in inner, subjective space: a voice heard "inside my head" rather than through the ears, a face seen "in my mind" rather than in the room. In much British and American usage it means instead an experience located in outer space that the person recognises as unreal. The two meanings do not pick out the same experiences, as Figure 2.4 shows: the first is defined by where the experience is, the second by what the person believes about it.

The confusion is not just a matter of exam questions. In 1996 the psychiatrists G. E. Berrios and T. R. Dening traced the term through its history in Psychological Medicine and concluded that it has no stable referent: it has been used for so many different imagined experiences that empirical research on "pseudohallucinations" can hardly get started, and it has served as a kind of joker in the diagnostic pack, taking whatever value the clinician needs at the moment. In a survey of British and Irish consultant psychiatrists by the same two authors, most believed in pseudohallucinations, yet 63 percent found the concept confusing and only 58 percent thought it clinically useful, and those who used it did so inconsistently. The practical lesson is to describe the experience rather than label it: say that the voice is heard inside the head, or that the figures are seen in the room but known to be unreal, and say which sense of the word you mean if you use it. Two cautions from Section 4.2.3 bear repeating in one sentence: many people with schizophrenia hear their voices inside the head, so the inner location does not make an experience harmless, and a "pseudohallucination" can be as distressing and as commanding as any other voice.

Exam trap

A pseudohallucination is either a vivid involuntary experience in inner, subjective space (Kandinsky, Jaspers) or an experience in outer space recognised as unreal (British and American usage); the word is credited to Hagen (1868). Imagery is willed and fainter; eidetic imagery is a vivid image retained after the stimulus has gone, commoner in children; a sensed presence is an awareness of someone nearby without seeing or hearing him. Berrios and Dening (1996) argued that "pseudohallucination" has no stable meaning, so describe the experience instead.

Hallucinations sense by sense, and what each points to

Hallucinations can occur in any sense, and the sense in which they occur is one of the most useful clues to their cause. The organising question of this subsection is therefore not "what do hallucinations look like in schizophrenia?" (Section 4.2.3 answers that) but "when a person describes a hallucination in this sense, of this kind, what should you think of first?" Table 2.7 at the end gathers the answers.

Auditory hallucinations. An auditory hallucination may be elementary or complex. Elementary noises (bangs, clicks, buzzing, whistling, a hum) occur with disease of the ear and the auditory nerve, in organic states and sometimes early in a psychosis. A distinct and often misunderstood variety is the musical hallucination: hearing music, usually a familiar song, a hymn, a bhajan or a film tune from one's youth, played over and over as if from a radio in the next room. It is commonest in older people with acquired hearing loss, many of them women living alone, and many know that the music is not real. The favoured explanation is the same release mechanism described for Charles Bonnet syndrome below: an auditory cortex starved of input from damaged ears generates its own sounds from memory. For this reason musical hallucinations in hearing loss are sometimes called "musical ear syndrome" and compared with an auditory Charles Bonnet syndrome, though Charles Bonnet syndrome proper is visual. Better hearing, through treatment of the ear or a hearing aid, sometimes helps, and the main clinical task is not to mistake the old woman who hears devotional songs at night for someone with dementia or psychosis.

Complex auditory hallucinations are usually voices. In schizophrenia and related disorders, DSM-5-TR notes, auditory hallucinations are the commonest hallucinations of all, usually voices, familiar or unfamiliar, heard as distinct from the person's own thoughts. They occur in roughly 60 to 80 percent of people with a schizophrenia spectrum diagnosis, and of the major psychiatric disorders schizophrenia is the one in which hallucinations are most frequent (Section 4.2.3). Voices are described by grammatical person. A second-person hallucination speaks to the patient ("You are a burden on your family"). In severe depression with psychotic features the voice accuses and condemns in keeping with the mood; in mania it may announce that the patient has been chosen for greatness; and second-person voices are common in schizophrenia too, so they carry little diagnostic weight on their own. A third-person hallucination speaks about the patient ("He thinks he is clever; look, now he is going out"). Voices discussing or arguing about the patient, a voice keeping up a running commentary on his actions, and thought echo (hearing one's own thoughts spoken aloud as one thinks them, Gedankenlautwerden or écho de la pensée) are among Kurt Schneider's first-rank symptoms of schizophrenia, whose list and limited specificity are taught in Section 4.1.6. A command hallucination is a voice that orders the patient to do something; because commands to harm oneself or others are sometimes obeyed, the clinician must always ask what the voice commands and whether the patient feels bound to obey (Book 2, Section 2.7).

One further auditory picture belongs to alcohol. In alcoholic hallucinosis a person who has been drinking heavily for years hears voices, often several, often threatening, accusing or talking about him, in clear consciousness, during or after a period of heavy drinking. DSM-5-TR, describing alcohol-induced psychotic disorder with hallucinations, notes that it usually occurs only after prolonged, heavy drinking in people with moderate to severe alcohol use disorder and that the hallucinations are generally auditory. The clear consciousness is what separates it from delirium tremens, with its clouding and its visual hallucinations (Chapter 10).

Visual hallucinations. A visual hallucination may again be elementary or complex. Elementary flashes of light (photopsia), zigzag lines, sparkles and coloured shapes occur in the aura of migraine, where a shimmering zigzag spreads slowly across the visual field over several minutes before fading, with lesions or seizures of the occipital lobe and with disease of the eye such as a detaching retina. Complex visual hallucinations of people, animals and scenes have a longer list of causes, and nearly all of them are organic.

  • Delirium. The visual hallucinations of delirium are often frightening and come with illusions and misinterpretations: insects on the walls, animals in the room, figures at the foot of the bed. In delirium tremens, the delirium of alcohol withdrawal, small animals and insects are classic.
  • Lilliputian hallucinations. The French psychiatrist Raoul Leroy described in 1909 hallucinations of tiny people or animals, which he named Lilliputian hallucinations after the little people of Jonathan Swift's Gulliver's Travels. They are classically taught as a feature of delirium tremens and other deliria. A systematic review of 226 case descriptions by the Dutch psychiatrist Jan Dirk Blom (2021) showed that they are less specific than that: schizophrenia spectrum disorders, alcohol use disorder and loss of vision together accounted for about half the cases and neurological disease for over a third (36 percent), and in 39 percent the tiny figures were also heard or felt.
  • Dementia with Lewy bodies and Parkinson's disease. DSM-5-TR lists "recurrent visual hallucinations that are well formed and detailed" as one of the core features of neurocognitive disorder with Lewy bodies, alongside fluctuating cognition and parkinsonism; the person sees people or animals in the house, often calmly. Visual hallucinations are common in Parkinson's disease as well, and the dopamine-boosting medicines used to treat it add to the risk (Chapter 10).
  • Peduncular hallucinosis. In 1922 the French neurologist Jean Lhermitte (1877 to 1959) described a 72-year-old woman who, after a lesion of the upper brain stem, saw vivid, formed visual hallucinations, especially at dusk. Peduncular hallucinosis, named after the cerebral peduncles of the midbrain, follows lesions of the midbrain and thalamus, and the images are often vivid and colourful.
  • Hallucinogens, epilepsy and eye disease. LSD and related drugs produce vivid visual hallucinations, which DSM-5-TR notes can be frightening. Seizures in the occipital lobe tend to produce elementary coloured shapes, and seizures in the temporal lobe complex scenes and faces. Eye disease with loss of vision produces the hallucinations of Charles Bonnet syndrome (Section 2.3.8).

Hence the old rule: visual hallucinations suggest an organic cause until proved otherwise. The rule is a good one in an emergency department, but it needs a caution. Visual hallucinations are not rare in schizophrenia: Section 4.2.3 gives a figure of about a quarter of patients, usually alongside voices. DSM-5-TR adds that in children with schizophrenia visual hallucinations are more common than in adults, and that their relative frequency varies between cultures and regions. What should raise suspicion is a visual hallucination that is prominent, occurs without voices, appears in a clouded or fluctuating state, comes with neurological signs, or begins late in life.

Olfactory and gustatory hallucinations. An olfactory hallucination is a smell with no source; a gustatory hallucination is a taste. The two often go together, because the senses of smell and taste are so closely linked. Their most important organic cause is epilepsy arising in the medial part of the temporal lobe. The English neurologist John Hughlings Jackson (1835 to 1911) described seizures that brought a sudden, brief, often unpleasant smell or taste, smacking and tasting movements of the lips and a strange "dreamy state", and in 1898, with Walter Colman, he reported a patient in whom the lesion was a small patch of softening in the uncinate gyrus. From that structure, the uncus, these seizures became known as uncinate fits. The smell in an uncinate fit is stereotyped, the same each time (burning rubber, rotting meat, something chemical), and lasts seconds. In psychiatric disorders the smells and tastes are longer-lasting and woven into the person's mood or beliefs. A man with severe psychotic depression smells his own body rotting and believes that he is decaying inside, a mood-congruent experience that sits close to the nihilistic delusions of Section 2.5. A woman with schizophrenia notices a bitter taste in her food and smells gas in the flat, and both feed her conviction that her family is poisoning her. Do not confuse any of these with olfactory reference disorder (Section 6.6.6), in which, in DSM-5-TR's words, the person is preoccupied with the belief that he gives off a foul or offensive body odour that others cannot notice, or can notice only slightly; there the problem is a belief and a preoccupation, not a smell perceived.

Tactile hallucinations. A tactile hallucination (haptic hallucination) is a false perception of touch, on or just under the skin. The classic form is formication, from the Latin formica, an ant: the sensation of insects crawling on or under the skin. It is typical of heavy use of cocaine and amphetamines, where users call the crawling "cocaine bugs". DSM-5-TR notes that in stimulant-induced psychosis the hallucination of bugs or vermin crawling in or under the skin can lead to scratching and extensive skin excoriations, and that in stimulant intoxication the tactile hallucinations are usually recognised by the person as drug effects. The French psychiatrist Valentin Magnan (1835 to 1916) described this sensation in chronic cocaine users in 1889, and in Indian notes it is sometimes called Magnan's sign. Formication also occurs in alcohol withdrawal, where DSM-5-TR lists transient visual, tactile or auditory hallucinations or illusions among the withdrawal signs. In schizophrenia tactile hallucinations take other forms: being touched, pricked, given electric shocks, sexually interfered with in the night, and they are usually experienced as being done by someone. Keep formication separate from delusional infestation, the belief that one's skin is infested by parasites (Ekbom syndrome, Section 2.6): formication is a perception, infestation a belief, though the one can feed the other.

Somatic hallucinations. A somatic hallucination (coenaesthetic or visceral hallucination) is a false perception arising inside the body: the liver burning, the intestines twisting, a snake moving in the stomach, the brain shrinking or splitting. Such experiences occur in schizophrenia and in psychotic depression, where a woman may feel that her bowels have rotted away. Two neighbouring terms must be kept apart. When the person feels that the bodily sensation is being produced in him by an outside agency (rays from the neighbour's house heating his genitals), it is somatic passivity, a disorder of the possession of experience taught in Section 2.4; when he holds a false belief about his body without any special sensation, it is a somatic delusion (Section 2.5).

Kinaesthetic and vestibular hallucinations. A kinaesthetic hallucination is a false sensation of movement of the body or limbs: an arm being raised, the legs twisting, the body being turned over in bed. A vestibular hallucination is a false sensation of balance or position: floating, flying, falling, the bed tilting or rising. They are reported in schizophrenia and in toxic states, including intoxication with hallucinogens. The brief sensation of falling, with a jerk, as one drops off to sleep is a normal relative.

Table 2.7 Hallucinations by sense: the experience and what it points to first.
Sense and kindThe experiencePoints first to
