Exam trap

Third-person voices, running commentary and thought echo are first-rank; second-person voices are not, and they are typical of psychotic depression as well as schizophrenia. Visual hallucinations suggest an organic cause, but about a quarter of people with schizophrenia have them. Olfactory hallucinations before or at the start of a seizure are the uncinate fits of temporal lobe epilepsy (Hughlings Jackson). Formication is tactile (cocaine, amphetamines, alcohol withdrawal); delusional infestation is a belief (Ekbom, Section 2.6). Lilliputian means tiny figures (Leroy, 1909), not small-looking real objects, which is micropsia.

The special types of hallucination

A handful of hallucinations are named not by their sense but by the circumstances in which they occur. They are favourites of examiners, and each teaches something about how perception works.

A functional hallucination is triggered by a real stimulus in the same sense, and the stimulus and the hallucination are perceived together. A woman with schizophrenia hears a man's voice calling her a thief every time the pressure cooker whistles, and the voice stops when the whistling stops. Notice how this differs from an illusion. In an affect illusion the whistle itself would be misheard as a voice; in a functional hallucination she hears the whistle as a whistle, correctly, and hears the voice as well, inside it or alongside it. Functional hallucinations occur mainly in schizophrenia.

A reflex hallucination is triggered by a real stimulus in one sense but occurs in another. A man with schizophrenia feels a hand pressing on his shoulder every time the bell of the temple near his house is rung. Reflex hallucinations are reported in schizophrenia and in drug states. They should be distinguished from synaesthesia (Book 1, Section 3.12.2), in which a stimulus in one sense regularly produces an experience in another (letters seen as coloured, music felt as shapes); synaesthesia is lifelong, consistent, not distressing, and never mistaken by the person for an event in the outside world.

An extracampine hallucination is experienced outside the possible field of the sense concerned. A woman sitting at her sewing machine says she can see her mother-in-law standing behind her chair, scowling, without turning round; a man in a Delhi hostel hears his family talking in the kitchen of their house in a Kerala village two thousand kilometres away. Such patients usually insist that they really see or hear the thing, not that they imagine it. Extracampine hallucinations occur in schizophrenia, in epilepsy and in other organic states.

An autoscopic hallucination is the experience of seeing one's own double out in the world, a figure that looks like oneself, often facing one and sometimes copying one's movements: the doppelgänger ("double-walker") of German folklore and of stories by Edgar Allan Poe and Fyodor Dostoevsky. In the related experience of heautoscopy the person sees the double and is unsure which of the two bodies he is in; in negative autoscopy he looks into a mirror and fails to see himself. The out-of-body experience, in which the self seems to float above the body and look down on it, belongs to the same family. These experiences occur with epilepsy and with lesions of the temporal and parietal lobes, and in 2002 Olaf Blanke and his colleagues in Geneva reported that electrical stimulation of the right angular gyrus, near the junction of the temporal and parietal lobes, repeatedly produced out-of-body experiences in a woman being assessed for epilepsy surgery. Their explanation was a failure to integrate the signals from the body and from the balance organs into a single sense of where one's body is.

Two hallucinations are defined by their place on the border of sleep. A hypnagogic hallucination occurs while falling asleep; a hypnopompic hallucination occurs while waking up. The first word was introduced by the French scholar Alfred Maury (1817 to 1892) in 1848, from the Greek hypnos, sleep, and agogos, leading: the hallucination that leads into sleep. The second was coined by the English classical scholar and psychical researcher Frederic Myers (1843 to 1901) and published after his death, from pompe, a sending away: the hallucination that sends sleep off. Typical experiences are hearing one's name called, a doorbell or a phone ringing, seeing faces, patterns or a figure beside the bed, or feeling a touch, and they are usually brief and often jolt the person awake.

How common are they in healthy people? Maurice Ohayon and his colleagues (1996) answered the question by telephoning a representative sample of 4,972 people aged 15 to 100 across the United Kingdom. Thirty-seven percent reported hypnagogic hallucinations and 12.5 percent hypnopompic ones. Both were commoner in people with insomnia, excessive daytime sleepiness or mental disorders, but their frequency far exceeded anything that narcolepsy could explain. DSM-5-TR accordingly requires that the hallucinations of psychosis occur in a clear sensorium and states that those occurring while falling asleep or waking up are considered to be within the range of normal experience.

One disorder is defined partly by them. In narcolepsy, DSM-5-TR says, approximately 20 to 60 percent of people experience vivid hypnagogic or hypnopompic hallucinations, typically visual or auditory and sometimes tactile, and a similar proportion experience sleep paralysis, waking unable to move or speak; the combination of excessive sleepiness, cataplexy, these hallucinations and sleep paralysis is taught in Chapter 8 and Book 1, Section 5.8.4. Ohayon's group suggested that, in a person with excessive daytime sleepiness, hypnopompic hallucinations may be the better pointer to narcolepsy, precisely because hypnagogic ones are so common in everyone.

Rukhsana, a 24-year-old nursing student in Lucknow, came to the student counselling service three months after her mother's death. Once or twice a week, she said, just as she was drifting off to sleep, she heard her mother call "Rukhsana, beti" from the doorway, as clearly as if she were standing there. Each time she jerked awake, heart pounding, and found the room empty. She had begun to fear that she was "going mad", and an aunt had suggested that her mother's spirit was unsettled. There were no voices during the day, no other unusual experiences, her mood was sad but not depressed, and her sleep apart from these moments was ordinary. Both her grief and the timing of the voice made this an ordinary hypnagogic hallucination, of the kind experienced by more than a third of healthy people, given its content by bereavement. Being told so, and that it said nothing about her sanity, was most of the treatment.

Finally, a negative hallucination is the failure to perceive something that is actually present. The French physician Hippolyte Bernheim, of the Nancy school of hypnosis, used the term for a phenomenon of hypnosis, in which a subject told that an object is not there behaves as though he cannot see it, and the idea was later carried into the study of hysteria and dissociation. Negative autoscopy, not seeing oneself in the mirror, is one form. It is rare in ordinary clinical practice, and its main use in exams is as the opposite of the usual meaning of hallucination. Table 2.8 gathers the special types, and Figure 2.5 draws four of them: the functional, reflex, extracampine and autoscopic hallucinations.

Table 2.8 The special types of hallucination.
TypeDefining featureExampleWhere found
FunctionalReal stimulus, same sense; both perceived togetherA voice in the cooker's whistle, stopping with itSchizophrenia
ReflexReal stimulus in one sense, hallucination in anotherTemple bell, then a hand on the shoulderSchizophrenia, drug states
ExtracampineOutside the possible sensory fieldSeeing someone behind one's chairSchizophrenia, epilepsy, organic states
AutoscopicSeeing one's own double"My double stood at the door"Epilepsy, temporal and parietal lesions
HypnagogicWhile falling asleepName called as one dozes offNormal people (about 37 percent); narcolepsy
HypnopompicWhile waking upA figure at the bedside on wakingNormal people (about 12.5 percent); narcolepsy
LilliputianTiny people or animalsSmall men on the window sillDelirium tremens and other deliria; others
CommandA voice ordering an act"Jump from the roof"Schizophrenia, psychotic depression
NegativeFailing to perceive what is presentNot seeing a chair after hypnotic suggestionHypnosis, dissociation
Exam trap

Hypnagogic hallucinations (falling asleep) occur most commonly in normal people, not in schizophrenia, delirium or drug dependence; Ohayon's survey found them in about 37 percent of adults, and DSM-5-TR counts them as normal. Narcolepsy is the disorder that features them (20 to 60 percent). Hypnopompic is on waking. Functional means the same sense, reflex a different sense, extracampine outside the sensory field, autoscopic seeing oneself. A functional hallucination is not an illusion: the trigger is perceived correctly and the voice is added to it.

Hallucinations in healthy people

A student who has just learned the word "hallucination" tends to hear "madness" in it. The evidence says otherwise. A great many hallucinations happen to people who are not ill, and a clinician who does not know this will turn ordinary human experiences into diagnoses. You have already met two of the commonest: the hallucinations at the borders of sleep, reported by more than a third of adults, and the sensed presence of the exhausted and endangered. Several other situations reliably produce them.

The first is bereavement. W. Dewi Rees, a general practitioner in mid-Wales, set out to interview every widowed person living in a defined area of the region, and he reached 293 of them, 227 widows and 66 widowers, 80.7 percent of all the widowed people in the area. Published in the British Medical Journal in 1971 as "The hallucinations of widowhood", his study found that almost half had experienced hallucinations or illusions of the dead spouse: feeling the spouse's presence, seeing or hearing him or her, even being spoken to. Men and women reported them equally. The experiences often continued for years, though they were commonest in the first ten years of widowhood; they were not linked to social isolation or to depressive illness, and they were more frequent after long and happy marriages. Most striking of all, people rarely mentioned them to anyone, even to close friends and relatives, for fear of being thought mad. Rees concluded that these hallucinations were a normal and often helpful accompaniment of widowhood.

Key study: The hallucinations of widowhood (Rees, 1971)

Question: How often do bereaved people perceive their dead spouse? Method: A general practitioner interviewed 293 widowed people, more than four-fifths of all those living in a defined part of mid-Wales. Result: Almost half had hallucinations or illusions of the dead spouse, often for years; they were unrelated to depression or isolation and commoner after long, happy marriages, and they had rarely been disclosed to anyone. Conclusion: Perceiving the dead is a normal part of grief, not a sign of illness. Why it matters: A clinician who asks gently will often be told; one who reacts with alarm will teach the bereaved to stay silent.

The second situation is a lack or monotony of sensory input. People kept in darkness and silence, prisoners in solitary confinement, sailors alone at sea and drivers on long night journeys begin to misperceive and then to hallucinate, and the experimental studies of sensory deprivation are described in Book 1, Sections 3.12.3 and 5.11. Charles Bonnet syndrome, below, is a natural experiment of the same kind, in which the deprivation comes from a failing eye. The third is lack of sleep: after several nights with little or no sleep, many healthy people begin to see things at the edges of vision, then more definite visual hallucinations. The fourth is extreme stress, danger and exhaustion, the setting of Shackleton's fourth companion. And the fifth is religious and cultural experience, which DSM-5-TR explicitly recognises when it states that hallucinations may be a normal part of religious experience in certain cultural contexts.

Indian context

A devotee who sees the deity during a pilgrimage, a woman who sees her dead grandmother standing in the courtyard during the annual ceremony for the ancestors, a man who hears his guru's voice during meditation: in India such experiences are common, are often shared and sanctioned by the person's community, and are not by themselves symptoms. The questions that matter are the same in every culture. Is the experience accepted as normal by the person's own group? Is it brief, and tied to a ritual or a time of grief? Does it bring distress or disability? Is it accompanied by other symptoms, such as delusions, disorganisation or a decline in functioning? Book 2, Section 1.6.8 discusses how culture shapes the form of distress, and Section 4.2.3 the community surveys showing how many people have psychotic-like experiences without ever becoming ill.

Charles Bonnet syndrome

Key study: Charles Bonnet's grandfather (1760)

The Swiss naturalist and philosopher Charles Bonnet (1720 to 1793), of Geneva, is remembered in biology for his work on insects. In his Essai analytique sur les facultés de l'âme (Analytical Essay on the Faculties of the Soul) of 1760 he described the experiences of his grandfather, Charles Lullin, then in his late eighties (sources differ on his exact age; 87 is the figure most often given) and nearly blind from cataracts in both eyes. Lullin, a man of sound mind, saw men, women, birds, carriages, buildings, tapestries and patterns like scaffolding that were not there, sometimes in impossible circumstances, and he knew perfectly well that they were not real. Two centuries later, in 1967, the Swiss neurologist Georges de Morsier gave the condition its name in a paper whose title describes it exactly: "Le syndrome de Charles Bonnet: hallucinations visuelles des vieillards sans déficience mentale" (visual hallucinations of the elderly without mental deficiency).

Charles Bonnet syndrome is today defined as complex visual hallucinations in a person with significant loss of vision, intact cognition and, usually, preserved insight. The visual loss can come from any part of the visual system, but in practice it is mostly age-related macular degeneration, glaucoma, cataract and diabetic retinopathy. In different studies the syndrome has been reported in roughly 10 to 40 percent of older people with marked visual loss, and since most of them never mention it, the true figure is uncertain. The hallucinations are typically vivid, silent and detailed: faces, often in strange costume; people, sometimes small; children; animals; buildings and landscapes; repeating patterns such as brickwork, grids and lattices; and printed text. Pinel and Barnes note that the content can reflect the person's own life: some musicians with the syndrome see musical notation. The images come and go, last from seconds to hours, and are usually neither frightening nor accompanied by delusions, though some people find them distressing.

Why does a failing eye produce visions? The favoured explanation is deafferentation, or release: when the visual cortex is deprived of its normal input from the eyes, its neurons become more excitable and begin to generate activity of their own, which is experienced as seeing. It is the visual counterpart of the phantom limb, in which an amputee goes on feeling the missing limb (Section 2.10). Dominic ffytche and his colleagues in London (1998) used functional brain imaging in people with the syndrome and found that while they were hallucinating, activity increased in the specialised visual areas of the cortex, and that hallucinations in colour went with activity in the areas specialised for colour: the brain was "seeing" with the same machinery it uses for real vision, only without input from the eye. Because the person usually knows that the images are not real, Michael Eysenck and Mark Keane point out that they are, strictly, pseudohallucinations in the British sense of Figure 2.4.

Sanjay, an 81-year-old retired railway station master, was brought by his son to the cataract clinic of an eye hospital in Madurai. He had cataracts in both eyes and macular degeneration in the left. For some months, he said, he had been seeing schoolchildren in uniform sitting quietly on the end of his cot, a row of small men in turbans on the window sill and red brickwork covering the white wall of his room. "My eyes are playing a cinema," he said, and laughed, but he admitted that he had told no one at first because he feared that people would say he had gone mad. His family had begun to wonder whether it was dementia, and a neighbour had suggested that the house was troubled by spirits. Sanjay was fully oriented, recalled the morning's news correctly, scored normally on a cognitive screening test, heard no voices and held no unusual beliefs. The diagnosis was Charles Bonnet syndrome. The explanation itself relieved him greatly, and after the cataract in his right eye was removed the visions came less often.

Clinical link

Ask about visual hallucinations in every older person with poor sight, directly and kindly, because most will not volunteer them. If cognition is intact, insight is preserved and there are no other psychotic features, the treatment is explanation and reassurance, attention to the eyes (glasses, cataract surgery, treatment of the underlying disease, better lighting) and simple tricks such as blinking, moving the eyes or changing the lighting when the images appear. Antipsychotic medicine is not the first step. The danger, in India as elsewhere, is the opposite mistake: an elderly man with cataracts who says he sees children on his bed being labelled psychotic or demented, or his family being told that a spirit is responsible.

Exam trap

Charles Bonnet syndrome means visual hallucinations in a cognitively intact person with visual loss, with insight preserved. Each wrong option names a different phenomenon: auditory hallucinations in deaf people are musical or release hallucinations of hearing loss; auditory hallucinations in dementia belong to the dementia (where hallucinations are more often visual, as in Lewy body disease); tactile hallucinations in alcohol withdrawal are formication; olfactory hallucinations before seizures are temporal lobe auras (uncinate fits); multimodal hallucinations in delirium belong to delirium, with its clouding. Bonnet described his grandfather in 1760; de Morsier named the syndrome in 1967.

Hallucinosis and the clues to an organic cause

The word hallucinosis is used for persistent or recurrent hallucinations in clear consciousness, without the other features of a psychosis such as primary delusions or disorganised thinking, and often with some awareness that the experiences are abnormal. Alcoholic hallucinosis, with its accusing voices in a clear mind, has been described above (and in full in Chapter 10). ICD-10 gave the organic form its own category, F06.0 organic hallucinosis, for persistent or recurrent hallucinations due to brain disease or another physical cause; the peduncular hallucinosis of midbrain lesions is one example.

The idea of hallucinosis leads naturally to the question a clinician must answer about every hallucination: is the cause organic, or is this a functional psychosis such as schizophrenia or a mood disorder with psychotic features? No single clue settles it, but several together are strong evidence, and Table 2.9 lists them. The general logic is simple. Organic hallucinations tend to come from a disturbance in the sensory system or in the brain's arousal, so they favour the senses other than hearing, appear with clouding or fluctuation, are often brief and stereotyped, begin later in life, come with sensory loss or a drug, and leave insight intact. The hallucinations of a functional psychosis tend to be voices, heard in a clear mind, talking about the person, believed to be real and woven together with delusions.

Table 2.9 Clues to the cause of a hallucination: organic or functional?
FeatureSuggests an organic causeSuggests a functional psychosis
SenseVisual, olfactory, gustatory or tactileAuditory, especially voices
ConsciousnessClouded or fluctuatingClear
Age at first onsetLater lifeAdolescence or early adulthood
FormBrief, stereotyped, elementary (as in an aura)Complex voices; third-person, commenting
InsightOften preservedUsually lost; taken as real
Link to a substanceIntoxication, withdrawal, medicationNone
Sensory lossFailing eyes or earsUsually none
CompanyDisorientation, memory loss, neurological signsDelusions, formal thought disorder, negative symptoms
Exam trap

Hallucinosis means hallucinations in clear consciousness without the rest of a psychosis (alcoholic hallucinosis, organic hallucinosis, peduncular hallucinosis). Delirium tremens has clouding and visual hallucinations; alcoholic hallucinosis has clear consciousness and voices. A hallucination in a clear mind, taken as real, in the third person, with delusions, points to a functional psychosis; a visual, olfactory or tactile one, with clouding, sensory loss, a drug or preserved insight, points to an organic cause. These are clues, not rules: about a quarter of people with schizophrenia also see things.

Two experiences that are sometimes listed among the disorders of perception are taught elsewhere in this chapter. Depersonalisation and derealisation, in which the self or the world feels unreal, are changes in the experience of the self and are taught with the self in Section 2.10. Déjà vu, jamais vu and their relatives are disorders of the recognition of familiarity and are taught with memory in Section 2.7. The next section turns from what the person perceives to how he thinks, and to the speech through which his thinking can be observed.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. The mildest lowering of consciousness, the core of delirium, is of consciousness.
  2. The form of delirium that is most often missed and mistaken for depression is the form.
  3. A narrowed consciousness in which the person carries out ordered, apparently purposeful acts and is amnesic afterwards, classically after epileptic seizures, is a state.
  4. Full consciousness with paralysis of everything except vertical eye movements and blinking, after a lesion of the ventral pons, is the syndrome.
  5. Orientation is lost in the order time, place and person, and it is that is lost first.
  6. A real object perceived as changed in size, such as a face that looks the size of a coin, is called .
  7. Faces seen in clouds or in a mosaic floor, which persist and grow richer with attention, are an example of .
  8. Insects felt crawling under the skin in heavy cocaine use are called .
  9. A voice heard only when the pressure cooker whistles, together with the whistle, is a hallucination.
  10. Complex visual hallucinations in a cognitively intact older person with visual loss and preserved insight make up syndrome.

Answers: (1) clouding, (2) hypoactive, (3) twilight, (4) locked-in, (5) time, (6) micropsia, (7) pareidolia, (8) formication, (9) functional, (10) Charles Bonnet.

2.4 Disorders of Thought and Speech: Stream, Form and PossessionMedium yield · 7 Qs

On a Monday morning in the psychiatry outpatient department of a district hospital in Andhra Pradesh, a trainee asks three new patients the same simple question: "What work do you do?" Hari, a 24-year-old who sells mobile phone covers in Vijayawada, starts answering before she has finished asking: "Phones, covers, covers for phones and covers for books, I read six books last night, nights are for thinkers, thinkers and tinkers, I will tinker with the whole economy, doctor, I am opening forty shops." Venkat, a 31-year-old who used to work in a cement factory, answers after a long pause: "I worked. The pillars are sixty feet. The river at Kurnool is wide. The colour of the manager is a Tuesday colour." A retired schoolmaster with a long history of temporal lobe seizures takes nearly five minutes to say that he taught Telugu for thirty-two years, having first described the school building, the headmaster who appointed him, the bus he caught every morning and the price of a teacher's diary in 1979. None of the three has yet said anything about what he believes. Yet the trainee already knows a great deal about each of them, and she has learned it almost entirely from the way the words were joined together.

This section is about that joining. It covers the speed and flow of thinking (its stream), the organisation of the links between one idea and the next (its form), the sense that one's thoughts are one's own (its possession), and the disorders of speech and language that must be told apart from disorders of thought before any conclusion about thinking is drawn. What a person thinks about, the content of thought, including the delusions, is the subject of Section 2.5.

Thought as it is heard: the four headings

Begin with a limitation that shapes the whole subject. Nobody has ever observed a thought. A clinician hears speech and watches behaviour, and everything said about the patient's thinking is an inference from those. The American psychiatrist Nancy Coover Andreasen (born 1938) built her research on this point: because thought is known only through speech, she argued, what clinicians call "thought disorder" is more honestly a disorder of thought, language and communication. That is why a good case record always quotes a sample of the patient's own words beside the technical term. Another clinician can then check the inference.

The distinction between how ideas are linked and what they say is older than any modern textbook. A historical review by Jerónimo and colleagues (2018) traces it to Wilhelm Griesinger (1817 to 1868), who in 1867 separated "formal" deviations of thinking from its "false contents". The British descriptive tradition that Indian case records follow, set out in Frank Fish's Clinical Psychopathology and Andrew Sims' Symptoms in the Mind (Section 2.1), turned that separation into four headings, and Book 2, Section 2.5.1 sets them out in full in a table of its own. In brief: the stream of thought is its flow, both its tempo (how fast ideas come) and its continuity (whether the flow is interrupted); the form of thought is the logical organisation of the links between ideas; the content of thought is what the person thinks about; and the possession of thought is the ordinary, unnoticed experience that one's thoughts belong to oneself and are under one's own control. American texts and most examination keys use two headings instead: thought process, which takes in the stream and the form together, and thought content, into which the possession phenomena are folded as delusions of control. German psychopathology, for its part, files the possession phenomena among the disturbances of the self (in German, Ich-Störungen), and Section 2.10 looks at them through that lens.

The headings are not just a filing system. Each asks a different question about the same sample of speech, and one sample can fail on several at once. Hari's answer in the opening is fast (stream), its links are rhymes and chance associations (form, or in some British texts stream again), and it ends with a plan to open forty shops (content: a grandiose idea, Section 2.5). Venkat's answer is slow to start (stream) and its links cannot be followed at all (form). The schoolmaster's is slow and over-detailed, yet it arrives (form, or stream in Fish's filing). Learning the headings is learning to ask all four questions of every answer.

Remember

Stream asks how fast and how continuous; form asks how the ideas are linked; content asks what is thought; possession asks whose the thoughts feel to be. American usage merges stream and form as "thought process" and puts possession under "content". Delusions and the other disorders of content are taught in Section 2.5.

The stream I: tempo

Think of the stream of thought as a river. Its tempo can run too fast or too slow, and each extreme has its own vocabulary. Speed first, because the fast forms are the ones examiners ask about most.

Pressure of thought is the subjective side of acceleration: the person reports that ideas are crowding into the mind faster than he can deal with them, often with an uncomfortable sense of being driven. "My head is like a railway station at rush hour," a woman in a hypomanic episode once put it; "everything arrives at once and nothing leaves." The everyday phrase is racing thoughts. Pressure of thought is reported by the patient; what the clinician hears is usually pressure of speech (Section 2.4.6) and, when the links between the ideas begin to give way, flight of ideas. DSM-5-TR puts the two sides together in one of its criteria for a manic episode, "flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing", and adds in its description that thoughts may race faster than speech can express them and may feel so crowded that speaking becomes difficult.

Flight of ideas is the observed form. Thoughts follow one another very rapidly; each idea is abandoned before it is completed; there is no general direction to the thinking; and yet each link from one idea to the next can be followed, because it is made by chance associations of meaning, by the sound of words, or by something the patient notices around him. Here is Hari again, a few minutes later, asked how he has been sleeping:

"Sleeping is for losers, I sleep two hours, two hours is enough for a lion, lions live in Gir, Gir cows give the best milk, milk and honey, honey, that is a lovely pen, is it a Parker? Park the car, I am buying a car tomorrow, a white one, white like your coat, coats of paint, I am painting the whole house tonight."

Take it apart and every hinge is visible. Two hours of sleep suggests strength, and strength suggests a lion; lions live in the Gir forest of Gujarat, and Gir is also a famous breed of cow; cows give milk; "milk and honey" is a stock phrase; the clinician's pen catches his eye (distraction by the surroundings, which DSM-5-TR describes as attention drawn to "the interviewer's attire, background noises or conversations, furnishings in the room"); "Parker" becomes "park" by sound; the car becomes white because the doctor's coat is white; "coats" slides into "coats of paint" by a pun. Three kinds of link carry the train: links of meaning that are loose but logical, links of sound (rhymes, puns and clang associations, in which a word is chosen for its sound rather than its sense), and links supplied by the room. What is lost is the goal. The question was about sleep, and the answer has ended up with house-painting, but the listener can reconstruct every step. That reconstructability is the single most important fact about flight of ideas, because it separates it from the loosening of associations of Section 2.4.4, where the steps themselves cannot be followed.

Flight of ideas is the characteristic thought disorder of mania. In hypomania it is often milder and better ordered, and a milder form with the same features has its own name. In prolixity the patient wanders through many irrelevant side-paths but, unlike the patient with flight of ideas, does keep the goal in view and returns to it: asked about sleep, the hypomanic patient may talk about lions, cows and milk, and then say, "but you asked about sleep, doctor: two hours." Prolixity is sometimes described as an ordered flight of ideas. When flight of ideas is very severe, on the other hand, DSM-5-TR notes that speech may become disorganised and incoherent, and the links that were once visible can no longer be traced. At that point the manic patient's speech can look exactly like the speech of schizophrenia, which is one reason Andreasen's 1979 study (Book 2, Section 2.5.3) found the "positive" forms of thought disorder in both. Section 4.2.4 and its Table 4.7 set out the usual differences between the two.

Now slow the river down. In retardation of thought, also called inhibition of thought or bradyphrenia, thinking itself is slowed. Fewer ideas present themselves, each arrives slowly, and the patient finds it an effort to think at all. Geeta, a 58-year-old retired nurse from Guntur in a severe depressive episode, waited many seconds before each answer and then said: "Everything takes so long. I begin to think what to cook, and before I reach the end of the thought the morning is gone." DSM-5-TR lists slowed speech, thinking and body movements, with longer pauses before answering, among the forms of psychomotor retardation in depression (the motor side is taught in Section 2.9). Retardation of thought is also a feature of hypothyroidism, and "bradyphrenia" is the usual word for the slowed thinking of Parkinson's disease and other disorders of the basal ganglia.

Poverty of thought is different again. Here it is not the speed but the number of thoughts that falls: the mind feels empty, nothing comes, and the patient may say exactly that. A man with long-standing schizophrenia, asked what he thinks about during the day, answered: "Nothing. I sit. There is nothing inside to think." Poverty of thought occurs in severe depression, in the negative syndrome of schizophrenia and in dementia. Its speech side is poverty of speech, or alogia, which DSM-5-TR defines simply as diminished speech output and counts among the negative symptoms (Section 4.3.2). Keep three things apart: retardation is thinking that is slowed but present, poverty is thinking that is scanty, and thought block (next subsection) is thinking that stops dead.

Exam trap

Flight of ideas is a rapid shift from one idea to another before any is completed, with links that can be followed (chance associations, rhymes, puns, clangs, distraction by the surroundings), so the goal is lost although every step makes sense. The distractors in the usual question are other phenomena: repeating the same idea over and over is perseveration (Section 2.4.3); preoccupation with irrelevant details is circumstantiality (Section 2.4.4); lack of spontaneity in speech is poverty of speech (Section 2.4.6). One explanation in circulation calls the links of flight of ideas "loose". Avoid that word here: loose associations are the schizophrenic form, in which the links cannot be followed. The links of flight of ideas are rapid but followable.

The stream II: continuity

A stream can also be interrupted. In thought block (thought blocking) the train of thought stops suddenly and completely, often in the middle of a sentence, leaving a blank. After a pause that may last many seconds, the patient may start again on a different subject, and he cannot say what he was about to say. Venkat, describing his last day at the factory, said: "The foreman came to the kiln and he said that I should..." He stopped. His eyes stayed on the clinician; nothing in the room had distracted him; after about fifteen seconds he said, "The canteen has good idlis," and when asked what the foreman had said, he answered, "It went. I don't know where it went."

Everybody loses the thread now and then, especially when tired, anxious or interrupted, and that is not thought block. The features that mark the real thing are its suddenness, its completeness and the absence of any ordinary explanation. Three look-alikes have to be excluded. A patient who stops because he is listening to voices usually shows it (he glances aside, tilts his head or mouths a reply; Book 2, Section 2.4.2 describes hallucinatory behaviour). An epileptic absence produces a brief gap of which the person is usually unaware, often with a blank stare and sometimes with fluttering eyelids. And an anxious person who "goes blank" in front of an examiner knows why it happened. True thought block is seen most characteristically in schizophrenia, although Andreasen found it rarer than its fame suggests.

Notice what kind of phenomenon thought block is. It is a sign: the clinician observes it. If the patient is asked about the blank and explains that someone or something took his thoughts away, he is describing a symptom of a different kind, thought withdrawal, which is a disorder of possession (Section 2.4.7). Venkat, asked a week later what happened when his thoughts "went", said: "The manager pulls them out. He has a hook for it." The observed gap was thought block; the experience he reported was thought withdrawal. Section 2.1 explains why so many phenomena have an observed and a reported side like this.

Perseveration is the opposite failure of continuity. Instead of stopping, the stream gets stuck: a word, phrase or idea that was appropriate a moment ago keeps coming back in response to new questions, as if the person could not shift set. A 68-year-old retired postmaster recovering from a stroke in the frontal lobe was shown a series of objects. He named the first, a pen, correctly. Shown a watch, he said "pen". Shown a key, he said "pen... no... pen". Asked his date of birth a minute later, he gave it correctly, and then gave the same date when asked the year of his retirement. Perseveration strongly suggests organic brain disease, above all damage to the frontal lobes, and it is common in dementia, in some aphasias and in delirium; it is uncommon in the functional psychoses. The same stuck quality can affect actions (continuing to wipe a table long after it is clean), and that perseveration of action is described with the motor signs in Section 2.9.

Perseveration belongs to a family of repetitions that examiners like to mix together, so learn the family now. In perseveration the person repeats his own earlier response when a new one is called for. In echolalia he repeats the examiner's words (Section 2.9). In palilalia he repeats his own last word or phrase, again and again, and in verbigeration he repeats meaningless words or phrases as a stereotypy of speech (both in Section 2.4.6). And the obsessional patient's thought that keeps returning is not perseveration at all: it is a recurrent idea that he recognises as his own and fights against (Section 2.5.7).

Exam trap

Thought block is an observed, sudden, complete stop in the stream; thought withdrawal is the patient's experience that an outside agency removed the thoughts (a disorder of possession). Perseveration repeats the patient's own earlier response to new questions and points to organic, especially frontal, brain disease; it is not echolalia (repeating the examiner), palilalia (repeating one's own last words), verbigeration (stereotyped meaningless repetition) or an obsession (a recurrent thought recognised as one's own and resisted). In British filing, thought block and perseveration are disorders of the stream (continuity).

Form I: when the links loosen

Normal thinking is goal-directed. Asked a question, a person travels from the question to the answer through a chain of ideas, each linked to the next in a way the listener can follow. Formal thought disorder is a disturbance in those links, inferred from the patient's speech: the chain bends, wanders, breaks or dissolves. British texts in Fish's tradition distinguish a negative and a positive type. In the negative type the patient has lost an ability he once had and cannot produce a concept at all; in the positive type he produces false concepts by blending together elements that do not belong together. Andreasen later drew a related but different line between "positive" forms, such as derailment and incoherence, and "negative" forms, such as poverty of speech and poverty of content (Book 2, Section 2.5.3). The forms are best learned in order of severity, and Figure 2.6 draws them as the rungs of a ladder.

The idea that the links between ideas can loosen comes from Eugen Bleuler (1857 to 1939), the Swiss psychiatrist who named schizophrenia in 1908. For Bleuler the disturbance of association was the fundamental symptom of the illness, the first of the four that students remember as the four As (associations, affect, ambivalence and autism; Section 4.1.4 tells the story). In loosening of associations the connection between successive ideas is lost or so remote that the listener cannot reconstruct it, and the patient seems unaware that anything is amiss. Look again at Venkat's answer from the opening: "I worked. The pillars are sixty feet. The river at Kurnool is wide. The colour of the manager is a Tuesday colour." You might guess a private hinge between "worked" and "pillars" (the factory had pillars), but there is no road at all from the pillars to the river, and "a Tuesday colour" belongs to no shared language. Compare Hari's answer about sleep. There you could annotate every step; here you can annotate almost none. British clinicians call the abrupt, oblique jumps knight's move thinking, after the chess knight, which moves two squares in one direction and then one to the side, so that it lands somewhere the eye did not expect.

Andreasen preferred the term derailment, because by the 1970s "loose associations" was being applied to almost any speech that puzzled the listener. In derailment the patient's ideas slip off one track onto another that is clearly but obliquely related, or onto one that is completely unrelated; the slippage may happen between clauses or between sentences, and the patient may slide further and further from the starting point. DSM-5-TR uses the two terms together ("derailment or loose associations") for the switch from one topic to another, and it counts severely disorganised speech of this kind among the core symptoms of the psychotic disorders, with the warning that mild disorganisation is common and nonspecific and that only speech disordered enough to impair communication substantially should count.

Tangentiality is the same failure seen in answers to questions. DSM-5-TR describes it as answers that are "obliquely related or completely unrelated" to the question. Andreasen drew the boundary neatly: tangentiality refers to replies to questions, and derailment to the transitions within spontaneous speech. The tangential answer usually begins from something in the question, often just a word, and then heads off in another direction and never comes back. Asked why he stopped going to work, Venkat said: "Work is a word with four letters. Letters come by post. My uncle was a postmaster in Nandyal, and Nandyal has very hot summers." The word "work" launched the reply; the question was never answered. Tangentiality occurs in schizophrenia and also in mania, and in milder degrees in tired, anxious or distractible people.

Circumstantiality is the gentlest disorder of form, and the one most often confused with tangentiality. The circumstantial speaker takes a long, indirect route to the goal, loaded with unnecessary and trivial detail and parenthetical asides, so that the listener grows impatient and may try to hurry him along. But he never loses the thread, and in the end he reaches the point and answers the question. The schoolmaster in the opening is the classic picture: he answered the question (he taught Telugu for thirty-two years) after a tour of the building, the headmaster, the bus and the price of a diary. The difference from tangentiality is simply whether the goal is reached, and a useful bedside test follows from it. Let the circumstantial patient run on, and he arrives; let the tangential patient run on, and he goes further away. Circumstantiality is seen classically in epilepsy, especially temporal lobe epilepsy, in people with obsessional personalities, in intellectual disability and early dementia, and in pedantic or anxious people who fear leaving anything out; a mild degree is normal in many talkers, and in Andreasen's data it leaned towards mania rather than schizophrenia. British definitions describe it as thinking that proceeds slowly, with many unnecessary and trivial details, before the point is finally reached, and that emphasis on slowness explains why British texts file it under the stream.

Exam trap

Tangentiality, derailment, loosening of associations and circumstantiality are disorders of the form or process of thought. They are not disorders of content (they say nothing about what the patient believes), of possession (the patient's thoughts feel entirely his own), of perception or of the level of consciousness. The complication, which Book 2, Section 2.5.1 also flags: Fish and many Indian notes list circumstantiality and tangentiality (and flight of ideas) under the stream, because they change how thinking flows towards its goal. So choose form (or "thought process") whenever it is offered, and choose stream only if form is not among the options. Circumstantiality reaches the goal; tangentiality never does. Loosening and knight's move thinking are Bleuler's fundamental symptom of schizophrenia, not a first-rank symptom.

Form II: incoherence, private words and the logic of concepts

At the top of the ladder, disorganisation reaches inside the sentence. In incoherence, also called word salad or schizophasia, the words no longer join even grammatically, so that nothing can be understood: "Window of the brother slowly lamp the because pension." DSM-5-TR describes this as speech "so severely disorganized that it is nearly incomprehensible", resembling receptive aphasia in its linguistic disorganisation, and it adds the word "rarely": true word salad is uncommon and is usually seen only in severe, often chronic, schizophrenia. It is the most severe formal thought disorder. Because it can look like the jargon of a stroke patient with receptive dysphasia, it is one of the places where a neurological examination is part of the psychiatric one (Section 2.4.6).

Disorganised speech also bends individual words. A neologism is a new word coined by the patient, or an ordinary word used in a private sense that only he understands: "They put prandomite in my tea so that I cannot sleep." Andreasen added the word approximation, in which an existing word is used in a new, unconventional way, or a new word is built from ordinary parts by the ordinary rules, so that a listener can often guess the meaning: a bedsheet becomes "my sleeping-cloth". Closely related is the metonym, a term of Norman Cameron (1896 to 1975), the Canadian-born American psychiatrist and psychologist who studied schizophrenic reasoning from the 1930s: an approximate, imprecise word or phrase is used in place of the exact one, usually because it is related to it in experience. "I came here by ticket," a patient says, meaning that he came by bus.

Cameron is best known for a different idea, and it is examined often. In a 1938 monograph on reasoning and communication in schizophrenia, and more fully in a 1944 chapter on the experimental analysis of schizophrenic thinking, he argued that the trouble with schizophrenic concepts is not that they are too narrow but that they are too wide. He called this overinclusive thinking (over-inclusion): the boundaries of concepts widen, so that ideas, objects and personal associations that are only loosely related are swept into the concept and into the line of reasoning. Asked to pick out from a pile of objects "the things used for writing", Venkat chose the pen and the pencil, and then the table "because you write on it", the lamp "because you need it to see the paper", and finally his own hand. Cameron described other features too: asyndesis, a lack of genuine connections between successive thoughts, so that thinking becomes a loose collection of fragments rather than a sequence; the metonyms above; and the "interpenetration" of the patient's personal themes into whatever task he is given.

Key study: is over-inclusion a schizophrenic sign? (Andreasen and Powers, 1975)

Cameron had described over-inclusion as a feature of schizophrenic thinking, and for decades it was treated as one. Nancy Andreasen and her colleague Powers asked whether it was really specific. They gave the Goldstein-Scheerer object sorting test, a task in which the person groups everyday objects and explains why they belong together, to three groups: patients with mania, patients with schizophrenia and creative writers.

The result reversed the expectation. The writers and the manic patients were the over-inclusive ones: both grouped objects in wide, unusual categories, the writers richly and the manic patients more idiosyncratically. The patients with schizophrenia tended instead to be under-inclusive, with less richness in their sorting. The authors concluded that if over-inclusion indexes thought disorder, mania may show it more floridly than schizophrenia. The lesson is the same one Andreasen drew from her 1979 study of speech: the forms of thought disorder are signs to be described precisely, not proof of any one diagnosis.

Cameron's view is usually set against a rival one, that the schizophrenic patient thinks too concretely. Kurt Goldstein (1878 to 1965), the German-American neurologist who had studied soldiers with brain wounds after the First World War, argued that brain damage robs a person of the abstract attitude: the capacity to step back from the immediate situation, to think in categories, to grasp what things have in common and to shift from one aspect of a situation to another. With his colleague Martin Scheerer he published a monograph in 1941, Abstract and Concrete Behavior, describing sorting tests for this capacity, and he later applied the idea to schizophrenia. Concrete thinking is the result: thinking tied to the literal, the particular and the immediate, unable to deal in metaphor or general rules. Asked what "empty vessels make the most noise" means, a concrete thinker answers, "If a pot is empty and you hit it, it rings, because nothing is inside." Concrete thinking occurs in organic brain disease and dementia, in intellectual disability and in schizophrenia, and it is the normal mode of young children (the Developmental book teaches Piaget's account). How proverbs and similarities are used to test for it, and why schooling, language and familiarity with the proverb must be taken into account before calling an answer concrete, is in Book 2, Section 2.6.7.

The German psychiatrist Carl Schneider (1891 to 1946), in a book on the psychology of schizophrenia published in 1930, proposed that normal thinking depends on the constancy, organisation and continuity of thought, and that all three break down in schizophrenia. Among the features he described were derailment, fusion (heterogeneous ideas merged into a single muddled one) and drivelling (a jumble of speech in which the intended meaning is lost, though its parts may be grammatical); English textbooks list his other features slightly differently, so learn these three. Do not confuse him with Kurt Schneider of the first-rank symptoms (Section 2.4.7), a different man with a different contribution. Earlier still, the German neuropsychiatrist Karl Kleist (1879 to 1960) described in 1914 patients who used particular words and phrases in idiosyncratic ways, which he called stock words and stock phrases (Section 2.4.6).

The last group of forms comes from Andreasen's 1979 definitions, which became her Scale for the Assessment of Thought, Language and Communication (Book 2, Section 2.5.3; the scale itself belongs to the Assessment book). Several have already appeared under their own names: derailment, tangentiality, incoherence, neologisms, word approximations, circumstantiality, clanging, blocking and perseveration. The others are worth knowing because they turn up as options. Poverty of content of speech is speech that is adequate in amount but conveys little information, because it is vague, empty, over-abstract or repetitive. Illogicality is reasoning in which conclusions do not follow from the premises: "My sister wears glasses, so she must be a spy." Loss of goal is the failure to follow a chain of thought through to its natural end. Distractible speech breaks off mid-sentence to comment on something nearby. Stilted speech is excessively formal, pompous or old-fashioned for the situation. And self-reference is the repeated, inappropriate return of every topic to the patient himself. Table 2.10 gathers every disorder of stream and form in one place.

Exam trap

Word salad (incoherence, schizophasia) is the most severe formal thought disorder: disorganised speech in which even the grammar fails. It is not a negative symptom (alogia and poverty of speech are), not a perceptual disturbance, not a mood symptom and not a memory impairment; disorganised speech counts among the positive features of psychosis. A neologism is one invented word; word salad is a jumble of real words. Overinclusive thinking is Norman Cameron's; concrete thinking and the abstract attitude are Kurt Goldstein's; Carl Schneider (derailment, fusion, drivelling) is not Kurt Schneider (first-rank symptoms).

Clinical link

Formal thought disorder is judged against the listener's own language. DSM-5-TR warns that the severity of disorganised speech is hard to judge when the clinician comes from a different linguistic background, and that alogia must not be confused with a language barrier. A Kannada-speaking farmer interviewed in halting Hindi may give short, disconnected answers that sound tangential or impoverished in Hindi and perfectly coherent in Kannada. Before writing "loosening of associations", make sure the patient was interviewed in the language he thinks in, or through a trained interpreter (Book 2, Section 2.2.9).

Disorders of speech and language

Everything so far has assumed that the speech apparatus and the language system are intact, so that speech is a fair window onto thought. That assumption has to be checked before any disorder of thought is diagnosed, because a fault in speech or language can imitate one. Book 2, Section 2.4.5 lists the dimensions of speech recorded in every examination (rate, volume, tone, quantity, spontaneity, latency, articulation and relevance). This subsection teaches the abnormal phenomena themselves.

The first distinction is between speech and language. Dysarthria is a disorder of articulation: the words are chosen and ordered correctly, but the muscles that form them, or their nerve supply, do not work properly, so speech sounds slurred, thick, explosive or indistinct. It occurs in intoxication with alcohol or sedatives, in cerebellar disease, after strokes affecting the brain stem, in motor neurone disease and as a side effect of some medicines, such as lithium at toxic levels. Dysphasia (aphasia when complete) is a disorder of language itself, caused by damage to the language areas of the dominant hemisphere: the person has difficulty finding, choosing, ordering or understanding words. In expressive (non-fluent) dysphasia speech is slow, effortful and telegraphic, with the small grammatical words dropped, and the person usually knows what he wants to say and is frustrated at not being able to say it. In receptive (fluent) dysphasia speech flows easily but is full of wrong or invented words and makes little sense, and comprehension is poor. That is the form that can be mistaken for word salad. The clues to a neurological cause are a sudden onset, often in an older person with vascular risk factors, a failure to understand simple spoken commands, errors in naming objects, other neurological signs, and the absence of the rest of a psychotic picture (the anatomy is the Physiological book's).

Then come the disorders of the amount of speech. Mutism is the complete absence of speech in a person who is awake. It is a sign, not a diagnosis, and it has many causes: the catatonic syndrome (Section 2.9), stupor of any kind (Section 2.2), severe depression, dissociative states, neurological disease, and in children the selective mutism of Section 6.4.8, in which a child speaks freely at home but not at school. At the other extreme, pressure of speech is speech that is increased in amount, rapid, often loud and difficult or impossible to interrupt; DSM-5-TR lists being "more talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking" among the criteria for a manic episode. Logorrhoea (literally a flow of words) is copious, voluble talk that the speaker cannot rein in, seen in mania and in some organic states. Between them lies poverty of speech or alogia (Section 2.4.2), the brief, unelaborated replies of the negative syndrome and of depression.

Several disorders are forms of repetition. Palilalia is the repetition of one's own last word or phrase, often several times, sometimes faster and more softly each time: "I want to go home, go home, go home, home." DSM-5-TR lists repeating one's own sounds or words (palilalia) among the complex vocal tics of the tic disorders, Tourette's disorder included (Chapter 3), and it is also seen in parkinsonism, pseudobulbar palsy and dementia. Logoclonia is the spasmodic repetition of a syllable within a word, usually the last one ("hospi-tal-tal-tal"); it is described in parkinsonism and above all in advanced Alzheimer's disease, and sources define it slightly differently. Echolalia, the repetition of the examiner's words, belongs with the catatonic signs and is taught in Section 2.9. Verbigeration is the repetition of meaningless words, phrases or sentences, sometimes for minutes or hours, in a monotonous, stereotyped way, as though speech had become a stereotyped movement; Karl Ludwig Kahlbaum (1828 to 1899) counted it among the signs of catatonia in 1874, and today it is seen mainly in chronic schizophrenia and catatonia. Stammering (stuttering) is a disruption of fluency by repeated or prolonged sounds and by blocks; it usually begins in childhood (the childhood-onset fluency disorder of Chapter 3), worsens with anxiety and has nothing to do with disorder of thought.

Some patients develop language that belongs to them alone. Kleist's stock words and stock phrases are ordinary words or expressions that a patient uses over and over with a special, private meaning, as a man with chronic schizophrenia who answered almost every question with "according to the system" and could not say what the system was. Taken to its extreme, with many neologisms and a private grammar, this becomes a private language that no one else can follow. Set against these is a form of speech that sounds disorganised but is entirely normal in its setting. DSM-5-TR notes that some religious groups practise glossolalia ("speaking in tongues") and that some cultures recognise states of possession trance, and that these are not signs of psychosis unless they come with other clearly psychotic symptoms. A worshipper speaking in tongues at a prayer meeting in Kerala, who stops when the meeting ends and is otherwise well, has no disorder of speech.

The last phenomenon is the one examiners ask about by its German name. In approximate answers, or Vorbeireden ("talking past the point"), the person gives answers to simple questions that are wrong but close to the right one, in a way that shows he has understood the question perfectly well. Asked how many wheels an autorickshaw has, he says "four"; asked what colour the sky is, "green"; asked to add three and three, "seven". The sign was described by the German psychiatrist Sigbert Ganser (1853 to 1931) in an 1898 paper on a peculiar hysterical twilight state, which he observed in prisoners awaiting trial in Halle, where the approximate answers came with a clouding of consciousness and were followed by amnesia. Approximate answers must be kept apart from the wrong answers of dementia and delirium (which miss the point because the question has not been understood), from confabulation (an invented memory that fills a gap, Section 2.7), from the flight of ideas of mania and from simple ignorance. Their association with stress and with situations in which illness might bring an advantage, such as a pending trial, also raises the question of deliberate feigning (Section 7.9.6). Ganser syndrome as a whole appears in the eponym table of Section 2.6 and is taught with the dissociative disorders in Section 7.6.7. Table 2.11 brings the disorders of speech together.

Table 2.11 The disorders of speech and language, the level each affects and the usual settings.
DisorderWhat is heardLevel affectedUsual settings
DysarthriaRight words, badly formed: slurred, thickArticulationIntoxication, cerebellar and brain stem disease, drug toxicity
Expressive dysphasiaSlow, effortful, telegraphic; knows what he wants to sayLanguageStroke or other lesion of the dominant frontal lobe
Receptive dysphasiaFluent jargon; poor comprehensionLanguageStroke or other lesion of the dominant temporal lobe
MutismNo speech in an awake personAmountCatatonia, stupor, severe depression, dissociation, selective mutism
Pressure of speech, logorrhoeaRapid, copious, hard to interruptAmountMania, hypomania, some organic states
Poverty of speech (alogia)Brief, unelaborated repliesAmountNegative syndrome of schizophrenia, depression
PalilaliaOwn last words repeated: "go home, home"RepetitionParkinsonism, Tourette's disorder, pseudobulbar palsy, dementia
LogocloniaA syllable repeated: "hospi-tal-tal"RepetitionAdvanced Alzheimer's disease, parkinsonism
