Exam trap

Dysarthria is a fault of articulation (the language is intact); dysphasia is a fault of language (finding, choosing or understanding words), and receptive dysphasia can imitate word salad. Among the repetitions: palilalia repeats one's own last words, logoclonia a syllable, echolalia the examiner's words, verbigeration meaningless stereotyped phrases, and perseveration an earlier response to a new question; "repetitive involuntary speech" describes palilalia or verbigeration, not a memory phenomenon such as déjà entendu (Section 2.7). Approximate answers (Ganser, 1898, prisoners awaiting trial) show that the question was understood; they are not confabulation. Glossolalia in a religious setting is not a sign of psychosis on its own. Mutism is a sign with many causes, not proof of catatonia.

Possession of thought: alienation and the made experiences

You do not normally wonder whether your thoughts are your own. When an idea occurs to you, it arrives already stamped as yours: you are the one thinking it, it arose in you, and you can turn your attention away from it. This sense of ownership and authorship is so basic that it goes unnoticed until it fails, and Section 2.10 places it within Karl Jaspers' account of the formal characteristics of self-awareness. The disorders of possession are the ways in which it fails. In the British vocabulary, thought alienation is the experience that one's thoughts are under the control of an outside agency, or that other people are taking part in one's thinking. It takes three classic forms, and in each the patient's statement has to be listened to closely, because the form of the experience, not its dramatic content, decides which it is.

In thought insertion the person experiences thoughts that are not his own being put into his mind. The thought is located inside his head, and he is aware of thinking it, yet it is felt as someone else's. Venkat explained: "A thought came into my head: 'Burn the papers.' It was not my thought. I know my own thoughts, the way you know your own handwriting, and this one was the manager's. He put it in from his office." Notice what he does not say. He does not hear the thought spoken (that would be a hallucination, Section 2.3). He does not say that it is his own thought, which he hates and cannot stop (that would be an obsession, below). He says that the thought itself is alien. Ownership, not location, is what has broken.

In thought withdrawal the person experiences his thoughts being taken out of his mind by an outside agency, leaving it suddenly empty. "I was thinking about my sister's wedding, and halfway through the thought it was lifted out, like a bucket pulled up out of a well. Then there was nothing. The manager took it." As Section 2.4.3 showed, the observed counterpart is thought block, and the clinician who sees a block should always ask the patient what happened to the thought.

In thought broadcasting, also called thought diffusion, the person experiences his thoughts as passing out of his mind so that other people know them, either because the thoughts spread out silently or because others can actually hear them. "When I sit in the bus my thoughts go out of my head and fill the whole bus. People turn and look, because they know what I am thinking." Keep it separate from three neighbours. In thought echo the person himself hears his thoughts spoken aloud; that is a hallucination (Section 2.3), although it sits beside these three in Schneider's list. In ideas and delusions of reference other people's remarks, gestures or the media are believed to refer to the person (Section 2.5). And the belief that a particular person can read one's mind, by telepathy or some machine, is a delusion about other people's powers rather than the experience that one's own thoughts are leaking out, although patients often describe both.

Exam trap

Thought broadcasting is the experience that one's thoughts pass out of one's mind so that others know them. Thought withdrawal is the experience that an outside force takes thoughts out. Thought insertion is the experience that alien thoughts are put in. Ideas of reference are the belief that other people's remarks or the media refer to oneself, a disorder of content (Section 2.5). Thought echo (Gedankenlautwerden) is hearing one's own thoughts spoken aloud, a hallucination (Section 2.3). All except ideas of reference are first-rank symptoms.

The same breakdown can affect feelings, drives, actions and the body. These are the passivity phenomena, also called the made experiences (from the German gemacht, "made"), in which the person experiences something that should be his own as imposed or controlled from outside. In made feelings an emotion is felt as not his own: "I am not sad. This sadness is put into me every evening from the transformer behind our house." In made impulses a drive or urge is felt to have been put into him, although he may carry out the act himself: "The urge to throw the plate came from them. I threw it, but the wanting was theirs." In made volitional acts the action itself is controlled, and the person feels like a spectator of his own body: "My hand picks up the spoon and puts it down again, and I am only watching. They are doing it." And in somatic passivity he experiences bodily sensations as imposed on him by an outside agency: "They send heat into my back from the mobile tower; I feel it at exactly nine every night." Somatic passivity must not be confused with its two neighbours: the somatic hallucination of Section 2.3 is the bodily sensation without a cause, and the somatic delusion of Section 2.5 is a false belief about the body; in somatic passivity the sensation is experienced as being done to the person from outside.

Why insist on the word "made"? Because ordinary life is full of feelings, urges and actions that we dislike, regret or struggle to control, and none of them is a passivity experience. A man who cannot resist a drink, a woman who is overwhelmed by anger at her mother-in-law, a student who scrolls his phone for hours against his own intentions: each experiences the urge or the feeling as his own, however unwelcome. The patient with a made impulse experiences the urge as authored by someone else. The patient with a made act experiences his body moving without his will. That loss of authorship, not the unpleasantness, is the sign. Patients usually add an explanation (the transformer, the tower, the manager, a spirit), and the explanation is a delusion of control, but the experience comes first.

DSM-5-TR treats these phenomena as delusions and says that delusions expressing a loss of control over mind or body, such as thought withdrawal, thought insertion and the belief that one's body or actions are manipulated by an outside force, are generally considered bizarre (Section 2.5.4). Look at all of them together and a single theme appears: the boundary between the self and the world has given way, so that thoughts, feelings, drives, acts and sensations that should be one's own are felt to come in from outside or to leak out. Figure 4.3 draws that theme, and Section 2.10 returns to it as a disorder of the self.

One more phenomenon is filed here in British usage, and it shows what possession means by contrast. Padma, a 26-year-old bank clerk in Chennai, is troubled during her morning puja by a blasphemous thought about the goddess. She says: "It is my own thought, my own dirty mind. I know nobody is putting it there. But it comes again and again, and the harder I push it away, the stronger it comes, so I start the prayers again from the beginning." That is an obsession (Section 2.5.7). Fish and many Indian texts list obsessions under the disorders of possession, because the person has lost control of her thinking: the thought intrudes against her will and cannot be dismissed. But possession in the obsessional sense is the opposite of thought insertion in the one respect that matters. Padma insists that the thought is hers; Venkat insists that his is not. American texts place obsessions under thought content, and Section 2.5 teaches them as a symptom. Table 2.12 lays the possession and passivity phenomena beside the obsession.

Table 2.12 The possession and passivity phenomena, what the person says, and how each differs from an obsession.
PhenomenonWhat the person saysHow it differs from an obsession
Thought insertion"That thought was not mine; the manager put it in."The thought is felt as someone else's; the obsessional thought is felt as one's own
Thought withdrawal"Halfway through, the thought was lifted out."Thoughts are removed by an outside agency; obsessions intrude and cannot be removed
Thought broadcasting (diffusion)"My thoughts fill the bus; everyone knows them."Thoughts escape and become public; obsessions stay private and shameful
Made feelings"This sadness is put into me from the transformer."The emotion is authored from outside; obsessional anxiety is one's own
Made impulses"I threw the plate, but the wanting was theirs."The urge is felt as implanted; an obsessional impulse is one's own and resisted
Made volitional acts"My hand moves the spoon; I only watch."The act is done without one's will; a compulsion is willed, however reluctantly
Somatic passivity"They send heat into my back at nine every night."A sensation imposed from outside; no counterpart in obsessions
Obsession (for contrast)"It is my own dirty thought, but I cannot stop it."Recognised as one's own, felt as senseless, resisted

These experiences have a special place in psychiatry because of Kurt Schneider (1887 to 1967), the Heidelberg psychiatrist who chose them, with a few others, as his first-rank symptoms of schizophrenia: experiences that could be identified reliably at a single interview and that, in the absence of organic brain disease, he regarded as strongly suggestive of the illness. The usual list has eleven items: three kinds of voices (audible thoughts or thought echo, voices arguing or discussing the patient in the third person, and voices giving a running commentary); thought insertion, withdrawal and broadcasting; made feelings, made impulses, made volitional acts and somatic passivity; and delusional perception (Section 2.5.2). Section 4.1.6 tells their history and gives the evidence that they are characteristic of schizophrenia but not specific to it (they also occur in mania and organic psychoses, and DSM-5-TR notes that the intruding identity states of dissociative identity disorder can resemble them); Book 2, Section 2.5.6 shows how to ask about them. What matters for this chapter is the list, and the items that are often mistaken for it.

Exam trap

Not first-rank: depersonalisation (an "as if" experience with insight, Section 2.10); loosening of associations (Bleuler's fundamental symptom, a sign the listener judges, Section 2.4.4); second-person voices ("You are useless") and hallucinations in senses other than hearing; delusional mood (only delusional perception is first-rank, Section 2.5.2); ideas or delusions of reference (defined by content, Section 2.5.5). An obsession is recognised as one's own thought and resisted; thought insertion is experienced as someone else's thought. Passivity means loss of authorship, not an urge that is merely strong or unwanted.

2.5 Delusions and the Other Disorders of Thought ContentHigh yield · 5 Qs

Geeta, the retired nurse from Guntur whose slowed thinking appeared in Section 2.4.2, told the doctor that her family was ruined. "There is no money left. We will have to sell the house and go and live in my brother's back room, and it is my fault, because when I was working I took money from the families of patients." She never had. Her son put her bank passbook on the table, open at a page that showed several lakhs of rupees in savings. She glanced at it and said quietly, "The bank prints those numbers to make me feel better." He had shown her the passbook every evening for a week. Each time the evidence changed nothing, except to widen the circle of the kind deceivers: first the bank, then her son, and by the end of the interview the doctor too.

Geeta's belief is a delusion, and Section 2.4 has already shown that the form of thinking can be disordered while its content is ordinary. This section turns to content: what a person thinks, and the ways in which belief itself goes wrong. It starts with the delusion, the most important disorder of content and one of the defining marks of psychosis: what makes a belief delusional, how delusions arise, how they are described and what their themes point to. It then turns to the neighbours with which delusions are confused (the overvalued idea, the obsession, the phobia, the preoccupation and magical thinking) and ends with a table that sets them side by side.

What makes a belief a delusion

The British definition that Indian teaching follows is a sentence in which every clause does work. A delusion is a false belief, firmly held, not amenable to reason or to contrary evidence, and out of keeping with the person's educational, cultural and social background. DSM-5-TR compresses it into one line: delusions are "fixed beliefs that are not amenable to change in light of conflicting evidence". Take the clauses one at a time. "Firmly held" separates a delusion from a suspicion, a hunch or a passing fear: the person is not wondering, he knows. "Not amenable to reason or contrary evidence" is the clinical test, and Geeta's passbook shows it at work: the evidence did not dent the belief, it was absorbed into it. "Out of keeping with the person's background" excludes beliefs that the person's family, community or religion share, however strange they seem to an outsider. A delusion is idiosyncratic: it belongs to the person, not to his group. And though no definition says so, most delusions are about the person himself. They concern his safety, his worth, his body, his relationships or his destiny, which is one reason they are held with such feeling.

Karl Jaspers gave the classic external marks of a delusion in his General Psychopathology of 1913: an extraordinary subjective certainty, an imperviousness to experience and to compelling counter-argument (incorrigibility), and an impossibility or falsity of content. Book 2, Section 2.5.4 and Section 4.2.2 set them out, and the opening of this section has just shown the first two. Barlow and Durand quote his description of delusion as "the basic characteristic of madness".

Jaspers' deeper point is the one examiners and interviewers like, because it overturns the obvious answer. Falsity, he argued, is the weakest of the three marks. Consider a cloth merchant in Surat who became convinced that his business partner was stealing from the firm because the partner's new car carried a number plate whose digits added up to thirteen, and because the partner had worn a black shirt on a new-moon day. A year later an audit showed that the partner had in fact been taking money. The merchant's belief turned out to be true, yet it was still a delusion, because of the way he reached it (from number plates and shirts) and the way he held it (with total certainty from the first moment, untouched by the absence of any real evidence at the time). The reverse holds too: a false belief is not necessarily a delusion. A student who believes, from a misprinted notice, that the examination is on Friday holds a false belief, but it gives way at once to the correct notice. What makes a belief delusional is less what is believed than how it arose and how it is held, which is the form-and-content principle of Section 2.1 applied to belief.

The definition has hard edges, and a clinician meets them every week. Some beliefs cannot be checked at all: that God speaks to one in prayer, that one was a king in a former birth, that a dead father watches over the family. These are judged not by their truth but by whether the person's group holds them, how they are held and what else is present. Religious and political convictions are held with great certainty by healthy people, but they are shared, learned and open at least to discussion within the group. DSM-5-TR concedes that the line between a delusion and a strongly held idea is "sometimes difficult to determine" and depends partly on the degree of conviction in the face of clear or reasonable contradictory evidence. Conviction can also change over time. At the beginning of an illness, and again during recovery, a delusion may be held with fluctuating conviction ("Sometimes I think it really happened; sometimes I think it was the illness"); this is called a partial delusion, and its appearance during treatment is usually a good sign. Conviction and action can come apart as well: Bleuler's patients who called themselves emperors went on with their ordinary ward work, the "double bookkeeping" that Section 4.2.2 describes, a cousin of the double orientation of Section 2.2. And knowledge can come apart from belief. DSM-5-TR observes that people with delusional disorder may be able to describe, as a fact, that others regard their beliefs as irrational, while being unable to accept it themselves: in its phrase, there may be "factual insight" but no true insight (Section 2.10). Finally, a delusion is not a lie, since the person is not trying to deceive, and it is not a perceptual error, since the senses may be working perfectly.

Delusions are one of the marks of psychosis, and this chapter uses that word in a single sense. Psychosis is a loss of contact with reality, or more precisely a gross impairment of reality testing, the ability to tell what goes on in one's own mind from what goes on in the world. It shows itself in delusions, in hallucinations that the person takes to be real, and in grossly disorganised speech or behaviour. DSM-5-TR defines the psychotic disorders by abnormalities in five domains: delusions, hallucinations, disorganised thinking (speech), grossly disorganised or abnormal motor behaviour (including catatonia) and negative symptoms. Psychosis is not a measure of severity: a person with severe depression or crippling panic can be desperately ill without being psychotic, and a person with one encapsulated delusion can be psychotic while holding down a job. The old division between the psychoses and the neuroses, in which preserved insight marked the neuroses, is discussed in Section 6.1.3.

Exam trap

A false belief that cannot be shaken by reason is a delusion, a disorder of thought content. An illusion is a misperception of a real stimulus and a hallucination is a perception without a stimulus: both are disorders of perception (Section 2.3). A false memory is an error of memory (Section 2.7; Book 1, Section 7.9). "Loss of contact with reality" defines psychosis, not anhedonia (Section 2.8). Falsity is the weakest of Jaspers' three marks; a true belief can still be delusional.

How delusions begin: the primary delusions

Jaspers' most influential contribution to the study of delusions was a question about origins. Some delusions can be understood as growing out of something else in the person's mental life: a mood, a hallucination, an earlier belief. Others cannot. A primary delusion arises directly, as a new and immediate experience of meaning that cannot be traced, by any psychological route the listener can follow, to anything that came before. Jaspers called the underlying event a primary delusional experience, and he took its ununderstandability as a sign that something new had broken into the person's life (Section 2.1 explains his distinction between understanding and explaining). British texts often call primary delusions autochthonous, from the Greek for "sprung from the soil itself", meaning that they arise from nowhere outside themselves; the same word is also used more narrowly for the sudden delusional idea described below. Section 4.2.2 follows the usual sequence of these experiences in a first episode of schizophrenia and teaches Klaus Conrad's phase model; what follows here is the phenomenology of each form.

Delusional mood (in German Wahnstimmung, also called delusional atmosphere) comes first in many patients, and it is not yet a belief at all. The person feels that the world has changed in some subtle, uncanny and usually ominous way, that something is going on and that it concerns him, but he cannot say what. For two weeks before Venkat "knew", he told his brother that the factory "felt wrong". The hooter sounded different. The tea glasses in the canteen seemed to have been set out in a particular order. The trucks in the yard were parked "as if for a reason". "Something is being prepared," he said, "and it is about me, but nobody will tell me what." Delusional mood is usually anxious and perplexed, sometimes strangely exalted, and patients often find it unbearable. When a definite delusion finally crystallises out of it, the relief can be visible, because at last the strangeness has an explanation.

Delusional perception (Wahnwahrnehmung) is the form that Kurt Schneider placed among his first-rank symptoms, and its structure is precise. Schneider described it as two-membered. The first member is a real perception, correctly perceived: nothing is wrong with the senses. The second is a delusional meaning attached to that perception, usually immediate, momentous and personal, and joined to it by no link that the listener can follow. Venkat's delusion arrived this way. "The foreman put his helmet down on the left side of the table, and I knew that I had been chosen to be killed on Friday." The helmet was there; he saw it accurately; the meaning came at once and with complete certainty; and nothing about a helmet on a table leads, by any ordinary reasoning, to a death sentence. Notice what delusional perception is not. It is not an illusion (the helmet was not misperceived) and not a hallucination (the helmet was real). Nor is it a delusional interpretation of a hallucination, in which a voice that says "Friday" is explained as a threat, because that is a secondary delusion (Section 2.5.3).

The sudden delusional idea (Wahneinfall, also called delusional intuition or delusional notion) arrives fully formed in the mind, like an inspiration, without any perception to trigger it. A second-year engineering student in Warangal was brushing his teeth one morning when he suddenly knew, beyond any doubt, that he was the grandson of a famous freedom fighter, hidden at birth and destined to lead the country. Schneider counted this form among the second-rank symptoms. In delusional memory (Wahnerinnerung) the delusion attaches to the past. Either a real memory is suddenly invested with a new delusional significance ("Now I understand why the nurse in the hospital where I was born wrote my name in green ink: I was registered for the experiment even then"), or the person recalls, with delusional content already built in, an event that never happened. The second kind must be told apart from confabulation (Section 2.7): confabulation fills the gaps of an amnesia, while delusional memory occurs with memory otherwise intact, as part of a psychosis.

Why is delusional perception first-rank while delusional mood is not? The reason usually given lies in what Schneider was looking for: experiences that could be identified reliably at a single interview and that pointed strongly towards schizophrenia. Delusional perception has a structure that can be checked (a real percept, a sudden meaning, no understandable bridge). Delusional mood has no content yet; it is a diffuse state of unease and perplexity, and a similar uncanny feeling can occur in severe anxiety, in intoxication, in epileptic auras and at the start of a delirium, so it can neither be recognised reliably nor tied to one illness. The sudden delusional idea has a related problem: healthy people also have sudden convictions, and without the two-membered structure it is harder to be sure what has happened.

One more distinction catches many students: delusional perception against the delusion of reference. They overlap, because many delusional perceptions are self-referential, but they are defined in different ways. A delusion of reference is defined by its content: the belief that events, remarks, objects or broadcasts refer to oneself (Section 2.5.5). It is often understandable as an outgrowth of an existing fear. A man who already believes that his workmates despise him sees them laughing together and is sure that they are laughing at him. Delusional perception is defined by its form: a particular percept is followed by an abrupt meaning that has no understandable connection with it, whether or not that meaning is about the person. "They laughed because they know my secret" is a delusion of reference; "When the foreman put down his helmet, I knew I would die on Friday" is a delusional perception. When a question asks which is first-rank, the answer is delusional perception; delusions of reference are not.

Exam trap

The four forms of primary (autochthonous) delusion are delusional mood (Wahnstimmung: something is going on, but what?), delusional perception (Wahnwahrnehmung: a real percept, then an unconnected delusional meaning; two-membered; first-rank), the sudden delusional idea (Wahneinfall: out of the blue, no percept; second-rank) and delusional memory (Wahnerinnerung). Delusional mood is not first-rank. Delusional perception is defined by form; a delusion of reference is defined by content. A primary delusion is Jaspers' concept (not Bleuler's or Schneider's), and the phases of trema and apophany are Conrad's.

Secondary delusions, and the two roads to a fixed false belief

A secondary delusion is one that can be understood as arising from another psychological experience. Jaspers called such beliefs delusion-like ideas, because the listener can follow how the person came to hold them, even though the belief is just as fixed as a primary delusion and must be treated just as seriously. Understandability is about origins, not about strength. The common sources are worth knowing, because each points to a different disorder.

  • A depressed mood produces beliefs that fit it: guilt and sin, poverty and ruin, illness and bodily decay, and at the extreme nihilism. Geeta's certainty that she has ruined the family grows out of a mood in which she already feels worthless and to blame.
  • An elevated mood produces beliefs of power, wealth, talent and mission. Hari, swept along by his elation, announced that God had given him a plan to end unemployment in Andhra Pradesh within forty days.
  • Hallucinations demand an explanation. A woman who hears threatening voices through the wall every night concludes that a gang is planning to kill her; a man with burning sensations in his abdomen decides that he is being operated on while he sleeps.
  • Passivity experiences are explained in the same way: the patient who feels his arm moved from outside infers a machine, a spirit or a satellite, and the explanation is a delusion of control (Section 2.4.7).
  • Other delusions breed new ones. Once a man is sure that his neighbour is poisoning him, the police who refuse to arrest the neighbour must be bribed, and the doctor who finds no poison must be in the plot. This elaboration is how a delusion becomes a system.
  • Cognitive impairment leaves gaps that suspicion fills. An 80-year-old woman with early dementia mislays her gold bangles and concludes that the maid has stolen them; the next week it is the daughter-in-law.
  • Sensory loss and isolation do the same in later life. DSM-5-TR notes that pre-existing cognitive impairment and vision and hearing impairments may increase the risk of psychosis in older people, possibly by lowering the threshold for it. A partially deaf widow living alone hears the muffled voices of her neighbours through the wall and decides that they are plotting to take her flat.
  • A real event can be magnified beyond all proportion: a man passed over for promotion comes to believe that the entire office is conspiring against him, which shades into the overvalued idea of Section 2.5.6.

Figure 2.7 draws the two roads. Primary delusions are especially characteristic of schizophrenia; the delusions of mood disorders are usually secondary and fit the mood; and the delusions of organic states are usually secondary to confusion, memory loss or sensory loss. But the roads are tendencies, not laws. Many delusions in schizophrenia are secondary to hallucinations or to earlier delusions, and a patient can travel both roads at once.

Why do delusions form at all? The full cognitive and biological accounts belong to Section 4.9.2 (and to Section 4.8 for dopamine), but two ideas are worth a paragraph here. Barlow and Durand, following McKay, Langdon and Coltheart (2007), sort the theories into two families. Motivational theories see a delusion as serving a purpose: a story that relieves anxiety or protects self-esteem, as when erotomania supplies love to a lonely life or persecution explains failure without blaming oneself. Deficit theories see the delusion as the product of a fault in perception, reasoning or brain function. Among the faults, the best studied is a reasoning style. In 1988 Huq, Garety and Hemsley gave people a probability task now called the beads task, in which the participant draws beads one at a time from a jar and must decide which of two jars, with opposite proportions of coloured beads, they come from. People with delusions asked for fewer beads before deciding and were more certain of their decision, a pattern now called the jumping-to-conclusions bias. The two-factor theory, which explains the misidentification syndromes by a strange experience plus a failure to reject the belief it suggests, is taught with those syndromes in Section 2.6.

Exam trap

A primary delusion cannot be traced to any other experience; a secondary delusion (Jaspers' delusion-like idea) can be understood from a mood, a hallucination, a passivity experience, another delusion or an impairment. "Secondary" does not mean "less firmly held" or "less serious". Jaspers' primary and secondary are not Bleuler's fundamental and accessory symptoms (Section 2.1). Delusions in depression and mania are usually secondary and mood-congruent; primary delusions are especially typical of schizophrenia. The jumping-to-conclusions bias comes from the beads task of Huq, Garety and Hemsley (1988).

Describing a delusion

Naming a delusion's theme is only the start of describing it. Several further descriptions are recorded, and each tells the clinician something different.

The first is organisation. A systematised delusion is a single, logically elaborated belief, or a connected set of beliefs, built around one theme, which the person can defend point by point and from which everything else follows. Unsystematised or fragmentary delusions are multiple, loosely knitted and sometimes contradictory. Systematisation is more typical of delusional disorder and fragmentation of schizophrenia, and DSM-5-TR uses the difference in its differential diagnosis: delusions in schizophrenia show greater disorganisation, while those of delusional disorder show greater conviction, greater extension into the various areas of the person's life and greater pressure (preoccupation and concern). A related term, the encapsulated delusion, describes a delusion that is walled off from the rest of the person's life, so that personality, work and conversation on other subjects remain intact; DSM-5-TR remarks on "the apparent normality" of behaviour and appearance in delusional disorder when the delusional ideas are not being discussed or acted on (Section 4.5.4).

The second is fit with mood. A mood-congruent delusion has content consistent with the prevailing mood. DSM-5-TR lists the typical depressive themes as personal inadequacy, guilt, disease, death, nihilism or deserved punishment, and the typical manic themes as grandiosity and invulnerability, which may include suspiciousness about other people's doubts of the person's abilities. A mood-incongruent delusion does not fit: a severely depressed woman who believes that her thoughts are being broadcast through the ceiling fan, or a manic man who believes that his neighbours are poisoning him for no reason connected with his greatness. The distinction enters the psychotic features specifier of the mood disorders (Section 5.3.9).

The third is plausibility. A bizarre delusion, in DSM-5-TR's words, is clearly implausible, not understandable to people of the same culture and not derived from ordinary life experience; its example is the belief that an outside force has removed one's internal organs and replaced them with someone else's without leaving a wound or scar. A non-bizarre delusion could happen, at least in principle, as with the belief that one is under police surveillance without convincing evidence. Delusions that express a loss of control over mind or body (thought withdrawal, thought insertion and delusions of control) are generally counted as bizarre. Two cautions follow. "Bizarre" is judged against the person's own culture, so the same belief can be bizarre in one setting and ordinary in another. And bizarre does not mean dangerous: some of the most dangerous delusions, of jealousy or persecution, are entirely plausible. DSM-IV allowed a single bizarre delusion to satisfy the first criterion for schizophrenia, and DSM-5 removed that special weight (Section 4.4.2); DSM-5-TR now offers "with bizarre content" as a specifier for delusional disorder. Delusions may also be shared, passing from one person to a close companion; folie à deux is in the eponym table of Section 2.6 and in Section 4.5.5.

The last description is the one that matters most for safety: what the delusion makes the person do. Delusions vary in conviction, in how much of the day they occupy (preoccupation, or pressure), in how far into life they extend, and above all in action. A man convinced that his food is poisoned stops eating at home; a woman sure that her medicines are part of the plot stops taking them; a persecuted man may move house again and again, complain to the police, file cases or, as DSM-5-TR notes of delusional disorder, send hundreds of letters of protest to the government. Some act against the supposed persecutor or rival. DSM-5-TR observes that anger and violent behaviour can occur with the persecutory, jealous and erotomanic types. A delusion that has begun to drive behaviour is a different clinical problem from one that is merely held.

Clinical link

Never argue with a delusion: it hardens the belief and costs the relationship. Ask for the belief in the person's words, how he came to know it, how sure he is, and, most important, what he has done or plans to do about it. "If the neighbour really is poisoning your tank, what will you do?" is a risk question, not a challenge. Persecutory, jealous and erotomanic delusions that name a particular person need a specific assessment of that person's safety, which Book 2, Section 2.7.5 teaches.

Exam trap

Systematised delusions (one logical system) are typical of delusional disorder; multiple, fragmentary ones of schizophrenia. Mood-congruent depressive themes are inadequacy, guilt, disease, death, nihilism and deserved punishment; manic themes are grandiosity and invulnerability. Bizarre means implausible, not understandable to same-culture peers and not drawn from ordinary life; it is not a measure of danger, and loss-of-control delusions count as bizarre. DSM-5 removed the rule that one bizarre delusion was enough for criterion A of schizophrenia.

Delusions by theme

Delusions are classified by what they are about, and the theme is one of the best single clues to the disorder behind them. Table 2.13 lists the themes with the disorders each points to; the eponymous syndromes of jealousy, love, infestation and misidentification are taught in full in Section 2.6, and the themes as they appear in schizophrenia are in Section 4.2.2, Table 4.5.

Table 2.13 The delusional themes, what the person says, the disorders each points to, and where each is taught.

Reference"The commentator said 'a long innings ahead' to warn me."Schizophrenia; ideas of reference in social anxiety and schizotypal traitsNot tied to a mood2.5.5
Grandiose"God gave me the plan to end unemployment in forty days."Mania; also schizophrenia, delusional disorderCongruent in mania2.5.5; 5.4.1

Control (passivity)"A spirit in the peepal tree moves my legs."Schizophrenia (first-rank)Not tied to a mood; bizarre2.4.7

Persecutory delusions come first because they are the commonest of all. DSM-5-TR defines them as the belief that one is going to be harmed, harassed and so on by an individual, an organisation or another group, and it states plainly that they are the most common delusions. Its description of the persecutory type of delusional disorder spells out the range: the person believes that he is being conspired against, cheated, spied on, followed, poisoned or drugged, maliciously maligned, harassed or obstructed in the pursuit of long-term goals. The persecutor may be a neighbour, a relative, a caste or political group, the police, the government, a company or an unseen force, and the method borrows from the times: poison in the water tank, black magic, mobile towers, hacked phones. Persecutory delusions are found above all in schizophrenia, where they are the most frequent delusion, and in delusional disorder, whose commonest subtype is persecutory. They also occur in mania, in psychotic depression (where the persecution is often felt to be deserved: "They are coming to punish me, and I deserve it"), in delirium and dementia, and in intoxication with stimulants, where Barlow and Durand note that cocaine-induced paranoia is common.

Two old words travel with persecution, and examiners use both. "Paranoia" comes from the Greek para (beside) and nous (mind), and in ancient Greek it meant madness in general; it appears in the Greek tragedians and in Plato and Hippocrates in roughly that sense. Physicians revived the word in the eighteenth century, and Emil Kraepelin (1856 to 1926) gave its narrower modern meaning the classic description: a chronic, systematised delusional state that develops slowly, usually later in life, without hallucinations and without the deterioration of personality seen in dementia praecox. Today that condition is called delusional disorder (Section 4.5.4). In modern clinical English, "paranoid" means persecutory or self-referential: paranoid ideation is suspiciousness and the sense of being persecuted or watched, held short of delusional certainty. Paranoid personality disorder, an enduring pattern of distrust and suspicion without fixed delusions, is taught in Chapter 11, and the paranoid subtype of schizophrenia, which DSM-5 abandoned, in Section 4.4.3.

Exam trap

A person who believes that others are "out to get" him and that he is being attacked by rays has a persecutory delusion, the hallmark of paranoia and paranoid states; it is not a symptom of depression, dissociative disorder or anxiety disorder as such. Being "deliberately interfered with, discriminated against, plotted against, threatened or otherwise mistreated" is persecution, not reference (special meaning in events), control (outside force directing one's acts) or grandiosity. Persecutory delusions are the most common delusions, and they are most commonly seen in schizophrenia; they also occur in bipolar disorder, so read "most commonly" as the deciding word. They are not features of OCD or panic disorder.

A delusion of reference is the belief that events, objects, gestures, remarks or broadcasts that have nothing to do with the person are directed at him; DSM-5-TR calls these referential delusions and counts them among the common ones. A man watching a cricket match hears the commentator say "a long innings ahead" and knows it is a warning to him; a woman is sure that the songs on the radio are chosen to comment on her life. The same kind of thought held without delusional conviction is an idea of reference, which DSM-5-TR describes, in its account of schizotypal personality disorder, as an incorrect interpretation of casual incidents and external events as having a particular and unusual meaning specifically for the person, and distinguishes from the delusion of reference held with full conviction. Ideas of reference are common in social anxiety, in schizotypal and paranoid personality (Chapter 11) and in ordinary self-consciousness: the student who walks into a silent classroom and wonders, for a moment, whether everyone was talking about him, and then accepts that they probably were not.

A grandiose delusion, in DSM-5-TR's words, is the belief that one has exceptional abilities, wealth or fame, and the grandiose type of delusional disorder adds the conviction of having some great but unrecognised talent or insight or of having made an important discovery. Grandiose delusions are the classic mood-congruent delusions of mania; they also occur in schizophrenia and delusional disorder, and historically they were the hallmark of general paresis, the brain syphilis of Book 2, Section 1.3.6. Closely related are religious delusions, in which the content concerns God, gods, saints, sin or a divine mission. Here the clinician must hold two cautions at once. Religious belief is part of ordinary life for most Indians and is not a symptom; yet DSM-5-TR warns that elevated religiosity can itself be a feature of many presentations of psychosis. The question, as always, is form: whether the belief is shared and held in the ordinary way, or is personal, unshakeable and new.

Three themes belong to depression. In delusions of guilt and sin the person believes that he has committed a terrible wrong, or that his past failings, often trivial, have caused real disasters, and that he deserves punishment. In delusions of poverty or ruin, like Geeta's, he believes that he and his family are destitute or about to be. And in the nihilistic delusion he believes that he, his body, his mind or the world does not exist, is dead or is decaying: his intestines have rotted, his blood has dried up, his brain is gone, the world has ended. Nihilistic delusions are most typical of severe psychotic depression, especially in older people, where they are mood-congruent; they can also occur in schizophrenia and in organic states. Their most extreme form, in which the person believes that he is dead, is Cotard syndrome, taught in Section 2.6. One wording trap deserves a sentence of its own: DSM-5-TR's own definition, in its chapter on the psychotic disorders, says that nihilistic delusions "involve the conviction that a major catastrophe will occur", a meaning wider than the British one, so read the stem of a question carefully.

Exam trap

Nihilistic delusions (oneself, one's organs or the world do not exist, are dead or are decaying) are commonly seen in depression, above all severe psychotic depression in older people. They are not features of obsessive-compulsive disorder (obsessions are resisted, not believed) or of somatoform disorder (bodily preoccupation without delusional conviction). They can occur in schizophrenia, but depression is the keyed and the best answer. DSM-5-TR defines them differently, as "the conviction that a major catastrophe will occur". Cotard syndrome (the belief that one is dead) is the extreme form (Section 2.6).

Hypochondriacal or somatic delusions are, in DSM-5-TR's words, focused on "preoccupations regarding health and organ function", and the somatic type of delusional disorder covers any central delusion about bodily functions or sensations. In psychotic depression they take mood-congruent forms ("My bowels are blocked with cement; nothing has passed for a month"); in schizophrenia they are often bizarre ("My kidneys have been changed for a goat's"); in delusional disorder they are usually plausible (an infestation, a foul smell, a misshapen feature). They must be kept apart from the health anxiety of illness anxiety disorder (Section 7.7.3), where the fear of disease can be reasoned with at least for a while, and from the conviction of body dysmorphic disorder, which DSM-5-TR grades by insight and diagnoses as body dysmorphic disorder "with absent insight/delusional beliefs" rather than as delusional disorder when conviction is complete (Section 6.6.2). Four further themes have eponyms and are taught in Section 2.6: delusional infestation (Ekbom syndrome), delusional jealousy (Othello syndrome), erotomania (de Clérambault syndrome) and the misidentification delusions (Capgras and Fregoli). Delusions of control, the explanations patients give for the passivity experiences, were taught in Section 2.4.7.

Every one of these themes must finally be judged against culture. DSM-5-TR warns that some religious and supernatural beliefs, such as the evil eye, illness caused by curses and the influence of spirits, may look bizarre and possibly delusional in one cultural setting and be generally accepted in another, and that people who have survived torture, political violence or discrimination may report fears that sound persecutory but are realistic fears of recurrence or post-traumatic symptoms. Book 2, Section 2.5.4 and Section 4.2.2 each give an Indian-context box on black magic, the evil eye and spirits; their shared rule is to ask whether the family and community hold the same belief in the same way, and whether the patient's conviction has gone beyond theirs in certainty, elaboration and personal reference. What a culture supplies is the vocabulary of a delusion; what makes it a delusion is still its form.

Overvalued ideas

Not every abnormal belief is a delusion, and the most important intermediate category has a German name. The overvalued idea (überwertige Idee) was introduced by Carl Wernicke (1848 to 1905), the German neuropsychiatrist whose name is also on a language area of the brain and on an encephalopathy of thiamine deficiency. He first described it in 1892, in a forensic context, and gave it a fuller account in his Grundriss der Psychiatrie (1900), the date many textbooks cite. An overvalued idea is a solitary, abnormal but understandable belief, charged with intense feeling, that comes to dominate the person's life and actions to a morbid degree. Four features separate it from a delusion. It is understandable in the light of the person's personality, history and values, often as an exaggeration of a value that others share. The person identifies with it and defends it, rather than experiencing it as an alien intrusion. It is held strongly but not with full delusional fixity, so that it can be argued about, even if not easily changed. And it dominates: the idea organises the person's time, choices and relationships far beyond what its truth or importance would justify. In the terms of Section 2.1 it is ego-syntonic.

The examples show how varied the category is. A 17-year-old schoolgirl in Bengaluru with anorexia nervosa says: "I know the doctor says I am underweight. But one chapati makes my stomach stick out, and being thin matters more to me than my board exams." The dread of fatness is the overvalued idea of anorexia nervosa (Chapter 8). A young man with body dysmorphic disorder is certain that his nose is crooked and that everyone notices it, though he will concede, when pressed, that the mirror may exaggerate (Section 6.6.2). A retired clerk has spent twenty years fighting a pension dispute through every court and has written to the Prime Minister eleven times; his grievance began with a real injustice, but it has consumed his marriage and his savings. Morbid jealousy that stops short of delusion (Section 2.6), hypochondriacal convictions and single-minded political or religious causes complete the list. In 1984 the British psychiatrist P. J. McKenna reviewed the disorders in which an overvalued idea is the central symptom. He argued that they are well documented but variably recognised, that they share a distinctive phenomenology and develop in similar settings, and that they carry a uniformly poor prognosis.

Where does the overvalued idea end and the delusion begin? There is no sharp line. DSM-5-TR's insight specifier for body dysmorphic disorder and obsessive-compulsive disorder (good or fair insight, poor insight, absent insight or delusional beliefs) is in effect a ladder across it, and DSM-5-TR also notes that in some cultural groups distress may take the form of overvalued ideas that can look like psychosis but are normative for that group. The practical guide is the one Wernicke gave the idea: an overvalued idea is understandable and not fully fixed; a delusion is neither.

Exam trap

The overvalued idea is Wernicke's, not Jaspers' or Schneider's. It is a solitary, understandable, emotionally charged belief that dominates the person's life, held strongly but less fixedly than a delusion, and defended rather than resisted (unlike an obsession). The anorexic dread of fatness, the body dysmorphic defect and querulous litigation are the classic examples. Morbid jealousy can be delusional, overvalued or obsessional (Section 2.6).

Obsessions, phobias, preoccupations and magical thinking

The remaining disorders of content are the ones the person does not believe in the way he believes a delusion, or does not believe at all. The obsession is the most important, and it was met in Section 2.4.7 through Padma's blasphemous thought during her puja. Its classical definition has four parts: an obsession is a recurrent, persistent thought, image, impulse or doubt; the person recognises it as a product of his own mind; he experiences it as intrusive and usually as senseless, excessive or repugnant; and he tries to resist, suppress or neutralise it. DSM-5-TR keeps the core, defining obsessions as recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges or images experienced as intrusive and unwanted, which the person tries to ignore, suppress or neutralise with another thought or action, usually a compulsion. Notice what it left out. DSM-IV had also required that the person recognise the obsessions as a product of his own mind (not imposed from without, as in thought insertion); DSM-5 dropped that clause, and DSM-5-TR's criteria do not contain it. The distinction from thought insertion has not become any less important at the bedside (Section 2.4.7), and ICD-10 still lists recognition of the thoughts as one's own among its tests (Section 6.5.2). Obsessions take several forms: obsessional thoughts and images ("a picture of my baby falling from the balcony"), impulses ("I might shout an obscenity in the temple"), doubts ("Did I switch off the gas?"), fears and obsessional ruminations, prolonged, inconclusive deliberations on questions that cannot be answered ("Why is the sky above and not below?"). Rumination in the looser sense, the brooding of depression over past failures and losses, is different: it is not resisted, it is tied to the low mood and it does not feel senseless to the person. The obsession is ego-dystonic (Section 2.1), and that is precisely what separates it from the delusion and the overvalued idea, which are accepted. What happens when conviction does creep in is captured by DSM-5-TR's rule: if a person with obsessive-compulsive disorder becomes completely convinced that his obsessional beliefs are true, the diagnosis remains obsessive-compulsive disorder, with absent insight or delusional beliefs, rather than delusional disorder. The disorder itself, its insight specifier and its look-alikes are in Sections 6.5.2, 6.5.5 and 6.5.8; compulsions are described with the motor signs in Section 2.9.

A phobia, as a symptom, is a persistent, excessive and irrational fear of a specific object, activity or situation, out of proportion to any real danger, that leads to avoidance or to endurance with intense distress; adults usually recognise that the fear is excessive. It is recorded under content because what a person fears and avoids is part of what occupies his mind. "I know the dog next door is chained, but I take the long road round to the bus stop every day." What makes a fear a phobia, and the names of the phobias, are in Sections 6.3.1 and 6.3.5.

The rest of thought content is recorded as preoccupations, the themes to which a person's thinking keeps returning without the intrusiveness of an obsession or the fixity of a delusion. In depression they are the depressive cognitions of worthlessness, hopelessness, helplessness and guilt (Chapter 5). Other common preoccupations are hypochondriacal (with health and illness), religious, philosophical (with questions about the meaning of life, seen in healthy people as well as in mild depression, obsessive-compulsive disorder and schizotypal personality) and with the event that precipitated the illness, as in a reactive depression after a business failure. Every examination also records thoughts of death, and here the wording matters. A passive death wish ("I wish I would not wake up") is not the same as suicidal ideation (thoughts of ending one's own life), which in turn is not a plan or an intent; how each is asked about and weighed is in Book 2, Section 2.7, and suicide itself in Section 5.8.

Finally, magical thinking is the belief that one's thoughts, words or actions can cause or prevent events in ways that defy ordinary cause and effect. It is normal in young children, and practices that a whole culture shares, such as hanging a lemon and green chillies at a shop door against the evil eye, are not magical thinking in the clinical sense. It is common in obsessive-compulsive disorder, where a ritual is felt to prevent a disaster, and DSM-5-TR lists odd beliefs or magical thinking that influence behaviour and are inconsistent with subcultural norms among the criteria for schizotypal personality disorder, beside ideas of reference (Book 2, Section 2.5.5 gives the clinical recording; Chapter 11 the disorder).

Table 2.14 sets the delusion, the overvalued idea and the obsession side by side, with a fourth column for the normal strong belief that every one of them has to be distinguished from. Figure 2.8 shows the same comparison as a line of rising conviction, with the obsession drawn off the line because it moves on a different scale altogether.

Exam trap

An overvalued idea is understandable and not fully fixed (Wernicke). An obsession is recognised as one's own, felt as senseless and resisted. A delusion is neither understandable in the primary case nor resisted, and it is held as a plain fact. Ego-syntonic and ego-dystonic are defined in Section 2.1. Depressive rumination is not an obsession: it is not resisted and it follows the mood. Magical thinking and ideas of reference are criteria of schizotypal personality disorder, and a practice shared by the whole culture is not magical thinking in the clinical sense. A phobia is excessive fear with avoidance, usually recognised as excessive by adults.
