2.6 The Named Syndromes: Misidentification, Cotard, Othello and the Other EponymsHigh yield · 12 Qs

Psychiatry inherited from the great nineteenth-century clinics of Paris a habit of putting a person's name on any picture striking enough to be remembered. A woman who insists that her husband is an impostor, a man who says that he has been dead for a month, a husband who searches his wife's clothes every night for proof of an affair, an expectant father who is sick every morning of his wife's pregnancy: each of these has been given an eponym, and the eponyms have become some of the most reliable marks in any psychopathology paper. Why should you learn them as more than a list? There are three good reasons. Each name is a precise shorthand: "Capgras" tells a colleague in one word what would otherwise take a paragraph. Each comes with a history that explains what the first observers thought was going wrong, and in several cases that history turned out to be a clue to the brain. And, most important for answering questions correctly, almost none of them is a diagnosis.

Look in DSM-5-TR or ICD-11 for "Capgras syndrome" or "Cotard syndrome" and you will not find a disorder with criteria. These are descriptive syndromes: recognisable clusters of beliefs or experiences that turn up inside many different disorders, among them schizophrenia, delusional disorder, psychotic depression, mania, dementia and brain injury. The practical consequence is a two-step habit. When a stem describes a patient, first name the syndrome (an impostor belief is Capgras), then ask which disorder it is sitting in (a woman with Parkinson's disease and visual hallucinations points to a dementia; a young man with voices and thought insertion points to schizophrenia). The delusional themes in general (persecution, reference, grandiosity, guilt, nihilism, jealousy, love, infestation) were introduced in Section 2.5, and Section 4.5.4 describes the types of delusional disorder; this section is where the named syndromes are taught in full, with who named them, where they occur and how they are explained.

Capgras syndrome and the misidentification family

Start with the family to which the most famous eponym belongs. In 1981 the Greek psychiatrist George N. Christodoulou, writing with S. Malliara-Loulakaki, grouped a set of strange beliefs under one heading, and the 1986 volume he edited, The Delusional Misidentification Syndromes, made the umbrella term standard. A delusional misidentification syndrome is a delusion in which the identity of a person, a place or an object is believed to have been changed, duplicated or disguised, although the person or place is perceived essentially normally. Christodoulou counted four core members: Capgras syndrome, Fregoli syndrome, intermetamorphosis and the syndrome of subjective doubles. He noticed three things about them. They often occur in the same patient, at the same time or one after another. They share a single theme, the idea of the double (French sosie). And they divide neatly into two directions. In hypoidentification a well-known person is under-identified: the familiar is declared unfamiliar, which is Capgras. In hyperidentification unknown people are over-identified: the unfamiliar is declared familiar, which is the other three. Because each patient's delusion is usually confined to this one theme, the syndromes are also called monothematic delusions.

You met the first patient at the start of this chapter. Madame M. agreed that the men who visited her resembled her husband, offering only trifles as proof that they were not him, and that agreement was the whole puzzle. Joseph Capgras (1873 to 1950) and his intern Jean Reboul-Lachaux called what she showed l'illusion des sosies, the illusion of doubles, in 1923, and in 1929 their colleague Joseph Lévy-Valensi proposed that it be called le syndrome de Capgras. Today Capgras syndrome (or the Capgras delusion) is the delusional belief that a person well known to the patient has been replaced by an impostor who is physically identical, or nearly so. Three features define it. The replaced person is almost always someone emotionally close: a spouse above all, then a parent, a child or a sibling. The patient accepts the physical resemblance; at most she points to trivial differences (a mark, a slightly thinner face) as her proof, and there is no failure to see the face. And the conviction is held with delusional certainty, so that proofs of identity (wedding photographs, shared memories, a scar in the right place) are absorbed into the belief: the impostor has simply studied his part very well. The impostors can multiply, and the same idea can attach to a pet, to a treasured object or to the patient's own home.

Here is what the syndrome looks and sounds like. Usha, a 71-year-old retired school principal in Thrissur, has changed over the past year. Her memory and alertness swing from one day to the next, her walk has become slow and shuffling, and for some months she has seen small children playing on the terrace in the evenings, a combination that her neurologist recognises as dementia with Lewy bodies (Chapter 10). One evening she bolts her bedroom door and telephones her son in Bengaluru. "Your father has gone somewhere. This man has his face, his voice, even his spectacles with the broken arm, but he is not your father. Your father would never leave me sitting in the dark." When her husband speaks to her on the telephone from the market the next morning she is warm and relieved; when he walks in through the door an hour later he is the stranger again, and she asks him what he has done with her real husband. Notice what is intact: she sees his face accurately, she names every feature correctly, and she remembers forty-six years of marriage. What has failed is the conclusion she draws from the sight of him.

Where does Capgras syndrome occur? In psychiatric practice it is seen most often in schizophrenia and the other psychoses, especially in paranoid presentations, and also in mood disorders with psychotic features. In neurological practice it is a well-recognised feature of dementia with Lewy bodies, Alzheimer's disease and Parkinson's disease, and it follows brain injury, stroke and other lesions, typically involving the right hemisphere and the frontal lobes; it has also been reported with migraine, diabetes and hypothyroidism. It may be acute and transient, or chronic. For decades after 1923 the explanations offered were psychoanalytic: the patient was said to resolve unbearable ambivalence towards a loved person by splitting off the hated part and attributing it to an impostor. Attention turned to the brain in the late 1970s and 1980s, when clinicians noticed how often the patients had organic lesions, and Christodoulou's advice still stands: the appearance of a misidentification syndrome should make the clinician look for an organic contribution, even in a patient with an established psychosis.

The syndrome matters clinically because of what the patient may do. A person who believes that an impostor has taken over her home, and has perhaps harmed her real husband, is frightened and angry. Patients refuse to eat food the "impostor" prepares, lock him out, demand that the police find the real spouse, and occasionally attack the double; assaults and even killings have been reported, so the safety of the misidentified person is part of every assessment (Book 2, Section 2.7.5 teaches the assessment of the risk of violence). Treatment is the treatment of the underlying condition: an antipsychotic for a psychosis, attention to the delirium or dementia in an older patient, and great caution with antipsychotics in dementia with Lewy bodies, in which severe neuroleptic sensitivity is a recognised feature (Chapter 10).

Exam trap

A familiar person replaced by an identical impostor is Capgras, whatever the setting (schizophrenia, dementia or brain injury). Strangers who are really one familiar person in disguise is Fregoli. The belief that one is dead or has lost one's organs is Cotard. Delusional infidelity is Othello. Folie à deux is a shared delusion passed from one person to another (Charles Lasègue and Jules Falret, 1877; Section 4.5.5). "One is being persecuted by a secret organisation" is an ordinary persecutory delusion (Section 2.5), and "one's body has been stolen and replaced" is not a named syndrome at all but a bizarre somatic delusion (Section 2.5). In the vignette of an older man with mild cognitive impairment who says his wife of forty years is an identical "duplicate", the answer is still Capgras: the object is a person. Reduplicative paramnesia is the near miss, because it classically duplicates a place; some authors (Michael Alexander, Donald Stuss and Frank Benson, 1979) have described Capgras as a "reduplication" of a person, which is why the two are confused.

Fregoli, intermetamorphosis, subjective doubles and reduplicative paramnesia

In 1927 Paul Courbon (1879 to 1958) and Gabriel Fail published a paper whose title linked a music-hall artist to schizophrenia: "Syndrome d'illusion de Frégoli et schizophrénie". Their patient was a 27-year-old woman who believed that two actors she often saw at the theatre were persecuting her, pursuing her closely by taking on the forms of people she knew or met. Courbon and Fail named the picture after Leopoldo Fregoli (1867 to 1936), an Italian actor famous across Europe for quick-change acts in which he played many characters in a single performance, reappearing within seconds in a new costume and a new voice. Fregoli syndrome is the delusional belief that different people the patient meets are in fact one and the same person, usually someone known to the patient, who keeps changing appearance or disguise. Because the person "behind" the disguises is usually a persecutor, the syndrome is nearly always paranoid. A 30-year-old man in Lucknow is sure that the supervisor who dismissed him is following him: he is the sugarcane-juice seller at the corner, the security guard at the mall, the new clerk at the bank, each wearing a different face but betrayed by "the same eyes". Fregoli syndrome is seen in schizophrenia and other psychoses and after brain injury, and it has been reported with levodopa treatment of Parkinson's disease. It is rarer than Capgras.

The third member was described by Courbon again, this time with Jean Tusques, in 1932. In intermetamorphosis the patient believes that people around him change into one another, in their external appearance and in their personality, and he may claim to see the transformation happen. One man with Alzheimer's disease took his wife first for his dead mother and later for his sister, and his son for his brother, and the misidentification continued even on the telephone, which showed that it was not a simple failure of seeing. Where Fregoli is a disguise (the same persecutor under different costumes), intermetamorphosis is a real change of identity, of mind as well as face.

The fourth core member concerns the self. In 1978, in the American Journal of Psychiatry, Christodoulou described the syndrome of subjective doubles: the delusional belief that one has a double, or several doubles, physically identical to oneself (sometimes with a different character) and leading separate lives. Patients may hold the double responsible for things they are accused of, or fear that the double will take their place with their family. Keep this apart from the autoscopic hallucination of Section 2.3. In autoscopy a person sees an image of himself, a perceptual experience; in the syndrome of subjective doubles he believes, without necessarily seeing anything, that another copy of him exists.

The last syndrome in the group is older than the others and concerns places. In 1903 the Prague psychiatrist Arnold Pick (1851 to 1924), best known for the dementia that bears his name, published a paper in Brain called "On reduplicative paramnesia". His patient, a woman with a progressive dementia, insisted that she had been moved from his clinic in the city to another clinic that looked exactly the same but stood in a suburb she knew well; to explain the coincidence she said that Pick and his staff worked in both places. Today reduplicative paramnesia means the delusional belief that a familiar place exists in two or more locations at once, or has been moved to another site. A 58-year-old woman recovering from a right-hemisphere stroke in a large Delhi hospital agrees that it is the same hospital, with the same doctors and the same canteen, "but it has been shifted to Meerut, near my mother's lane; that is why I can hear the trains." For seventy years the condition was a curiosity. Then in 1976 D. Frank Benson, Howard Gardner and J. C. Meadows described three patients who, after severe head injuries, had recovered most of their memory but persistently relocated the hospital to another town, even in the face of compelling evidence; they drew attention to damage in the right hemisphere and the frontal lobes, the same pattern that later work found in Capgras syndrome. The condition is seen after head injury and stroke, with tumours and in dementia.

Why did Pick call it a paramnesia, a distortion of memory, rather than a delusion? Because the place is recognised: the patient knows the ward, the faces, the routine. What has gone wrong is the attachment of that familiarity to the right location, a fault in the recognition of the familiar, which is why Section 2.7 places reduplicative paramnesia on the recognition branch of the paramnesias. Modern accounts combine two deficits, much like the two-factor account you will meet below: right-hemisphere damage that disturbs spatial orientation and visual memory, and frontal damage that stops the patient from rejecting the impossible conclusion.

Two further pictures are seen mainly in dementia. In mirrored-self misidentification the patient believes that her reflection in a mirror is another person, a stranger, a younger version of herself or a relative, while still recognising other people's reflections; she may talk to the woman in the mirror, offer her tea, or complain that someone is watching her from the bathroom. It is associated with right-hemisphere dysfunction. The phantom boarder belief, that strangers are living in the house, eating the food and moving things, is common in Alzheimer's disease and dementia with Lewy bodies. Figure 2.9 draws the whole family.

Remember

Capgras: the familiar becomes a stranger (one known person, an identical impostor). Fregoli: strangers become one familiar person (usually a persecutor in disguise). Intermetamorphosis: people change into each other, body and mind. Subjective doubles: I have a double. Reduplicative paramnesia: a place is duplicated or relocated (Arnold Pick, 1903). Courbon named two of them: Fregoli with Fail in 1927, intermetamorphosis with Tusques in 1932.

Why a face can look right and feel wrong: the Ellis and Young account

Return to Usha's question, the one Capgras could not answer: if she sees her husband's face correctly and remembers him perfectly, why does she conclude that he is someone else? The answer that most researchers now accept came from an unexpected direction, the study of a neurological disorder that looks like Capgras turned inside out. In prosopagnosia, after damage to the face-processing regions of the temporal and occipital lobes, a person can see a face clearly, describe its features and tell that it is a face, but cannot recognise whose face it is, even when it belongs to a spouse or a child (the Physiological book teaches the condition itself). In 1984 the American neuropsychologist Russell M. Bauer showed a prosopagnosic patient photographs of familiar people while reading out several names for each and recording his skin conductance, the tiny change in the electrical resistance of the skin that accompanies autonomic arousal. The patient could not say who the people were. Yet his skin conductance rose more when the correct name was read than when a wrong one was. Some part of his brain knew the faces and responded to them emotionally, without his being aware of it. This is called covert recognition, and it implied that recognising a face involves at least two routes: one that delivers the conscious identification ("that is my wife") and another that delivers an automatic, emotional response to a face that matters.

In 1990 two British psychologists, Hadyn D. Ellis and Andrew W. Young, put the pieces together in a paper in the British Journal of Psychiatry called "Accounting for delusional misidentifications". They proposed that Capgras syndrome is the mirror image of prosopagnosia. In prosopagnosia the conscious route is broken and the affective route survives, so the face is not recognised but still "feels" familiar to the autonomic nervous system. In Capgras syndrome the conscious route survives and the affective route is broken, so the face is recognised as looking exactly like the husband's while the expected glow of familiarity is missing. Faced with a face that looks right and feels wrong, the patient reaches for the only explanation that fits both facts: this is someone who looks exactly like my husband, an impostor. The account also explains why the impostor is nearly always a person the patient loves. The stronger the emotional response a face normally produces, the more conspicuous its absence. The same paper explained Fregoli syndrome and intermetamorphosis as faults at other stages of the same chain of face processing, in which a sense of familiarity is wrongly attached to strangers. Figure 2.10 shows the two routes and the two opposite breaks.

Key study: the skin that did not recognise (Ellis, Young, Quayle and de Pauw, 1997)

A theory that says Capgras patients lack the autonomic response to familiar faces makes a testable prediction, and in 1997 Ellis, Young and their colleagues A. H. Quayle and K. W. de Pauw tested it in the Proceedings of the Royal Society B. They recorded skin conductance in five people with the Capgras delusion while they looked at photographs of familiar and of unfamiliar faces, and compared them with five healthy middle-aged volunteers and with five psychiatric patients taking similar antipsychotic medication. The healthy and the psychiatric controls responded more strongly to the familiar faces. The Capgras patients did not: their skin made no distinction between faces they knew and faces they had never seen. Their responses to simple tones were normal, so the deficit was not a general flattening of arousal but specific to the personal significance of faces. In the same year William Hirstein and Vilayanur S. Ramachandran described a young man, DS, who developed the delusion after a head injury. His skin conductance responses to photographs of his own parents were no larger than to strangers, and his delusion was tied to the eyes: his parents were impostors when he looked at them, but not when he spoke to them on the telephone, because the route from the voice to the emotions was intact. They proposed a disconnection between the face areas of the temporal lobe and the limbic system, particularly the amygdala. Criticism: the samples are tiny, and the same loss of autonomic response has been found in some patients with frontal damage who never developed a delusion, which is the strongest argument that the odd experience alone is not enough.

That last criticism led to the most influential refinement of the theory. If an absent feeling of familiarity were enough to create the belief, everyone with that deficit would have the delusion, and they do not. The Australian cognitive neuropsychologist Max Coltheart, with Robyn Langdon, Martin Davies, Ryan McKay and others, therefore proposed a two-factor theory of monothematic delusions, set out in a series of papers from about 2000 and summarised in a 2011 review. The first factor is a neuropsychological abnormality that produces an anomalous experience and explains the delusion's content: in Capgras, the missing autonomic response to a loved face. The second factor is an impairment in the system that evaluates beliefs, which explains why the bizarre explanation is adopted and kept instead of being rejected, as a healthy person would reject the thought "my husband has been replaced". The second factor has most often been linked to damage in the right frontal lobe, which fits the frequency of right frontal lesions in Capgras syndrome and reduplicative paramnesia. In the terms of Section 2.5, this is a deficit account rather than a motivational one: the delusion is the product of a damaged recognition system plus damaged belief checking, not a defence against unacceptable feelings, although the two kinds of explanation may each fit some patients.

Andrew Young and his colleagues went one step further in the 1990s and suggested that the same abnormal experience, a world of faces that has lost its emotional colour, may be interpreted in two opposite ways. A suspicious person who looks outwards for causes concludes that something is wrong with the others: they are impostors (Capgras). A depressed person who looks inwards concludes that something is wrong with himself: perhaps he is dead (Cotard). Whether or not that bridge holds, it leads naturally to the next syndrome.

Cotard syndrome: the walking corpse

Jules Cotard (1840 to 1889) trained in Paris and worked at the Salpêtrière with Jean-Martin Charcot before spending the last fifteen years of his life in practice at Vanves, near Paris. In 1880, in a lecture to the Société Médico-Psychologique titled "On hypochondriacal delusions in a severe form of anxious melancholia", he described a woman he called Mademoiselle X. She said that she had no brain, no nerves, no chest and no entrails, that she was nothing but skin and bones over a decomposing body. She therefore had no need to eat. She also said that she could not die a natural death and was condemned to exist for ever. Cotard thought that he had found a new and grave form of agitated melancholia, and in 1882 he gave the central symptom the name by which the French still know the syndrome, le délire des négations, the delusion of negation.

Cotard syndrome (Cotard's delusion, the "walking corpse" syndrome) is a set of nihilistic delusions centred on the patient's own body and existence: the belief that one is dead, does not exist, is rotting or putrefying, or has lost one's blood, internal organs or soul. The denial may spread outwards to the world (the family no longer exists, the city has been destroyed, there is no God) and, paradoxically, it is often combined with a delusion of immortality: having no body, the patient cannot die and will suffer for ever. Listen to how it sounds in a 66-year-old retired railway clerk in Vijayawada, admitted after three weeks of refusing food: "Why do you give me rice? There is no stomach to put it in. The pipes inside me have closed. I died in the month of Shravan; you are talking to what is left." He is agitated, wrings his hands, and asks the nurses to send him to the burning ghat. Note the logic of the delusion, which is also its danger: a person who believes himself dead sees no reason to eat, drink, take medicines or avoid harm, so starvation, dehydration and suicide are the main risks.

How common is each feature, and in which illnesses? The best-known answer is a 1995 statistical analysis by the Cambridge historian of psychiatry Germán E. Berrios and R. Luque of 100 published cases. Depression was reported in 89 per cent; the commonest nihilistic delusions concerned the body (86 per cent) and existence (69 per cent); anxiety (65 per cent), guilt (63 per cent), hypochondriacal delusions (58 per cent) and delusions of immortality (55 per cent) were also frequent; and the likelihood of the syndrome increased with age. A factor analysis divided the cases into three groups: psychotic depression with few nihilistic delusions, a "pure" Cotard type I closer to the delusional disorders than to mood disorder, and a mixed Cotard type II with anxiety, depression and auditory hallucinations. The usual setting, then, is a severe, often agitated psychotic depression in an older person (Section 5.3.9), but the syndrome also occurs in schizophrenia, in bipolar disorder (in adolescents and young adults it has been linked to a later bipolar course) and in neurological illness, including brain injury, epilepsy, stroke and dementia. Treatment follows the setting: an antidepressant combined with an antipsychotic for psychotic depression, and electroconvulsive therapy, which works quickly and is often the treatment of choice when the patient is refusing food or is at high risk of suicide (Section 5.7.7).

One distinction must be made at the bedside every time. Many people with depersonalisation say that they feel "as if I were dead", "like a robot", "not really here". That is an experience held as if, with reality testing intact: the person knows that he is alive. The Cotard patient says, without any "as if", that he is dead, and argues the point. The first is depersonalisation (Section 2.10 and Table 7.14); the second is a delusion.

Exam trap

Cotard syndrome is defined by nihilistic delusions: of being dead, not existing, rotting, or having lost one's organs or blood (Jules Cotard, 1880; délire des négations, 1882). It is not grandiose (special powers or abilities are the grandiose delusions of mania), not persecutory (being followed or monitored), not an impostor belief (that is Capgras) and not a somatic delusion of serious illness such as cancer or AIDS, even though its content concerns the body: the Cotard patient does not believe the organs are diseased but that they are gone or dead. Watch the wording of the classifications too: DSM-5-TR uses "nihilistic delusions" for the conviction that a major catastrophe will occur, so read each stem to see which meaning is being tested. The usual setting is severe psychotic depression in later life, but the syndrome is not confined to it.

Othello syndrome and the forms of morbid jealousy

In Shakespeare's play the Moorish general Othello is led by his ensign Iago to believe that his faithful wife Desdemona has betrayed him with his lieutenant Cassio, and the "evidence" is a handkerchief planted where he will find it. He smothers her. In 1955 two British psychiatrists, John Todd and Kenneth Dewhurst, borrowed his name for a paper in the Journal of Nervous and Mental Disease, "The Othello syndrome: a study in the psychopathology of sexual jealousy". Othello syndrome is now used for delusional jealousy: the fixed, false belief that one's spouse or sexual partner is being unfaithful, held without adequate cause and unshaken by evidence. Critics point out that the eponym fits poorly, because Othello was deceived by a skilled manipulator rather than deluded, and because it suggests a single condition where there is really a family.

That family is called morbid jealousy (or pathological jealousy), and the first job of the clinician is to decide which member is in front of him. In delusional jealousy the belief has the full character of a delusion: certain, ego-syntonic, not resisted, and often elaborated with care and plausibility. DSM-5-TR's description of delusional disorder, jealous type, catches the reasoning exactly: the belief is arrived at without due cause and rests on incorrect inferences from small bits of "evidence", such as disarrayed clothing, and the person usually confronts the partner and tries to intervene in the imagined infidelity. Delusional jealousy is seen in delusional disorder (Section 4.5.4), in schizophrenia, in organic states such as dementia and brain injury, and with alcohol and stimulants. In overvalued jealousy the idea is understandable and not absurd (perhaps the partner once did have an affair) but it is pursued beyond the bounds of reason and dominates the person's life, though it remains open to some argument. In obsessional jealousy the thoughts "what if she is with someone?" are recognised as excessive and senseless, resisted, ego-dystonic and shameful, and they drive checking and reassurance-seeking; DSM-5-TR lists this non-delusional preoccupation with a partner's perceived infidelity as an example of other specified obsessive-compulsive and related disorder (Section 6.6.6). Table 2.15 sets the forms side by side.

Table 2.15 The forms of morbid jealousy compared.
FormThe beliefInsight and resistanceTypical settings
Delusional (Othello syndrome)Certain that the partner is unfaithful; built on trivial "evidence"No insight; ego-syntonic; not resistedDelusional disorder, jealous type; schizophrenia; alcohol and stimulants; dementia and brain injury
OvervaluedAn understandable suspicion that dominates life beyond reasonPartly open to argument; ego-syntonic; not resistedParanoid and other personality disorders; after a real betrayal
ObsessionalIntrusive doubts ("what if...?")Recognised as excessive; ego-dystonic; resisted; checking and reassurance-seekingObsessive-compulsive and related disorders (Section 6.6.6)
Normal jealousyA response to a real threat to the relationshipProportionate; fades with reassuranceAnyone

What does morbid jealousy look like at home? Naveen, a 45-year-old dealer in tractor spare parts in Hubballi, has been drinking heavily for twelve years. For the last two he has been sure that his wife is meeting the young man who delivers milk. He checks the time she was last seen on WhatsApp against the time she says she slept; he smells her dupatta for a stranger's perfume; he marks the position of the gate latch before he leaves for the shop and phones home four times a day; he has twice come back unannounced at noon. A crumpled bedsheet, a new hair clip, the fact that she smiled at the milkman's mother at the temple, all of it is proof. When she cries and denies it, he says that her tears are the proof of her guilt, and one night, drunk, he held a kitchen knife to her throat and demanded a confession. The behaviour in this vignette is typical: searching the partner's clothes, possessions, phone and correspondence, examining bed linen and underclothes for signs of sexual activity, repeated telephone calls and surprise visits, following, and sometimes hidden recording devices.

Naveen's drinking is not a coincidence. Alcohol dependence has the best-known association with morbid jealousy: in two studies of men attending alcohol treatment services, morbid jealousy was present in 27 per cent and 34 per cent. Several mechanisms are suggested. Chronic drinking causes sexual dysfunction, and a man who fails sexually may explain the failure by the partner's infidelity; the drinking estranges the partner, who withdraws, which the drinker reads as evidence; intoxication disinhibits the expression of suspicion and aggression; and alcohol can produce a delusional psychosis of its own. Some authors think alcohol more often aggravates an existing jealousy than creates it, while others, finding that in most of their patients the jealousy began only after the alcohol dependence, give it a causal role. The treatment of the drinking is part of the treatment of the jealousy (Chapter 10).

Clinical link

Morbid jealousy is one of the most dangerous phenomena in psychiatry, and the danger falls on the partner. In a classic forensic series from a British secure hospital, Mowat in 1966 described 110 morbidly jealous people who had killed or seriously assaulted someone; in 94 the victim was the partner. Every assessment therefore includes a risk assessment for the partner (threats, weapons, previous violence, intoxication, a partner who is planning to leave), and the clinician may have to warn her, involve the police or admit the patient even without his consent when the risk is serious and immediate (Book 2, Section 2.7.5). The partner should be interviewed separately. Treatment depends on the form: an antipsychotic for delusional jealousy, an SSRI with cognitive behavioural work (Book 2) for the obsessional form, treatment of the alcohol dependence when it is present, and in refractory cases the only effective measure may be geographical separation, after which the jealousy often returns if the couple resume living together.

Exam trap

A fixed belief that the spouse or sexual partner is unfaithful, without evidence, is Othello syndrome (John Todd and Kenneth Dewhurst, 1955), the delusional form of morbid jealousy and the jealous type of delusional disorder. The distractors each name a different syndrome: Ekbom is infestation by parasites (and, separately, restless legs); de Clérambault is erotomania, the belief that someone is secretly in love with the patient; Couvade is pregnancy-like symptoms in an expectant father; Capgras (impostor) and Fregoli (disguised persecutor) are misidentifications. Remember the classic association with alcohol dependence, and that obsessional jealousy, which is resisted and ego-dystonic, is not Othello syndrome.

De Clérambault syndrome and Ekbom syndrome

If Othello syndrome is the delusion of being betrayed, de Clérambault syndrome is the delusion of being loved. It is named after Gaëtan Gatian de Clérambault (1872 to 1934), the psychiatrist in charge of the special infirmary for the insane attached to the Paris police prefecture, a clinician of unusual gifts who was also an accomplished photographer and an authority on draped costume. In 1921, in Les psychoses passionnelles, he gave the classic account of erotomania: the delusional conviction that another person, usually of higher social status and often a stranger, a public figure or a superior at work, is secretly in love with the patient. Notice the details de Clérambault insisted on. It is the other person who fell in love first and made the first advance; the love cannot be openly declared, for reasons the patient can always supply (the object's marriage, career or family); and the object communicates it by secret signals, so that ideas of reference run through the delusion. Any rejection is explained away as concealment or as a test of the patient's devotion, usually described as the object's "paradoxical conduct". His best-known case, the woman who kept a vigil outside Buckingham Palace for a sign from King George V, is told in Section 4.5.4.

De Clérambault described a typical evolution, usually summarised as three phases: a phase of hope, sometimes lasting years, in which the patient waits for and pursues the lover; a phase of spite (in French dépit), as the lover's failure to respond becomes undeniable; and a phase of rancour (rancune), in which love turns to grievance, complaint and sometimes threats. He also distinguished a pure or primary erotomania, beginning suddenly in an otherwise intact person and running a chronic course, from secondary erotomania, which appears within another illness such as schizophrenia or mania. DSM-5-TR's erotomanic type of delusional disorder carries the same core: another person, usually of higher status but sometimes a complete stranger, is in love with the individual, and efforts to contact the object of the delusion are common. Those efforts are where the clinical problems lie: letters, gifts, telephone calls, messages on social media, unannounced visits and stalking, sometimes for years, with distress to the object and legal trouble for the patient. Clinical samples have been predominantly women, while the men who come to attention tend to do so through forensic services, because they are more likely to stalk and to be violent, sometimes towards a perceived rival.

Karl-Axel Ekbom (1907 to 1977) was a Swedish neurologist who worked at the Karolinska Institute and then held the chair of neurology at Uppsala. In a paper of 1938 (he had also described cases in 1937) called "Der präsenile Dermatozoenwahn", the presenile delusion of skin parasites, he gave the classic account (the French physician Georges Thibierge had written of "acarophobia" in the 1890s) of what is now called delusional parasitosis (or delusional infestation), and his name came to be attached to it as Ekbom syndrome. It is the fixed belief that one's skin or body is infested by insects, mites, worms or other parasites, when no infestation exists. The belief is usually accompanied by crawling, biting or itching sensations (formication, Section 2.3), and the patient's attempts to get rid of the parasites, with scratching, picking, tweezers, kerosene, harsh disinfectants and repeated washing, damage the skin and create the sores that are then offered as proof. The most famous sign is the matchbox sign (also called the specimen sign): the patient brings "specimens" of the parasites, in practice flakes of skin, scabs, hairs, threads or fluff, wrapped in paper or carried in a matchbox, a pill bottle or, today, a plastic bag or a set of photographs on a phone. It is found in roughly half to four-fifths of patients.

The typical patient is a woman, socially isolated, in late middle or old age: the condition is diagnosed about twice as often in women as in men, with an average age in the late fifties. Because she believes her problem is a skin disease, she goes to dermatologists, pest-control firms and general practitioners, often for years, and refuses referral to a psychiatrist. A 63-year-old widow in Kozhikode who lives alone brings a dermatologist a folded newspaper containing fragments of skin and hair, saying that the "white worms" come out at night and that she has had the house fumigated three times. In primary delusional parasitosis the belief is the only psychiatric abnormality, and the diagnosis is delusional disorder, somatic type (Section 4.5.4). In secondary forms it arises in schizophrenia, depression or dementia, from a medical illness (vitamin B12 deficiency, hypothyroidism, diabetes, kidney or liver disease) or from drugs, above all cocaine and amphetamine, which produce the "cocaine bugs" of formication (Chapter 10). Treatment is a low-dose antipsychotic, and the key skill is engagement: many patients accept treatment only from a dermatologist who takes the skin seriously, examines the specimens and works jointly with a psychiatrist.

Exam trap

Two eponyms in this subsection carry a second meaning. Ekbom's name is also on restless legs syndrome, which he described in detail in his doctoral thesis of the mid-1940s and which is now called Willis-Ekbom disease: an urge to move the legs at rest, worse in the evening, with unpleasant creeping sensations (Chapter 8). In a delusion question, "Ekbom syndrome" means delusional parasitosis. De Clérambault also described mental automatism (the Kandinsky-Clérambault syndrome), a different set of experiences of thoughts and acts as alien and automatic; in a delusion question, de Clérambault means erotomania. And do not confuse erotomania with Othello syndrome: the first is "someone loves me", the second "my partner betrays me".

Couvade syndrome and Diogenes syndrome

The last two eponyms in the main table are not delusions at all, which is exactly why examiners use them as distractors. The word couvade comes from the French couver, to brood or hatch, and was adopted in 1865 by the British anthropologist Edward Burnett Tylor for customs, recorded in many societies from South America to Asia, in which the father takes to his bed during or after his wife's labour, imitates the pains of childbirth, fasts or observes taboos, and is treated as if he had given birth. In 1965 the British psychiatrists W. H. Trethowan and M. F. Conlon published a paper in the British Journal of Psychiatry called "The couvade syndrome", describing its modern, unritualised counterpart. In Couvade syndrome (sometimes called sympathetic pregnancy) an expectant father develops pregnancy-like symptoms during his partner's pregnancy: nausea and morning sickness, indigestion and heartburn, abdominal pain or bloating, changes in appetite, weight gain, toothache, backache and disturbed sleep. The symptoms come and go with the pregnancy and usually disappear after the baby is born. The man does not believe that he is pregnant and has no physical disease to explain his symptoms. Proposed explanations include anxiety about the pregnancy and fatherhood, identification with the partner, ambivalence and envy of her capacity to bear a child, and hormonal change: expectant fathers show rises in prolactin and falls in testosterone, and men with more symptoms show larger changes. It is not a DSM-5-TR or ICD diagnosis.

Keep it apart from its female counterpart, pseudocyesis, which DSM-5-TR lists as an example of other specified somatic symptom and related disorder: a false belief of being pregnant associated with objective signs and reported symptoms of pregnancy (the abdomen swells, periods stop). In pseudocyesis the woman believes she is pregnant; in Couvade syndrome the man knows perfectly well that his partner is.

The final syndrome begins with an image every hospital social worker in India will recognise. A retired professor, a widower of 79, is brought to a Pune emergency department after a neighbour noticed the smell from his flat and found him on the floor, too weak to stand. The flat is waist-deep in newspapers, plastic bags, empty milk packets and rotting food; there is no clean surface and no working tap. He is malnourished and filthy, and he is courteous, articulate and quite unembarrassed. He has refused every offer of help from his nephew for years and asks to be taken home. In 1975 A. N. G. Clark, G. D. Mankikar and Ian Gray described thirty such patients (fourteen men and sixteen women) admitted to hospital with acute illness and extreme self-neglect, in a Lancet paper titled "Diogenes syndrome: a clinical study of gross neglect in old age". All had dirty, untidy homes and a filthy personal appearance about which they showed no shame; some hoarded rubbish (syllogomania); all but two lived alone; poverty and bad housing were not the cause; a third had persistently refused help; and their mortality was high. Strikingly, half had no psychiatric disorder and were of higher than average intelligence, and many had led successful professional lives; their personalities tended to be aloof, suspicious, emotionally labile and aggressive. Clark and his colleagues had been anticipated in 1966 by Duncan Macmillan and Patricia Shaw, whose British Medical Journal paper spoke of "senile breakdown in standards of personal and environmental cleanliness".

Diogenes syndrome (also called senile squalor syndrome) is therefore the combination of gross self-neglect, domestic squalor, social withdrawal and refusal of help, often with hoarding, usually in older people living alone. About half the patients have a mental disorder, most often a dementia (frontotemporal dementia in particular), frontal lobe damage, alcohol misuse or a psychosis; the other half have no major disorder but a long-standing reclusive, suspicious or paranoid personality, and the syndrome has been read as a late-life reaction to stress in such a personality. The name is a well-known misnomer: the Greek philosopher Diogenes of Sinope, the Cynic who is said to have lived in a large jar in Athens, owned almost nothing, so he hoarded nothing, and he sought company every day in the market place. When hoarding occurs without self-neglect and squalor, and is not caused by a dementia, the diagnosis to consider is hoarding disorder (Section 6.6.4); DSM-5-TR notes that when accumulation follows frontotemporal degeneration or Alzheimer's disease it may come with self-neglect and severe domestic squalor and is not diagnosed as hoarding disorder. The hardest clinical question is usually ethical: whether a competent person has the right to live in squalor, which turns on the assessment of capacity (Book 2, Section 1.10.3).

Exam trap

Couvade: an expectant father develops pregnancy symptoms; not a delusion, and the man knows he is not pregnant (Trethowan and Conlon, 1965). Pseudocyesis: a woman falsely believes she is pregnant and shows signs of pregnancy. Diogenes: gross self-neglect, squalor and hoarding in the elderly living alone (Clark, Mankikar and Gray, 1975); about half have no psychiatric disorder, so do not assume dementia. Neither Couvade nor Diogenes is a misidentification syndrome or a delusion of infidelity.

The eponyms taught elsewhere, and the master table

Several other eponyms appear in the same lists in examination papers. Each belongs to a disorder taught in full elsewhere in this book, so Table 2.16 gives only the essentials and the place to look. Two deserve a sentence each because they connect to signs in this chapter. Ganser syndrome is built around approximate answers (Section 2.4), the patient who, asked how many legs a cow has, says "five", showing that he knows the right answer and is passing it by. Munchausen syndrome is built around pseudologia fantastica, the pathological lying taught in Section 2.7.

With the named syndromes in place, one thread runs on into the next section. Reduplicative paramnesia was called a disorder of memory by the man who named it, and the misidentification syndromes as a whole have been described as lying at the far end of a continuum of anomalies of familiarity whose mild, everyday end is déjà vu. Section 2.7 starts from the other end, with what happens when memory itself is lost or distorted.

2.7 Disorders of Memory: Amnesia and the ParamnesiasMedium yield · 5 Qs

Everybody forgets. You walk into a room and cannot remember why; you meet a colleague at a wedding and her name has gone; you are sure you locked the scooter and you go back to check. None of that is psychopathology. Ordinary forgetting is selective (we lose the unimportant and the unrehearsed), it is proportionate to how well something was learned and how long ago, it improves with cues, and the person knows that he has forgotten. Forgetting becomes a sign when it breaks those rules: when it is out of proportion to the person's age, attention and the importance of what was lost, when it is a change from his own previous level, when it takes a pattern that ordinary forgetting never takes (a man who cannot remember that his wife died last year, a woman who forgets her own name but not the price of rice), or when what comes back is not a gap but a memory of something that never happened.

Descriptive psychopathology sorts the disorders of memory into two great families and one small one. The first family is loss: amnesia in its several forms. The second is distortion: the paramnesias, in which what is remembered, or the feeling that something is remembered, is false. The small family is excess: hypermnesia. Where memory is normally stored, how it is forgotten and what the brain does in amnesia belong to Book 1, Chapter 7, and Book 1, Sections 7.10.1 and 7.10.3 teach the kinds of amnesia and Korsakoff's syndrome with their neurology. How memory is tested at the bedside belongs to Book 2, Section 2.6.5, which you should have in mind before reading on. In brief: immediate memory (registration, tested with digit span or by repeating three words at once) is retention over seconds; recent memory (the three words after about five minutes, the events of yesterday) is retention over minutes to days; remote memory (personal and public events of years ago) is retention over years. By Ribot's law, described by Théodule-Armand Ribot (1839 to 1916) in 1881, recent memories are lost before remote ones in brain disease. And in depression the complaint of poor memory often exceeds the measured deficit, the picture called pseudodementia. This section describes the phenomena those tests uncover.

Amnesia: when forgetting becomes a symptom

In psychopathology, amnesia is a pathological loss of memory: partial or total, temporary or permanent, out of proportion to ordinary forgetting and, in its pure forms, out of proportion to any other cognitive impairment. It is defined by what is missing from memory, not by how the person behaves, feels or perceives. A man with amnesia may also be agitated, low in mood or misperceiving his surroundings, but those are separate findings recorded under their own headings. The cause may be organic (a head injury, a stroke, a tumour, an infection such as herpes simplex encephalitis, a deficiency such as that of thiamine, a degenerative disease, a drug, a seizure, electroconvulsive therapy) or psychological, as in dissociative amnesia after overwhelming stress. One rule follows from the definition and saves many errors: amnesia is never diagnosed from the patient's word alone. A memory gap must be tested, its edges mapped and its content checked against an informant, because a fluent answer can be invented (as you will see under confabulation) and a claimed loss can be feigned.

Exam trap

"Amnesia is a clinical condition of..." has one answer: pathological loss of memory. It is not a behavioural disturbance (that would be recorded under appearance and behaviour or under disorders of movement and will, Section 2.9), not depressed affect (a disorder of emotion, Section 2.8) and not impaired perception (illusions and hallucinations, Section 2.3). Depression, delirium and dementia can all cause amnesia, which is exactly why the distractors are tempting; but the word itself names only the memory loss.

The forms of amnesia

The first and most useful way to describe an amnesia is by time. Fix the moment of onset (the blow to the head, the stroke, the start of the illness) as a point on a line. The first, anterograde amnesia, is the inability to lay down new memories from that point onwards: the person cannot learn the name of his new doctor, forgets within minutes that his daughter visited, reads the same newspaper article three times with fresh interest. The second, retrograde amnesia, is the loss of memories formed before that point. It usually follows Ribot's gradient, the last months or years before the onset being lost while childhood is preserved, and after a head injury it often shrinks during recovery, the older memories returning first, until only a short gap of seconds or minutes before the injury remains, a gap that is usually permanent because those memories were never consolidated. Most organic amnesias combine the two, and the anterograde part is usually the more disabling, because a person who cannot form new memories cannot keep track of his own life.

A second description is by extent. A lacunar amnesia (circumscribed amnesia) is a gap, a lacuna, for a defined period, with good memory on either side of it. The commonest causes are a head injury, an epileptic seizure, an episode of delirium, a course of electroconvulsive therapy and heavy drinking. The amnesia of intoxication is the alcoholic blackout, also called a palimpsest (after the old parchments that were scraped clean and written over): the drinker wakes with no memory of how he got home, though he was awake, talking and even driving at the time, because the alcohol prevented the events from being recorded. Blackouts may be complete (en bloc) or patchy, the "greyouts" that drinkers describe, in which islands of memory return with cues. They are an early warning sign of harmful drinking (Chapter 10). Electroconvulsive therapy produces an anterograde difficulty during the course of treatment and a patchy retrograde loss for the period around it; most of this recovers over the following weeks and months, though some gaps for the treatment period can persist (Section 5.7.7).

Two time-limited amnesias have names of their own. The first, post-traumatic amnesia (PTA), is the period after a head injury during which the person is awake but unable to lay down continuous memories; he may talk, eat and answer questions, but he is confused and later remembers nothing of the period except perhaps "islands". It ends when continuous day-to-day memory returns, and because its length is closely related to the amount of brain damage, it is one of the best clinical measures of the severity of a head injury: in one widely used scheme, a PTA of under an hour marks a mild injury, one of one to 24 hours a moderate injury and one of more than a day a severe injury. Clinicians track it day by day with brief orientation and memory questionnaires (the Assessment book names them). The second, transient global amnesia, named by the American neurologists C. Miller Fisher and Raymond Adams, whose full description appeared in 1964, is a sudden attack of dense anterograde amnesia, with a patchy loss of the recent past, in a middle-aged or older person, usually in the fifties, sixties or seventies. The attack often follows physical exertion, a plunge into cold water, sexual intercourse or an emotional shock. For a few hours the person is bewildered and asks the same questions over and over ("What day is it? How did we get here?"), forgetting each answer within a minute, yet he keeps his personal identity throughout and can still drive a car or follow a recipe. The attack resolves within 24 hours, leaving a permanent gap for the hours it lasted, and in most people it never recurs. The British neurologists John Hodges and Charles Warlow, in a 1990 study of 153 patients with transient amnesia, separated true transient global amnesia, with its excellent outlook, from attacks that are brief or repeatedly recurrent, which are more often epileptic.

The most famous organic amnesia is the one described in 1887 by the Russian psychiatrist Sergei Sergeievich Korsakoff (1854 to 1900) in chronic heavy drinkers. Korsakoff syndrome results from thiamine (vitamin B1) deficiency, often after an acute Wernicke encephalopathy, and damages the mammillary bodies and the medial thalamus (Book 1, Section 7.10.3; Chapter 10). DSM-5-TR no longer uses the eponym as a diagnosis: it is recorded as alcohol-induced major neurocognitive disorder, amnestic-confabulatory type. Its text describes the picture exactly: an impairment in recent memory out of proportion to the other neurocognitive symptoms, with prominent amnesia (severe difficulty learning new information, with rapid forgetting) and a tendency to confabulate, although, it adds, confabulation can be seen with any severe loss of recent memory. Immediate memory, measured by digit span, is typically normal, which is one of the clues that the problem is in storing memories, not in attending to them.

Finally there is the amnesia that has no lesion. In dissociative amnesia (Section 7.5.5) the person loses autobiographical memories, usually of a traumatic or intensely stressful period, and sometimes of his whole identity, while his ability to learn new information is intact. The organic and the dissociative amnesias fail in almost opposite directions, and Section 7.5.7 sets out the full comparison. The essentials are in the box below and the forms of amnesia in Table 2.18.

Remember

Organic amnesia: mainly anterograde; new learning fails; retrograde loss follows Ribot's gradient; personal identity preserved until very late; a medical cause; not reversed by hypnosis or sedative interviews. Dissociative amnesia: retrograde and autobiographical; new learning intact; the gap is often for a traumatic period, and identity can be the first thing lost; linked in time to severe stress; the memories may return suddenly. Transient global amnesia is the classic distractor: sudden and dramatic, but identity is never lost.

Table 2.18 The kinds of amnesia.
KindWhat is lostTime spanUsual causes
AnterogradeThe ability to form new memoriesFrom the onset onwardsKorsakoff syndrome; hippocampal and thalamic damage; Alzheimer's disease; benzodiazepines; transient global amnesia
RetrogradeMemories formed before the onset; recent before remote (Ribot)Backwards from the onset; may shrink with recoveryHead injury; Korsakoff syndrome; dementia; electroconvulsive therapy
Lacunar (circumscribed)Memory for one defined periodMinutes to days, with normal memory on either sideHead injury; seizures; delirium; alcohol blackouts; electroconvulsive therapy
Post-traumaticContinuous memory after a head injuryFrom the injury until continuous memory returnsTraumatic brain injury; its length measures severity
Transient globalNew learning, with patchy loss of the recent past; identity keptHours; under 24Unknown; after exertion, cold water or emotion, usually aged 50 to 70
DissociativeAutobiographical memory, often of a trauma; new learning intactLocalised, selective or generalisedSevere stress or trauma (Section 7.5.5)

Hypermnesia: memory that is too vivid

The opposite of amnesia is rarer and less often noticed. The term hypermnesia means an exaggerated degree of retention and recall: memories, usually of the past, return with unusual vividness, clarity and detail. In psychopathology it is described in mania, where a flood of old memories may feed the pressure of speech; under hypnosis, where people recall more (though, as Book 1, Section 7.9 shows, they also recall more that is false, and they are more confident about all of it); in some fevers and near-death states; and in the intrusive memories of trauma, which return with sensory sharpness and can be relived as flashbacks (Section 7.2.4). Experimental psychology uses the word in a narrower sense: the American psychologist Matthew Erdelyi, from the mid-1970s, showed that when people try repeatedly to recall the same material, especially pictures, their total recall can improve from one attempt to the next, a net gain also called hypermnesia. For the examination, the clinical sense is the one to know: "memory of past events with unusual clarity" is hypermnesia.

The paramnesias: a map

In the paramnesias memory is not lost but falsified. The word covers two quite different kinds of error, and it helps to separate them before meeting the individual phenomena. In a distortion of recall the content of what is remembered is false: the person retrieves a memory of something that did not happen, or did not happen that way, or did not happen to him. In a distortion of recognition the content is not the problem; it is the feeling of familiarity attached to an experience that is wrong. A new situation feels already known (déjà vu), or a known situation feels never seen (jamais vu), or a known place feels as if it were somewhere else (reduplicative paramnesia). Figure 2.11 draws the family tree. Two of its branches belong to other sections: delusional memory, a memory given sudden delusional significance, is one of Jaspers' forms of primary delusion (Section 2.5), and reduplicative paramnesia was taught with the misidentification syndromes (Section 2.6).

Confabulation

Ramesh is 52 and has been on the medical ward of a district hospital in Mandya for nine days, admitted confused and unsteady after a long period of heavy drinking and very little eating. His confusion has cleared with intravenous thiamine, and he is friendly, polite and talkative. Asked what he did this morning, he answers at once: "This morning? I went to my godown at six. A lorry of cement came from Tumakuru, three hundred bags, and I had to stand there till the boys unloaded it, otherwise they steal. Then I had idli at Udupi Krishna Bhavan and came here to see you." He has not left the ward. When the house surgeon gently points this out, he smiles and agrees that perhaps it was yesterday, and when she asks him the same question an hour later he describes, with the same easy confidence, a meeting with his bank manager. Some of the pieces are real. He did own a hardware godown, years ago; he used to eat at that hotel. What he cannot do is place them in time, and he does not know that he cannot.

That is confabulation: the production of false memories, often detailed, fluent and believable, that fill the gaps in an amnesia, given without any intention to deceive and without awareness that they are false. The standard British definition is the one given in 1972 by N. Berlyne: confabulation is "a falsification of memory occurring in clear consciousness in association with an organically derived amnesia". Each phrase of that definition rules something out. "Falsification of memory" separates it from lying about the present. "In clear consciousness" separates it from the muddled, fluctuating talk of delirium, in which the person is not fully alert at all (Section 2.2). "Organically derived amnesia" ties it to a memory deficit, and separates it from the fantastic stories of psychosis and from pathological lying.

Two kinds have been recognised since the German psychiatrist Karl Bonhoeffer (1868 to 1948) studied alcoholic patients in 1901. In momentary confabulation (Bonhoeffer called it the confabulation of embarrassment) the patient, asked a question he cannot answer from memory, fills the gap with a plausible account, usually built from real but misplaced fragments of his own past, as Ramesh did. In fantastic confabulation the patient produces, unprompted, stories that are grandiose, bizarre or impossible: a voyage on a warship, a meeting with a prime minister, a battle won. In 1987 the London neuropsychiatrist Michael D. Kopelman renamed the two as provoked confabulation, which he argued may be a normal response to a faulty memory (healthy people produce similar errors when asked to recall a story after a long delay), and spontaneous confabulation, which he argued results from frontal dysfunction superimposed on an organic amnesia. Spontaneous confabulators sometimes act on their false memories: a retired man gets dressed to go to the office he left years ago, a woman packs to catch a train to a wedding that took place decades before.

Where is confabulation seen? Above all in Korsakoff syndrome, especially in its early stage; after rupture of an aneurysm of the anterior communicating artery, which damages the basal forebrain and the ventromedial frontal lobes; with other frontal lesions; in Alzheimer's disease and other dementias; and in the denial of disability after right-hemisphere stroke (Section 2.10). How does it arise? The leading account links it to failed source monitoring (Book 1, Section 7.9.4): the patient retrieves real fragments of memory but cannot tell when, where or in what context they occurred, or whether he did something or only thought about it, and his damaged frontal lobes fail to check the result. That is why so many confabulations are true memories in the wrong place. One last point is worth carrying to the ward: a fluent answer to "What did you do yesterday?" proves nothing about memory until someone who was there has confirmed it.

Exam trap

A Korsakoff patient who gives detailed, believable but false accounts of his past to fill memory gaps, without intending to deceive, is confabulating. It is not lying, because there is no intent to deceive and no awareness of falsity. It is not malingering, the deliberate faking or exaggeration of symptoms for an external gain such as compensation or avoiding duty (Section 7.9.6). It is not confusion or delirium, which are disturbances of consciousness and attention (Section 2.2); Berlyne's definition requires clear consciousness. And it is not the delusional memory of psychosis, in which a memory is given a sudden delusional meaning (Section 2.5). Confabulation is a paramnesia, a distortion of recall.

Pseudologia fantastica, retrospective falsification and cryptomnesia

If confabulation is the honest error of an amnesic person, pseudologia fantastica (pathological lying, mythomania) is its unsettling cousin: the telling of elaborate, often grandiose stories that mix fact and fantasy, which the teller seems half to believe. It was described in 1891 by the German psychiatrist Anton Delbrück. The stories are usually about the teller himself: a distinguished family, heroic deeds, important connections, dramatic illnesses, a career as a pilot, an army officer or a surgeon. They are plausible enough to be believed for a while, they are told repeatedly and embroidered over the years, and the lying seems to serve the teller's self-image more than any obvious external gain. When the teller is confronted with clear evidence he may drop the story, admit that it was untrue, or simply move on to a new one. Pseudologia fantastica is the second of the four features of Munchausen syndrome (Section 7.9.3), and it is seen in factitious disorder generally and in antisocial, histrionic and narcissistic personality disorders (Chapter 11).

Set it beside confabulation and the differences are clear. The pseudologue has no amnesia and his memory tests normally; he retains some awareness that the stories are untrue, enough to drop them under pressure; and the stories are not filling gaps in memory but building a more impressive self. Set it beside ordinary lying and the difference is that ordinary lies serve a clear purpose (to escape punishment, to gain money) and are abandoned once the purpose is served, while pathological lies are excessive, repetitive and out of proportion to any gain, and the teller seems caught up in them.

The third distortion of recall happens to everyone a little and to some people a great deal: retrospective falsification, the unconscious distortion of past memories so that they fit the person's present mood, beliefs or circumstances. The depressed woman who says that her childhood was entirely miserable and that her marriage has always been empty, when her sister remembers a happy girl and the photographs show a smiling bride, is not lying; her present mood is selecting and colouring what she can retrieve, the mood-congruent memory of Book 1, Section 7.7.5. The man with a persecutory delusion who reinterprets a quarrel with a neighbour twenty years ago as the first move in the plot is doing the same in the service of a belief. The practical lesson is that a history taken from a patient in the grip of a mood or a delusion must be checked against an informant. Delusional memory, in which a real or false memory is suddenly given delusional significance, is a different phenomenon and belongs to Section 2.5. The larger question of whether whole false memories of events, including childhood abuse, can be created by suggestion is the false memory and recovered memory debate (Book 1, Section 7.9, and Section 7.6.5).

The last distortion of recall is a failure to recognise a memory as a memory. In cryptomnesia (hidden memory) something once read, heard or seen is forgotten, then returns without being recognised as a memory, so that the person experiences it as an original idea or creation. The term was used by the Swiss psychologist Théodore Flournoy (1854 to 1920) of the University of Geneva, who spent five years studying a medium he called Hélène Smith and published his account, From India to the Planet Mars, in 1900. Many of the "revelations" she produced in trance, he concluded, were latent memories of things she had once encountered, transformed by imagination. Cryptomnesia is the basis of unconscious plagiarism; Book 1, Section 7.9.4 tells the case of a famous popular song and the laboratory studies that reproduced the effect.

Déjà vu and the distortions of recognition

Shabnam is 19, a second-year engineering student in Pune. For about a year she has had brief, strange spells. She will be sitting in the canteen, and suddenly the whole scene, the clatter of plates, a friend's sentence, the light on the table, feels as if it has happened before, exactly so, and she has an eerie sense that she knows what will be said next. It lasts less than a minute. Sometimes there is a rising feeling in her stomach and a faint smell of burning rubber that no one else notices, and twice her friends have told her that she stared and smacked her lips for a few seconds and did not answer them. An EEG shows epileptic discharges over the right temporal lobe. Shabnam's spells are the aura and the onset of focal seizures, and the feeling of having lived through the moment before is one of the classic experiences of temporal lobe epilepsy.

The everyday form of the experience is déjà vu (French, "already seen"), the illusion of recognition: the strong but false feeling that a new situation has been experienced before, while the person knows perfectly well that it has not. That last part is important: in ordinary déjà vu insight is preserved, and the experience is puzzling precisely because the person can see that the familiarity is wrong. The term was used in 1876 by the French philosopher Émile Boirac. Most people have had the experience. In a 2003 review of more than a century of surveys, the American psychologist Alan S. Brown estimated that about 60 per cent of people have experienced déjà vu (some surveys give about two-thirds); its frequency decreases with age, so that it is commonest in adolescents and young adults; it is associated with stress and fatigue; and, perhaps surprisingly, it is reported more often by people with more education and higher socioeconomic status, and by those who travel often. Brown grouped the explanations into four kinds: dual processing (two normally synchronised processes, such as familiarity and recollection, momentarily out of step), neurological (a small seizure-like discharge or a delay in neural transmission), memory (implicit familiarity from something genuinely similar that is not consciously recalled) and attentional (a scene glimpsed without attention and then perceived again with attention a moment later).

The pathological form belongs to epilepsy. The English neurologist John Hughlings Jackson (1835 to 1911), who worked out much of what we know about focal seizures, described the "dreamy state" of temporal lobe seizures, with its intense feelings of reminiscence and unreality, in the 1880s. Déjà vu that is frequent, prolonged, accompanied by other aura symptoms (a rising epigastric feeling, smells, fear), followed by confusion, or witnessed with automatisms should be investigated as possible epilepsy, as Shabnam's was. Déjà vu is also reported in anxiety and depersonalisation, and with some drugs.

The other "déjà" experiences are variations on the same theme in other forms. The first is déjà entendu ("already heard"), the illusion of having heard something before: a sentence, a piece of music or a conversation feels as if it had been heard previously when it has not. A more complete form is déjà vécu ("already lived"), in which the whole present moment feels as if it is being relived, often with the sense of knowing what will happen next, as Shabnam felt. In some older people with dementia or mild cognitive impairment the feeling becomes persistent and is believed: they stop watching the news because "this is a repeat", or refuse an appointment because they "have already been". Chris Moulin and his colleagues, who have studied such patients, call this recollective confabulation, because the patient supports the false feeling of familiarity with invented details, and families describe it as persistent déjà vu. Finally, jamais vu ("never seen") is the reverse of déjà vu: a familiar situation, place, person or word suddenly feels strange, new and unfamiliar, although the person knows that it is not. It occurs with fatigue, in temporal lobe epilepsy and in anxiety, and you can produce it in yourself: write a common word over and over and it soon begins to look foreign. In one study by Moulin, about two-thirds of volunteers who wrote the word "door" thirty times in a minute reported this kind of strangeness, some beginning to doubt that it was a real word.

Reduplicative paramnesia (Section 2.6) belongs on this branch too: the familiarity of a place is real, but it is attached to the wrong location. Table 2.19 gathers every paramnesia in one place.

Table 2.19 The paramnesias.

Pseudologia fantasticaElaborate, often grandiose stories mixing fact and fantasy, half believed by the tellerRecallFactitious disorder and Munchausen syndrome; antisocial, histrionic and narcissistic personality
Retrospective falsificationThe past unconsciously rewritten to fit present mood or beliefRecallDepression; paranoid states; everyday life

Exam trap

Déjà entendu is the illusion of having heard something before. Its distractors each name a different phenomenon: the illusion of having seen something before is déjà vu; "memory of past events with clarity" is hypermnesia (Section 2.7.3); "repetitive involuntary speech" is a speech sign such as palilalia (repeating one's own words) or verbigeration (repeating words or phrases senselessly), taught in Section 2.4. The French words do the work for you: vu seen, entendu heard, vécu lived.

Exam trap

The "illusion of recognition", in which a new situation feels as if it had been experienced before, is déjà vu. Jamais vu is its opposite, the familiar felt as unfamiliar. Pseudologia fantastica is pathological lying (Anton Delbrück, 1891). Confabulation fills memory gaps with false memories without intent to deceive. All four are paramnesias, but only déjà vu and jamais vu are distortions of recognition.

Memory in the major disorders

Each of the phenomena in this section turns up in particular disorders, and the pattern of a memory disturbance is often more useful for diagnosis than its severity. Delirium spoils memory at the first step: a person who cannot attend cannot register, so immediate recall is poor, and afterwards there is a patchy amnesia for the whole episode. Dementia, by contrast, typically begins with a failure to retain new information while immediate memory and remote memories hold up, following Ribot's law. Korsakoff syndrome is the purest amnesia, dense and anterograde, with intact immediate memory and confabulation. Depression produces memory complaints that are greater than the deficit found on testing, with effortful, "I don't know" answers. Dissociative amnesia removes a block of autobiographical memory while sparing new learning; post-traumatic stress disorder floods the person with intrusive memories while leaving gaps for parts of the trauma; and in malingered amnesia the pattern is inconsistent and implausible, and performance on forced-choice memory tests can fall below chance, the level that guessing alone would produce. Table 2.20 sets out the patterns with the places where each disorder is taught.

Table 2.20 The memory picture in the major disorders.

Dementia (Alzheimer's disease)New learning fails first; recent memory lost before remote (Ribot); insight often reducedChapter 10

Answers: (1) Capgras, (2) Fregoli, (3) prosopagnosia, (4) négations (negation), (5) Othello, (6) matchbox (specimen), (7) Diogenes, (8) confabulation, (9) pseudologia fantastica, (10) entendu; jamais.

2.8 Disorders of Emotion: Mood, Affect and Their VocabularyHigh yield · 8 Qs

Two people wept in the psychiatry outpatient department of a Bengaluru hospital on the same Tuesday morning. Preeti, a 34-year-old schoolteacher, cried quietly as she described the last four months. She woke at four every morning with a weight on her chest; the Carnatic songs she had sung since childhood now meant nothing to her; and when her daughter came first in her class, she had smiled for the photograph and felt, in her words, "nothing, like a wall". Jagdish, a 68-year-old retired railway clerk who had come south to live with his son after a second stroke, cried far more dramatically. Great heaving sobs began when the receptionist wished him good morning and stopped, as abruptly as they had started, half a minute later. "I am not sad, doctor," he said, wiping his face and looking embarrassed. "My grandson is getting married next month. These tears just come. Last week I laughed like this at my cousin's funeral."

From the doorway the two scenes look alike: an adult in tears in front of a doctor. Underneath, they are opposites. Preeti's tears expressed a feeling that had soaked into every hour of her day for months. Jagdish's tears expressed nothing that he felt; they were closer to a reflex, released by damage to the brain pathways that normally keep emotional expression in step with emotional experience. To tell the two apart you need a vocabulary that separates what a person feels from what a person shows, a lasting state from a passing one, and a disorder of feeling from a disorder of expression. This section builds that vocabulary. Book 2, Sections 2.4.6 and 2.4.7 show how the clinician asks about mood and records affect at interview; here the aim is to understand each term as a phenomenon, where it came from, and which disorders it points to.

Four words: emotion, feeling, mood and affect

Everyday English uses "emotion", "feeling", "mood" and "affect" almost interchangeably. Psychology does not, and Book 1, Section 11.1.2 sets out how general psychology separates them. An emotion is a brief, many-sided reaction to a particular event, lasting seconds to minutes: a flash of anger when someone pushes ahead of you in the ration-shop queue, a jolt of fear when a dog lunges at your scooter. A feeling is the experienced side of that reaction, what the anger or the fear is like from the inside. Research psychologists also use "affect" as an umbrella word for every state that feels good or bad, which is why questionnaires speak of "positive affect" and "negative affect". Clinical psychopathology narrows all this into two working terms, and almost everything else in this section hangs on them.

Mood is a pervasive and sustained emotional state, lasting days or weeks rather than minutes, that colours how the person sees himself, the world and the future. It is known chiefly from what the person tells you, so it is subjective, and it is judged over a period of time. Affect is the way emotion shows itself, here and now, second by second, carried by the face, the eyes, the voice and its melody (prosody), posture, gesture and tears. It is judged by the clinician, here and now, during the interview. The usual image, which Book 2 uses and Section 5.1.1 repeats, is that mood is the climate and affect the weather. Kerala has a wet climate, yet a particular afternoon in Kochi may be dry and bright; a man whose mood is depressed may laugh at a joke for a few seconds without his mood having lifted at all.

Why insist on two words when one would seem to do? Three reasons stand out. First, mood and affect are measured differently and can disagree, and the disagreement is itself a finding. Preeti reported a depressed mood and showed a sad, tearful affect: the two matched. Jagdish reported a contented mood and showed sudden sobbing: his affect was wildly out of keeping with his mood, and that dissociation is the clue to his diagnosis. A young man who insists "I am perfectly fine" while his eyes fill with tears shows a mismatch of the opposite kind. Second, the diagnostic systems define the mood disorders by mood, not affect: a major depressive episode requires depressed mood or loss of interest most of the day, nearly every day, for two weeks (Section 5.2.2), and no single tearful moment in a clinic can establish that. Third, several of the most important signs in this section are disorders of affect alone. Blunting, incongruity and pseudobulbar outbursts are things the clinician sees, and the person may feel quite differently inside, as the study of blunting described in Section 4.3.2 showed.

When the affect fits the reported mood, the clinician records it as mood-congruent affect: the sad face and slowed voice of a person who also says she feels low. The phrase turns up in examination options, usually as a distractor ("mood-congruent affect in major depressive disorder"), and it simply describes the normal relation between feeling and showing.
