Do not confuse it with mood-congruent psychotic features, in which the content of a delusion or hallucination matches the mood, as with delusions of guilt and ruin in severe depression (Section 2.5 and Chapter 5).

Indian context

Few patients say "my mood is depressed". In many North Indian clinics you will hear "man nahi lagta" (my mind does not settle on anything, which often turns out to be loss of interest), "ghabrahat" (a mixture of nervousness, restlessness and a pounding heart) or simply "tension", which can mean worry, sadness, anger or a headache. In every part of the country many people describe distress first through the body: tiredness, burning, a heavy head. Record the person's own words in quotation marks, then ask what they mean. The technical label comes afterwards, and it must describe the phenomenon, not translate the idiom word for word.

Exam trap

"Our current mood and feelings", meaning the emotion shown at this moment, is best called affect. Mood is the sustained state over days or weeks; an emotion is a brief reaction to a particular event; a feeling is the experienced side of an emotion. Mood is reported (subjective), affect is observed (objective): mood is the climate, affect the weather. In general psychology "affect" is also the umbrella word for all good or bad feeling states (Book 1, Section 11.1.2), so a research question may use it in that wider sense. "Mood-congruent affect" is affect that fits the mood; it is normal, not a sign of disorder.

Disorders of mood: quality, reactivity and rhythm

The first thing to describe about a mood is its quality, the kind of feeling it is. The commonest abnormal quality is depressed mood: sadness, lowness, gloom or hopelessness, often felt in the body as heaviness or, as Preeti put it, a weight on the chest. Not every low mood is alike. In its specifier for melancholic features DSM-5-TR asks whether the mood has a "distinct quality", a profound despondency, despair or moroseness, or a so-called empty mood, which is experienced as different in kind from the low mood of a nonmelancholic depression, not simply a bigger dose of it. A mood that is simply more severe, longer lasting or without a reason does not count as distinct. People with melancholia often say that it is not like grief at all: grief has an object and comes in waves, whereas this is a flat, grey heaviness that lies over everything.

Elevated mood is a mood more cheerful than usual and more cheerful than the person's circumstances justify; its grades are the subject of Section 2.8.3. Expansive mood is a different idea: not how high the mood is but how far it reaches outwards. Niraj Ahuja's Short Textbook of Psychiatry, a standard Indian text, describes it as an unceasing and unselective enthusiasm for interacting with people and the surroundings, and DSM-5-TR's account of mania speaks of an unlimited and haphazard enthusiasm for interpersonal, sexual or occupational interactions, with the person striking up long conversations with strangers in public. The expansive person shows feeling without restraint and often carries an inflated sense of his own importance. Irritable mood is a readiness to be annoyed and provoked to anger. In mania it often replaces elation, especially when the person's wishes are blocked, and DSM-5-TR then asks for four rather than three of the additional manic symptoms (Section 5.4.1). In children and adolescents an irritable mood can stand in for a depressed one when a major depressive episode is diagnosed, and irritability is common in post-traumatic stress, dementia, withdrawal states and many medical illnesses.

Anxious mood is apprehension, a tense expectation that something bad is about to happen, and it is taken up with the other morbid emotional states in Section 2.8.4. Dysphoria (from the Greek for "hard to bear") is an unpleasant mood that is not simply sadness but a mixture of sadness, tension, irritability and discontent. It is the mood of premenstrual dysphoric disorder (Section 5.3.3), of withdrawal from stimulants (DSM-5-TR builds its stimulant withdrawal syndrome on "dysphoric mood"), and of the mixed states in which manic and depressive features combine (Chapter 5). Euthymia is normal mood: neither low nor high, the ordinary middle of the range. It is the right word for a person whose mood shows nothing abnormal, and for a person with bipolar disorder who is well between episodes. It does not mean "happy". An apathetic mood is one in which feeling, interest and concern seem to have drained away (Section 2.8.6). Finally, some people in an acute psychosis or a delirium show perplexity, a puzzled bewilderment, as if the world had suddenly stopped making sense and they were searching for its meaning.

A mood has a quality, but it also has a behaviour over time, and two features of that behaviour matter clinically. The first is mood reactivity: does the mood lift, even briefly, when something good happens? Ordinary low moods do. So do many depressions: in depression with atypical features, reactivity is the defining requirement, and the person brightens when a friend visits or good news arrives, only to sink back afterwards (Section 5.3.8). In melancholic depression the mood is unreactive. DSM-5-TR gives a practical guideline: even highly desired events bring no marked brightening, and if the mood lifts at all it reaches perhaps 20 to 40 percent of normal for only minutes at a time. Preeti's smile for the photograph, with "nothing, like a wall" behind it, is exactly this.

The second feature is diurnal variation, a regular change in the mood across the twenty-four hours. In melancholia the depression is regularly worse in the morning, and the person often wakes two or more hours early (both appear among the DSM-5-TR melancholic criteria), lies awake in the dark at the lowest point of the day and feels a little better by evening. The opposite rhythm, worsening in the late afternoon and at night, is typical of delirium (the "sundowning" of Section 2.2), and it is one of the clues that an older person's withdrawal on a medical ward is not depression. Two further time words complete the picture. Lability of mood means rapid swings over minutes or hours, which DSM-5-TR describes in mania as an alternation among euphoria, dysphoria and irritability; it is taught with labile affect in Section 2.8.8. Persistence is how long a mood state lasts, and the time rules of the mood disorders depend on it: at least one week for a manic episode, four days for a hypomanic one, two weeks for a major depressive episode and two years for persistent depressive disorder (Chapter 5). Table 2.21 brings the mood terms together.

Table 2.21 The vocabulary of mood: what the person reports and where each state is found.
TermWhat the person reportsTypical settings
DepressedSadness, lowness, hopelessness; in melancholia a distinct or "empty" qualityDepressive episodes, bipolar depression, many medical illnesses
ElevatedCheerfulness beyond what events justify; graded from euphoria to ecstasyHypomania, mania; frontal lobe disease, drugs
ExpansiveUnrestrained, unselective enthusiasm for people and activity; inflated self-importanceMania, hypomania
IrritableEasily annoyed and provoked to angerMania when thwarted, depression in young people, PTSD, dementia, withdrawal
AnxiousApprehension, tense expectation of harmAnxiety disorders, depression, psychosis, hyperthyroidism, alcohol withdrawal
DysphoricAn unpleasant mixture of sadness, tension and irritabilityPremenstrual dysphoric disorder, stimulant withdrawal, mixed states
EuthymicNormal range, neither low nor highHealth; bipolar disorder between episodes
ApatheticAbsence of feeling, interest and concernDementia, frontal lesions, chronic schizophrenia, severe depression
PerplexedPuzzled bewildermentAcute psychosis, delirium
Reactive or unreactiveMood does or does not brighten with good eventsKept in atypical depression; lost in melancholia
Diurnal variationA regular rise and fall across the dayWorst in the morning: melancholia; worst in the evening: delirium
Exam trap

Euthymic means normal mood, not happy. Expansive describes how far the mood reaches out (unrestrained enthusiasm, self-importance), not how high it is. Dysphoria is an unpleasant mixed mood, not a synonym for depression. Mood reactivity is kept in atypical depression and lost in melancholia. Worse in the morning points to melancholia; worse in the evening points to delirium. In mania an irritable mood can replace an elevated one, but then four additional symptoms are needed instead of three.

The grades of elevated mood: euphoria, elation, exaltation, ecstasy

How do you describe a mood that is too high? Indian examiners like a precise answer, and it comes as a ladder of four steps. The clearest way to learn the ladder is to watch someone climb it. Ashok, a 27-year-old engineer with a Pune software firm, had one previous episode of depression in college. In the first week of a new illness his colleagues noticed only that he was in unusually good spirits. He joked through meetings, praised everyone's work, slept five hours instead of seven and said he had never felt better in his life. Nothing in his circumstances had changed. This first step is euphoria: a mild elevation of mood, an exaggerated sense of psychological well-being and happiness that is out of keeping with what is actually happening. It is the mood of hypomania, and on its own it can look like a pleasant personality on a good day.

By the second week Ashok's cheerfulness had become infectious and energetic. He laughed loudly, made everyone around him laugh, took on three new projects, started planning a start-up at two in the morning and could not understand why his manager wanted him to slow down. This is elation: a moderate elevation, a feeling of confidence and enjoyment together with increased psychomotor activity. It is the classic mood of mania. A few days later the mood had become something larger. Ashok announced that his software would end poverty in India, that the Prime Minister's office would soon call him, and that ordinary rules about money no longer applied to him; he transferred most of his savings to strangers who had praised his ideas. This is exaltation: intense elation combined with grandiosity, typically with delusions of grandeur, the mood of severe mania. Finally, on the night his family brought him to hospital, he stood motionless on the terrace with his arms raised, tears of joy on his face, saying that he had become one with God and could hear the universe singing, and he barely responded when spoken to. This last step is ecstasy: a feeling of intense rapture or bliss, often with a sense of union with something greater than oneself, seen at the most severe end of mania, in what older texts call delirious or stuporous mania.

Where does this ladder come from? It is not in DSM-5-TR, which grades nothing: its criterion for mania speaks of an "abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood" and adds that the mood is often described as euphoric, excessively cheerful, high or "feeling on top of the world". The four-step scale is a teaching device of Indian textbooks built on the older European vocabulary of mood. Ahuja's Short Textbook of Psychiatry sets it out as four stages that the elevated mood can pass through as a manic episode becomes more severe: euphoria (Stage I, usually hypomania), elation (Stage II, classically mania), exaltation (Stage III, severe mania with delusions of grandeur) and ecstasy (Stage IV, typically delirious or stuporous mania). Indian question papers follow it closely, and Figure 2.12 draws it as a stair.

Put ordinary happiness at the foot of the stair and the meaning of "morbid" becomes clear. When Ashok's sister passed her chartered accountancy examination after three attempts, she was overjoyed: she laughed, phoned everyone she knew and could not sleep that night. Her joy was intense, but it had a cause, it was in proportion to the cause, it faded over a day or two, it did not make her believe she could run the Reserve Bank, and it cost her neither judgement nor money. Each of those five features was missing from Ashok's elevation. Morbid elevated mood is out of proportion or without a cause, persists for days, drives a rise in energy and activity with a reduced need for sleep, and progressively erodes judgement and insight, so that the person feels best precisely when he is most ill.

Elevated mood is not confined to mania. Euphoria appears with alcohol, opioids, cannabis and stimulants (DSM-5-TR lists euphoria among the effects of intoxication with several substances), with corticosteroids, and in diseases of the brain. A placid, unconcerned cheerfulness has long been described in advanced multiple sclerosis, and frontal lobe damage produces a euphoria of its own. In 1888 the German physician Moritz Jastrowitz described moria, a childish, silly, euphoric excitement, in patients with frontal tumours, and in 1890 the German neurologist Hermann Oppenheim (1858 to 1919) described Witzelsucht ("joking addiction"), a compulsive habit of making trivial, pointless and often sarcastic jokes and puns, in patients with frontal lobe tumours, linking it especially to the right side. DSM-5-TR still lists facetiousness, disinhibition, euphoria and a lack of judgement or foresight as the picture that frontal lobe injury can produce in personality change due to another medical condition. Ecstasy, for its part, occurs outside illness in religious and mystical experience, and in disease as the ecstatic aura of some temporal lobe seizures and with hallucinogens; the name of the drug MDMA, "ecstasy", borrows the word but is irrelevant to the grading.

Exam trap

The order from mildest to most severe is euphoria, elation, exaltation, ecstasy. Given i) elation, ii) exaltation, iii) euphoria and iv) ecstasy, the answer is iii, i, ii, iv. Ecstasy is the extreme, not euphoria. Memory hook: eu-phoria "feels good", e-lation adds labour (activity), e-xaltation adds an exaggerated self (grandiosity), e-cstasy is the climax (rapture). The grading comes from Indian and older European teaching; DSM-5-TR speaks only of elevated, expansive or irritable mood. A reduced need for sleep belongs to mania, not to anhedonia or depression.

Anxiety, fear and irritability as symptoms

Anxiety and fear are taught as disorders in Chapter 6, but as phenomena they belong here, because they appear in almost every psychiatric condition. Fear is the emotional response to a definite threat that is present now: the dog in front of you, the bus that swerves towards you. Anxiety is the response to a threat that is anticipated, uncertain or vague: a diffuse, unpleasant apprehension about what might happen, with bodily arousal (a racing heart, tight muscles, a dry mouth) and a mind that keeps scanning for danger. When anxiety is attached to a particular object or situation, it is situational or phobic; when it is not attached to anything in particular, it is called free-floating anxiety, the restless, pervasive worry about everything and nothing that marks generalised anxiety disorder (Chapter 6). A panic attack is different again: in DSM-5-TR's words an abrupt surge of intense fear or intense discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes, with palpitations, breathlessness, trembling and a fear of dying or losing control (Section 6.2.1).

Anxiety as a symptom is just as common outside the anxiety disorders. It accompanies most depressions; it is the emotional core of a persecutory delusion (the man who believes he is being poisoned is, above all, frightened); it marks alcohol and benzodiazepine withdrawal, hyperthyroidism and stimulant intoxication; and it can be the first sign of a delirium. The anxious affect of a person with generalised anxiety disorder (a strained face, restless hands, a tense voice that keeps returning to "what if") is a good example of a mood-congruent affect, and like the mood-congruent affect of depression it is a distractor in questions about pseudobulbar affect. Irritability, described with the moods in Section 2.8.2, completes the morbid emotional states: it is a lowered threshold for anger, common in mania, in depression among the young, in post-traumatic stress disorder, in dementia and in hypoglycaemia, and it must be judged against the person's usual temperament.

Exam trap

Distractors in anhedonia questions borrow from this territory. An excessive fear of social situations is social anxiety disorder (Section 6.3.6). A pervasive fear of open spaces is a description of agoraphobia, although DSM-5-TR's agoraphobia is fear of at least two of five kinds of situation, of which open spaces are only one (Section 6.2.6). Neither is anhedonia. Free-floating anxiety is attached to nothing in particular; phobic anxiety is attached to a specific object or situation.

Anhedonia: when pleasure goes

Of all the changes in emotion that depression brings, the one people find hardest to explain to their families is not sadness but the disappearance of pleasure. Preeti could describe it exactly. Singing had been the centre of her life since she was six; now she sat at the harmonium and felt nothing. Festival preparations, which she used to love, felt like a list of chores. When her daughter won the class prize, she knew she ought to feel proud and could not. The French psychologist Théodule-Armand Ribot (1839 to 1916) gave this loss its name in 1896 (Book 2, Section 2.4.6 records the attribution). He coined anhedonia (Greek an, "without", and hēdonē, "pleasure") as a counterpart to analgesia: as analgesia is insensitivity to pain, anhedonia is insensitivity to pleasure.

Today anhedonia means the inability to experience pleasure, or the loss of interest, in activities that were previously enjoyed. Notice the two halves of that definition. One is the loss of liking (the music no longer feels good when it is playing); the other is the loss of wanting (there is no pull towards the harmonium in the first place). DSM-5-TR's second criterion for a major depressive episode joins them: "markedly diminished interest or pleasure in all, or almost all, activities most of the day, nearly every day", as shown either by what the person says or by what others observe. It is one of the two gateway symptoms of the episode, the other being depressed mood, and at least one of the two must be present (Section 5.2.2). DSM-5-TR adds that some diminished interest or pleasure is nearly always present in a depressive episode, that people describe "not caring anymore", and that families notice the abandoned hobby before the patient admits to it. In melancholia the loss is near-complete, not merely a reduction.

The two halves of pleasure can come apart, and that separation explains a puzzle about schizophrenia. People with schizophrenia report enjoying a pleasant activity while it is happening about as much as anyone else, yet they seek out such activities far less often. David Gard, Ann Kring and their colleagues (2007) resolved this by distinguishing consummatory pleasure, the enjoyment felt in the moment, from anticipatory pleasure, the pleasure of looking forward to something, which is what motivates us to go and get it. Their patients showed a deficit in anticipatory but not consummatory pleasure. Section 4.3.2 tells that study in full. In severe depression, by contrast, the enjoyment of the moment is usually lost as well, which is part of what makes melancholia so bleak.

Where else is anhedonia found? It is one of the five negative symptoms of schizophrenia (Section 4.3.2), and in 1962 the American psychologist Paul Everett Meehl (1920 to 2003) made a constitutional weakness in the capacity for pleasure one of the four core signs of schizotypy (the personality organisation that, in his model, grows out of an inherited neural weakness and can lead on to schizophrenia), alongside cognitive slippage, aversiveness to other people and ambivalence. It is a common and distressing part of withdrawal from stimulants (DSM-5-TR notes that anhedonia and craving are often present although they are not among the criteria) and of the protracted phase of opioid withdrawal, which can last weeks to months. It is reported in Parkinson's disease, alongside the apathy that DSM-5-TR lists among its frequent features, and it appears in the alternative personality model of DSM-5-TR as a trait facet of detachment. Rating scales such as the Snaith-Hamilton Pleasure Scale measure it, and they belong to the Assessment book.

Two clinical facts give anhedonia its weight. First, DSM-5-TR notes that anhedonia has a particularly strong association with suicidal ideation, so the person who says "nothing gives me any pleasure any more" should always be asked about thoughts of death (Book 2, Section 2.7 and Section 5.8). Second, anhedonia helps to separate a depressive illness from demoralisation, the understandable reaction of a person with a chronic medical illness who feels helpless, incompetent and ready to give up. DSM-5-TR points out that the demoralised person usually still finds pleasure in meaningful activities and can have moments of happiness, whereas anhedonia is typical of a depressive disorder. A man on a dialysis unit who feels hopeless about his kidneys but still lights up when his grandchildren visit is demoralised; the man who feels nothing at all when they arrive may be depressed.

Clinical link

Families often read anhedonia as ingratitude or laziness ("we took her to the temple festival and she just sat there"). Explaining that the capacity for pleasure has itself been switched off by the illness, and that it usually returns as the depression lifts, relieves guilt on both sides. Ask about it directly and concretely: "What did you used to enjoy? When did you last do it? How did it feel?" A vague "Do you enjoy things?" invites a polite "yes".

Exam trap

Anhedonia is the inability to experience pleasure or interest in activities once enjoyed (Ribot, 1896). Every other option in the classic questions is a different phenomenon: a reduced need for sleep is a feature of mania (Section 5.4.1); an excessive fear of social situations is social anxiety disorder (6.3.6); a pervasive fear of open spaces is agoraphobia (6.2.6); a loss of contact with reality is psychosis (Section 2.5); a period of abnormally elevated mood is mania or hypomania; excessive guilt belongs to the content of thought (2.5); psychomotor agitation is a motor sign (2.9); difficulty concentrating is a cognitive symptom (2.2). All of these can occur in the same depressive episode, but only one of them is anhedonia.

Apathy, alexithymia, ambivalence, shallowness and coldness

Several other emotional signs are easily muddled with anhedonia, because each involves something missing. The first is apathy, a lack of feeling, interest and concern, in which the person is not distressed by his own indifference. The American psychiatrist Robert S. Marin argued in 1991 that apathy is, at heart, a loss of motivation, and he separated the symptom from the syndrome: apathy is a symptom when the loss of motivation is explained by something else (a depressed mood, an intellectual impairment, a lowered level of consciousness), and a syndrome when the loss of motivation is primary and cannot be explained that way. DSM-5-TR describes the apathy of neurocognitive disorders in similar terms, as diminished motivation and reduced goal-directed behaviour accompanied by decreased emotional responsiveness, common in Alzheimer's disease and often prominent in frontotemporal degeneration, where "apathy or inertia" is one of the behavioural criteria. Apathy is also found in Parkinson's disease, in severe depression and in chronic schizophrenia. The disorders of will that sit beside it, avolition and abulia, are taught in Section 2.9.

The second is alexithymia, a word coined by the Greek-born American psychiatrist Peter Emanuel Sifneos (1920 to 2008) and usually dated to his 1973 paper "The prevalence of 'alexithymic' characteristics in psychosomatic patients" (some sources date the term to 1972). The word is built from Greek roots meaning "no words for feelings". A person with marked alexithymia has difficulty identifying what he feels, difficulty describing it to others and difficulty telling a feeling apart from the bodily sensations that go with it; his thinking is turned outwards, towards concrete facts and events, with a thin inner life of fantasy. Picture a 45-year-old factory supervisor sent by the cardiologist because of chest pain with a normal angiogram. Asked how he felt when his only son left for a job in Dubai, he looks puzzled: "What is there to feel? He has a good job. That week my BP was high and I had acidity." Sifneos first met this style among psychosomatic patients: they would list every ache and pulse rate, yet fall silent when asked what they had felt.

Alexithymia is a dimension on which everyone can be placed, not a diagnosis. It is commoner in somatic symptom disorder and the other conditions in which distress is expressed through the body (Section 7.7), in autism spectrum disorder, in some people with post-traumatic stress disorder, and in eating and substance use disorders, but many healthy people score high on it. The questionnaire most often used to rate it, the Toronto Alexithymia Scale, belongs to the Assessment book. One caution matters in India: a person who speaks of distress through the body because that is the respectable language of suffering in his family or community is not necessarily alexithymic, and a questionnaire translated without care can mistake a cultural idiom for a deficit.

The third is ambivalence, a word introduced by the Swiss psychiatrist Eugen Bleuler (1857 to 1939) in a lecture in 1910 and placed in his 1911 monograph among the fundamental symptoms of schizophrenia, the "four As" of Section 4.1.4. Ambivalence is the simultaneous presence of opposite feelings, wishes or ideas towards the same object: loving and hating the same person at the same moment, wanting and not wanting the same thing. Bleuler distinguished three forms: an emotional (affective) ambivalence, as in a husband who both loves and hates his wife; a volitional ambivalence, an inability to decide for or against an action, which shows itself in the catatonic sign of ambitendency (Section 2.9); and an intellectual ambivalence, in which an idea and its contradiction are asserted together, as when a patient insists in one breath that his food is poisoned and that it is perfectly safe. Everyone feels mixed feelings, about leaving home or about a marriage; what Bleuler meant was something stronger, in which the opposite feelings coexist without being weighed, resolved or even noticed as contradictory, and can paralyse action. Freud took the word over for psychoanalysis, where it describes the mixture of love and hostility towards the same person (psychoanalysis is taught in Book 2, Chapter 3). Whether a feeling is accepted as one's own or resisted as alien, its ego-syntonic or ego-dystonic quality, is a different distinction, taught in Section 2.1.

Finally, two older descriptive words describe the depth and warmth of feeling. Shallow affect is emotion that is shown on the surface without any sense of depth or conviction behind it: it comes and goes quickly and leaves no mark, so that the observer does not quite believe it. It was a classic feature of the hebephrenic form of schizophrenia, it is close to the "rapidly shifting and shallow expression of emotions" of histrionic personality disorder (Chapter 11), and DSM-5-TR uses "shallow or deficient affect" as one of the features of conduct disorder with limited prosocial emotions, in which emotions seem to be switched on and off or displayed to manipulate others. Emotional coldness is a lack of warmth and tender feeling towards other people. DSM-5-TR includes "emotional coldness, detachment, or flattened affectivity" among the criteria for schizoid personality disorder, and coldness without remorse is part of the callousness of psychopathy (Chapter 11).

Exam trap

Alexithymia (Sifneos, 1973) is having no words for one's feelings; anhedonia (Ribot, 1896) is having no pleasure. They are not the same, and neither is blunted affect, which is reduced outward expression. Apathy is a lack of feeling, interest and concern, a loss of motivation (Marin, 1991). Ambivalence (Bleuler, one of his four As) means holding contrary feelings about one and the same object at once; its volitional form is ambitendency. Alexithymia is a trait dimension, not a DSM-5-TR diagnosis.

Affect and its dimensions

Mood is described mainly by its quality and its course. Affect, because it is observed, has to be described along several dimensions at once, much as a doctor describes a pulse by its rate, rhythm, volume and character. Book 2, Section 2.4.7 shows how each dimension is recorded during an interview. Here the aim is to see the dimensions as a system, because examiners test exactly that: which descriptors belong to affect, and which do not. Figure 2.13 sets them out as the spokes of a wheel.

Take the spokes in turn. Quality of affect names the emotion on display: sadness, anxiety, anger, cheerfulness, fear, perplexity, suspicion. Range of affect is how many different emotions appear as the conversation moves; a broad or full range moves naturally from seriousness to worry to amusement. Intensity is how strongly emotion is shown. At one end lies heightened or exaggerated affect, more emotion than the subject warrants; at the other lies a ladder of reduction that Book 2, Section 2.4.7 draws in its Figure 2.3. Restricted affect (also called constricted affect) is a mild reduction in range and intensity; blunted affect is a marked loss of intensity, a face that barely moves and a voice without colour even when the subject matters; flat affect is the end of the ladder, where virtually no emotion can be seen at all. Reactivity (or mobility) is whether the affect moves with the conversation, softening at a painful subject and brightening at a pleasant one; an affect that does not budge, whether the talk turns to a death or a wedding, is fixed or unreactive, as in Preeti's melancholia. Appropriateness (or congruence) asks whether the emotion fits its occasion: the topic of the moment and the setting. Stability is whether the affect changes in step with events or swings abruptly. Communicability is whether the emotion reaches the observer at all: whether you can feel with the patient.

Inappropriate affect, also called incongruous affect, is emotion that clashes with what is being said or with where the person is: a young man who smiles broadly while describing how his father beat him, a woman who looks furious while saying that she is perfectly happy. Its place in schizophrenia, and the way ICD-10 groups it with blunting among the negative symptoms, are taught in Section 4.2.5, along with the caution that a person who suddenly laughs may be answering a voice. Indian examinations use two older European words here that DSM-5-TR does not use, so treat them as exam vocabulary, as Book 2 advises. Parathymia is the expression of an emotion opposite to the one the circumstances call for, such as laughing at news of a death; paramimia is a disagreement among the channels of expression themselves, such as a smiling mouth with a tearful voice.

Communicability deserves a word of history, because it gave rise to one of the strangest ideas in psychiatry. In 1941 the Dutch psychiatrist Henricus Cornelius Rümke (1893 to 1967) published a paper on "the nuclear symptom of schizophrenia and the 'praecox feeling'". The praecox feeling (from dementia praecox, the old name for schizophrenia) is the examiner's sense, often in the first minutes of an interview, that he cannot make emotional contact with the person in front of him: a peculiar strangeness, as if an invisible pane of glass stood between them. Rümke thought that this intuition captured the core of the illness better than any list of symptoms, though he limited its use to acute phases. Later work found it hard to standardise, and empirical studies gave inconclusive results, so today it is not a diagnostic criterion. It survives as a cue: when you cannot feel your way into a patient's emotional world, look harder for the reason, which may be schizophrenia, but may equally be fear, a language gap, an antipsychotic drug or your own fatigue.

That last point applies to blunting too. Antipsychotic-induced parkinsonism, the slowing of severe depression and cultural rules about showing emotion to a stranger can each mimic blunted affect, as Book 2, Section 2.4.7 and Section 4.3.2 explain. A final mismatch is worth one line here: la belle indifférence, an odd calm in a person who has, for example, suddenly lost the use of a leg, long associated with conversion disorder, which DSM-5-TR now says is not specific and should not be used to make the diagnosis (Sections 7.1 and 7.8.7). Table 2.22 gathers the vocabulary of affect with the disorders in which each term is used.

Table 2.22 The vocabulary of affect, the dimension each term describes, and where it is typically found.
TermDimensionWhat the observer seesTypical disorders
Broad (full)RangeMany emotions, each matching the talkHealth
Restricted (constricted)Range, intensityMildly reduced variety and strengthDepression, anxiety, early schizophrenia, guardedness
BluntedIntensitySeverely reduced expressionSchizophrenia (negative symptom), severe depression, drug parkinsonism
FlatIntensityAlmost no expression at allChronic schizophrenia, severe parkinsonism, catatonia
Heightened (exaggerated)IntensityMore emotion than the subject warrantsMania, histrionic personality, intoxication
ShallowIntensity, communicabilityEmotion shown without depth or convictionHebephrenic schizophrenia, histrionic personality, psychopathy
Fixed (unreactive)ReactivityUnchanged whatever is discussedMelancholic depression, chronic schizophrenia
Inappropriate (incongruous)AppropriatenessEmotion does not fit content or situation; parathymia, paramimiaSchizophrenia, frontal lobe disease
LabileStabilitySudden swings far larger than events warrantMania, delirium, dementia, brain injury, personality disorder
PseudobulbarStability, appropriatenessBrief involuntary laughing or crying unrelated to the felt moodStroke, MS, ALS, brain injury, dementia
Exam trap

Asked which factor does not describe the outward, objective expression of immediate emotion, choose persistence of mood. Quality, range and reactivity are dimensions of affect; persistence (how long a state lasts) describes mood. One option is worded "reactivity of mood", which looks as if it too should be excluded. It is a deliberate trap: reactivity is normally listed as a dimension of affect, and the "mood reactivity" of atypical depression (Section 2.8.2) is a different use of the word, so the key, persistence, stands. Restricted, blunted and flat form a ladder of increasing severity; inappropriate is a problem of fit, labile a problem of stability.

Lability, emotional incontinence and pseudobulbar affect

Return to Jagdish, sobbing at the receptionist's "good morning". His affect was unstable, but in a very particular way, and to understand it you need to place it at the far end of a range of instabilities. Labile affect is affect that lurches from one emotion to another faster and further than the conversation can explain: tears one moment, laughter the next. In lability as usually meant, the person feels each emotion as it comes, even if it comes too fast. Lability is seen in mania (DSM-5-TR's alternation among euphoria, dysphoria and irritability), in delirium, dementia and head injury, in intoxication, and in some personality disorders. Two personality patterns are easily confused with it and are taught in Chapter 11: the affective instability of borderline personality disorder, which DSM-5-TR attributes to a marked reactivity of mood with intense episodes of dysphoria, irritability or anxiety lasting a few hours, and the "rapidly shifting and shallow expression of emotions" of histrionic personality disorder, in which emotion is displayed dramatically and often for an audience.

Push lability far enough and control over expression is lost altogether. Older British and Indian textbooks call this emotional incontinence, a loss of control over the expression of emotion and the extreme form of emotional lability, and in British and Indian usage the term is often used for the same phenomenon that neurologists now call pseudobulbar affect (PBA). Other names for it are pathological laughing and crying and involuntary emotional expression disorder. Pseudobulbar affect is a neurological disorder of emotional expression. Its episodes are sudden, brief (seconds to a minute or two), involuntary bursts of crying or laughing that are out of proportion to the felt mood or flatly contrary to it, often set off by trivial or emotionally neutral stimuli (a greeting, a television advertisement, a question about breakfast), ending as abruptly as they began and leaving the person embarrassed. Jagdish's laughter at his cousin's funeral was the classic incongruent episode. Crucially, the mood between and beneath the episodes may be quite normal: Jagdish was looking forward to his grandson's wedding, slept and ate well, and enjoyed his days.

Why "pseudobulbar"? The bulb is the old name for the medulla, the lowest part of the brain stem, which contains the motor nuclei for the muscles of the face, tongue, throat and voice. A bulbar palsy is weakness from damage to those nuclei themselves. A pseudobulbar palsy produces similar difficulties (slurred speech, trouble swallowing, a brisk jaw jerk) from damage higher up, to the corticobulbar pathways that carry commands from the cerebral cortex down to the bulb, usually on both sides. The emotional outbursts often accompany that palsy, hence the name. DSM-5-TR mentions a pseudobulbar syndrome as one of the physical signs that point to cerebrovascular disease in vascular neurocognitive disorder, and pseudobulbar signs in progressive supranuclear palsy. Pseudobulbar affect occurs after strokes (especially multiple or bilateral ones), in multiple sclerosis, in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS, motor neuron disease, where some series find it in up to half of patients), after traumatic brain injury, and in Alzheimer's disease, other dementias and Parkinson's disease.

Key study: Where does the uncontrollable laughter come from? (Wilson, 1924; Parvizi and colleagues, 2001)

In 1924 the British neurologist Samuel Alexander Kinnier Wilson (1878 to 1937), whose name is also on Wilson's disease, analysed pathological laughing and crying in a paper in his series "Some problems in neurology". He proposed that the brain stem holds centres that coordinate the facial, vocal and breathing movements of laughing and crying, and that these centres are normally held in check by pathways descending from the cerebral cortex. Damage those corticobulbar pathways on both sides, and the centres are released: they fire in response to stimuli that would never have moved the person before. This "release" or "disinhibition" account explained why the condition travels with pseudobulbar palsy, multiple strokes, ALS and multiple sclerosis, and it remained the accepted view for most of the century.

In 2001 Josef Parvizi, Antonio Damasio and their colleagues at the University of Iowa reported a patient whose lesions lay instead in the cerebro-ponto-cerebellar pathways, the routes that carry information from the cortex through the pons to the cerebellum. They proposed that the cerebellum normally adjusts the execution of laughter or crying to the cognitive and social context; deprived of full information about that context, it produces responses that are inadequate and even chaotic. Their paper drew the conclusion that matters most for psychopathology: pathological laughing and crying is a disorder of emotional expression rather than a primary disturbance of feeling, and so is distinct from the mood disorders, in which laughter and crying come with real happiness or sadness. The two accounts may well be complementary, and the evidence for the cerebellar view still rests largely on single cases and small series.

Because pseudobulbar affect looks so much like emotion, it is regularly mistaken for something else, and the mistakes lead to the wrong treatment. Its most common mimic is depression. The difference lies in the relation between expression and feeling: in depression the tears match a low mood that is present most of the day and comes with the other symptoms of a depressive episode, whereas in PBA the tears are brief, stereotyped, provoked by trivia and disowned ("these are not my feelings"). The two can coexist, since depression after a stroke is common, so the clinician asks about the mood between the episodes. The labile affect of mania differs because the person feels the shifting emotions and the mood is elevated or irritable, with the other features of mania. Histrionic display differs because the emotions are felt or performed for an effect, shift with the audience and belong to a lifelong pattern of personality, not to a recent brain lesion. Table 2.23 sets the four side by side.

Table 2.23 Pseudobulbar affect compared with the three presentations most often mistaken for it.
FeaturePseudobulbar affectDepressionLabile affect of maniaHistrionic display
Mood underneathOften normalPersistently lowElevated or irritableVariable, often normal
Expression and feelingDissociated: expression not feltMatchedMatched but fast-changingFelt or performed for effect
EpisodesSudden, seconds to minutes, stereotyped, trivial triggersTearfulness through the dayRapid swings over minutes to hoursDramatic, shallow, audience-related
SettingStroke, MS, ALS, brain injury, dementiaDepressive disordersBipolar disorderHistrionic personality disorder

The first treatment approved for pseudobulbar affect came in October 2010, when the United States Food and Drug Administration approved a capsule combining dextromethorphan (a drug long used as a cough suppressant, which acts on brain glutamate and sigma receptors) with a very low dose of quinidine. The quinidine is there for a pharmacokinetic reason: it blocks the liver enzyme (CYP2D6) that would otherwise break dextromethorphan down within hours, so that useful levels reach the brain. In the pivotal 12-week trial reported by Erik Pioro and colleagues in 2010, 326 patients with ALS or multiple sclerosis and clinically significant PBA were randomised to one of two doses of the combination or to placebo; the daily rate of episodes was about 47 to 49 percent lower on either dose than on placebo, and both doses were well tolerated. Before and alongside it, clinicians have used tricyclic antidepressants and SSRIs off-label, with more limited evidence. Severity can be rated with the Center for Neurologic Study Lability Scale, an instrument for the Assessment book. As important as any drug is the explanation: once Jagdish's family understood that his tears were a short circuit, not grief, they stopped tiptoeing around him, and he stopped avoiding weddings.

Two further organic emotional signs complete the picture. The German-American neurologist and psychiatrist Kurt Goldstein (1878 to 1965), who worked with brain-injured soldiers during and after the First World War, described the catastrophic reaction: a sudden storm of anxiety, distress, tears or anger when a person with brain damage is faced with a task he can no longer perform, as when a man with aphasia is pressed to name objects he cannot name. Ahuja lists it among the additional features of dementia, where an overtaxed patient may fly into a sudden rage. In 1972 the Italian neuropsychologist Guido Gainotti reported that catastrophic reactions were commoner after left-hemisphere lesions, while an "indifference reaction", with unconcern, joking and minimising of the deficit, was commoner after right-hemisphere lesions; DSM-5-TR similarly notes that right hemisphere strokes can bring personality change with neglect and anosognosia (Section 2.10). The practical lesson is that an outburst during cognitive testing may be a sign of the brain disease being tested, not of a difficult personality.

Clinical link

Before you write "depressed" for a tearful stroke patient, ask three questions. How long does each bout of crying last, and what sets it off? Does the crying match what he feels inside? How is his mood between the bouts? Brief, trivially triggered, disowned outbursts with a reasonable mood in between point to pseudobulbar affect; a persistently low mood with loss of interest points to post-stroke depression; both can be present. The distinction decides whether the family hears "he is grieving" or "his brain's brakes on expression are damaged", and whether the dose of an antidepressant is pushed up needlessly.

Exam trap

Pseudobulbar affect means short, uncontrollable bursts of laughing or crying that are too big for, or contrary to, what the person feels, occurring in neurological disease: stroke, multiple sclerosis, ALS, brain injury and dementia. It is not the anxious affect of GAD, not the blunted or restricted affect of schizophrenia, not the mood-congruent affect of depression and not the histrionic emotional shifts of a personality disorder. In PBA expression is dissociated from feeling; in all four distractors expression and feeling go together (or, in histrionic display, the emotion is shown for an effect). Treatment: dextromethorphan with quinidine (approved in the United States in 2010), or SSRIs and tricyclics off-label. Labile affect is the wider category; emotional incontinence is its extreme.

2.9 Disorders of Movement and WillLower yield · 1 Q

Stand at the entrance of a busy psychiatric ward for ten minutes and, before anyone has said a word, you will already have seen a great deal of psychopathology. In one corner a woman sits so still that the tea beside her has grown a skin; in another a young man paces the length of the corridor, turns, and paces back, rubbing his hands; an older man by the window raises his right hand to his ear, again and again, in exactly the same way. Most of what this chapter has described so far (hallucinations, delusions, the loss of pleasure, the feeling of unreality) is invisible and has to be inferred from what people say. Movement is the great exception. It can be watched, filmed, timed and agreed upon by two observers, and that makes the motor signs some of the most reliable findings in the whole of psychiatry. This section describes them: the slowing and the agitation of the whole body, the catatonic signs, the other repeated and abnormal movements, and finally the disorders of the will that lies behind movement.

Why movement belongs to psychopathology

Why should a book about the mind spend so long on the body's movements? Because a voluntary movement is the last link in a chain that begins in the mind. A wish becomes an intention, the intention becomes a decision, and the decision becomes an act. The faculty that turns an intention into action is the will, or volition. Older psychology called the striving, acting side of the mind conation (from the Latin conari, "to try"), and psychiatry textbooks still divide mental life into the classic three domains: cognition (thought), conation (action) and affect (feeling). When this chain is disturbed, the disturbance shows itself in movement: too little of it, too much of it, the wrong kind of it, or movement that seems to have escaped from the person's control. The word psychomotor captures the link: in many mental states the speed of the body rises and falls with the speed of the mind.

Before any movement is called psychiatric, however, one question must be asked: could a drug be causing it? Antipsychotic medicines in particular produce four movement disorders that mimic psychopathology, and Book 2, Section 2.4.4 and Section 4.10.4 teach them in the order in which they usually appear. An acute dystonia is a sudden, painful, sustained muscle spasm (the neck twisted, the eyes rolled upwards, the jaw locked) within hours or days of starting the drug. Akathisia, usually within days or weeks, is a torment of restlessness: the person cannot stay seated and keeps shifting his weight from foot to foot. Drug-induced parkinsonism brings tremor, rigidity, slowness and a mask-like face over the first weeks. Tardive dyskinesia appears after months or years as chewing, lip-smacking and tongue-rolling movements that the person cannot stop. Each has a psychiatric double: dystonia can look like catatonic posturing, akathisia like agitation, parkinsonism like retardation or blunted affect, and tardive dyskinesia like grimacing or tics. Keep this list in mind through everything that follows.

Psychomotor retardation and psychomotor agitation

Preeti, the schoolteacher with melancholic depression of Section 2.8, showed the commonest change of all. Every answer she gave came after a pause of several seconds, as if the question had to travel a long way to reach her. Her voice was low and monotonous, she hardly moved her hands as she spoke, she sat slumped with her eyes on the floor, and when she stood up to leave, the movement seemed to take enormous effort. This is psychomotor retardation: a general slowing of movement, speech and thought, with a long delay before responses, few spontaneous gestures, a soft and unvaried voice, a slow gait and an air of heaviness about everything. From the inside, people describe it as moving through deep water or wet sand, and as thinking with a mind full of glue (the slowing of thought itself is described in Section 2.4).

DSM-5-TR is careful about one point. Its criterion for a major depressive episode counts psychomotor agitation or retardation only when it is "observable by others, not merely subjective feelings of restlessness or being slowed down". The reason is reliability: a feeling of being slowed is common in many states, but a slowing that a stranger can see is a firmer sign, and in melancholia DSM-5-TR notes that psychomotor changes are nearly always present and observable by others. Retardation is the motor face of melancholic and bipolar depression, and at its extreme it becomes depressive stupor (Section 2.2). It is also seen in hypothyroidism, in Parkinson's disease and drug-induced parkinsonism (where neurologists call the slowing bradykinesia), with sedative drugs, in the negative syndrome of schizophrenia and in the hypoactive form of delirium (Section 2.2), and every one of these must be considered before the slowing is put down to depression.

At the other pole lies psychomotor agitation: restless overactivity that serves no purpose and is pushed along by tension inside. A 70-year-old retired headmaster with agitated depression cannot sit for more than a minute. He wrings his hands, rubs his thighs, picks at the edge of his kurta, gets up, walks to the door, comes back and follows the nurse down the corridor asking, "What will happen to me? What will happen to my family?" The movements achieve nothing and are not meant to; they discharge a tension that he feels as unbearable. Agitation is typical of agitated depression, which is especially common in later life, and it appears in severe anxiety, in delirium and dementia, in stimulant intoxication and in alcohol and sedative withdrawal. Catatonic excitement, one of the catatonic signs below, is a further form, distinguished by being uninfluenced by anything in the surroundings.

Agitation has to be told apart from three other kinds of overactivity. The first is akathisia, the drug-induced restlessness above, which the person feels mainly in the legs and describes as an urge to keep moving; raising the dose of the drug that causes it makes it worse, which is why it is so dangerous to mistake it for agitation. The second is the overactivity of mania. When Ashok, the young engineer of Section 2.8.3, was elated, he was constantly on the move, but his activity was purposeful: he was starting projects, making calls, rearranging his flat and inviting the neighbours to a party. It was goal-directed but scattered, shifting with every new thing that caught his attention, so that he started ten tasks and finished none. DSM-5-TR captures both forms in one manic criterion, an "increase in goal-directed activity" or psychomotor agitation, which it glosses as purposeless, non-goal-directed activity. The third is the hyperactivity of attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, a developmental pattern of fidgeting, restlessness and difficulty staying seated that dates from childhood and is present across settings, not an episode (Chapter 3). The hyperactive and hypoactive forms of delirium, finally, are the motor faces of a disturbance of consciousness, described in Section 2.2.

Exam trap

Psychomotor agitation is a motor sign, not a disorder of thought, mood or perception, and in DSM-5-TR it counts only when observable by others. Agitation is purposeless and driven by inner tension; manic overactivity is goal-directed but distractible; ADHD hyperactivity is a lifelong developmental pattern. Akathisia is a drug side effect that mimics agitation. Retardation also occurs in hypothyroidism and parkinsonism, so slowness alone does not mean depression.

Catatonia: the syndrome in brief

The most dramatic of all the motor syndromes has a history that Book 2 and Chapter 4 tell in full, so it is recapped here in a paragraph. The German psychiatrist Karl Ludwig Kahlbaum (1828 to 1899) named catatonia in an 1874 monograph that also called it "tension insanity" (Spannungsirresein), an illness of the muscles and the will in which stupor, mutism, rigid postures and imitation came and went. Emil Kraepelin then absorbed it into dementia praecox, and generations of students learned it as one variety of schizophrenia until DSM-5 (2013) broke that link. DSM-5-TR recognises catatonia associated with another mental disorder, used as a specifier with psychotic, bipolar, depressive, neurodevelopmental and other disorders; catatonic disorder due to another medical condition; and unspecified catatonia. The rule is three or more of twelve signs. Treatment begins with a benzodiazepine, the lorazepam challenge being both a test and a first dose, and turns to electroconvulsive therapy when benzodiazepines fail or in malignant catatonia. Book 2, Section 2.4.4 (Table 2.5) shows how the signs are elicited at the bedside, Section 4.5.7 (Table 4.21) explains catatonia across the disorders and its treatment, and Figure 4.5 in Section 4.2.6 separates the signs that are most often confused.

What does this section add? It treats each sign as a phenomenon in its own right, a thing that can appear outside catatonia, with its own differential diagnosis. DSM-5-TR itself suggests the best way to organise them, by what has happened to movement. Motor activity may be decreased, severely (stupor) or moderately (catalepsy and waxy flexibility). Engagement with the examiner may be decreased, severely (mutism) or moderately (negativism). And motor activity may be excessive and peculiar, either complex (stereotypy) or simple (agitation), including the echoing of speech and movement. The same person can swing between the decreased and the excessive forms, which is one reason the syndrome confuses clinicians. DSM-5-TR also blames teaching for part of the problem: by dwelling on rare and spectacular signs such as waxy flexibility, it has left clinicians less able to recognise catatonia at all. In practice the syndrome is more often a silent patient who stares, resists and will not eat than a statue holding an arm in the air. Table 2.24 gives each sign a fresh example and lists where else it is found.

Table 2.24 The DSM-5-TR catatonic signs sorted by what has happened to movement, each with an example and the settings in which the same phenomenon occurs outside catatonia (three or more signs are needed for the syndrome).
GroupSignAn exampleAlso found in
Decreased movementStuporLies motionless all day, eyes open, unresponsive; later recalls what the doctors saidSevere depression, dissociation, organic states (Section 2.2)
Decreased movementCatalepsyThe arm the nurse lifted to check a cannula stays lifted for minutesHypnosis; not to be confused with cataplexy
Decreased movementWaxy flexibilityBending the elbow meets a slight, even resistance, like bending a thick roll of plasticineRare outside catatonia
Decreased movementPosturingSits for hours with one hand cupped at the ear, as if on a telephoneDrug-induced dystonia, functional disorders
Decreased engagementMutismSpeaks to no one for three days, though not aphasicSevere depression, dissociation, selective mutism, akinetic mutism
Decreased engagementNegativismTurns her face away whenever the spoon comes nearFrontal disease and dementia (as Gegenhalten)
Excessive, peculiarMannerismLifts every spoonful in a slow, high arc before eating itSchizophrenia
Excessive, peculiarStereotypyRubs the same spot on the bed rail for an hourAutism, intellectual disability, stereotypic movement disorder
Excessive, peculiarAgitationRuns shouting up and down the ward, unaffected by anything said to himMania and delirium (but these respond to surroundings)
Excessive, peculiarGrimacingHolds the mouth pulled sideways in a fixed, odd expressionTics, tardive dyskinesia
Echo phenomenaEcholaliaA visitor says "very hot today"; the patient repeats "very hot today"Autism, Tourette's disorder, dementia, transcortical aphasia, latah
Echo phenomenaEchopraxiaScratches his own head each time the doctor scratches hisTourette's disorder, frontal lobe damage, latah
Key study: Catatonia is not a schizophrenic sign (Abrams and Taylor, 1976)

For a century after Kahlbaum, most psychiatrists who saw a catatonic patient wrote "catatonic schizophrenia" almost automatically. The American psychiatrists Richard Abrams and Michael Alan Taylor decided to test the habit. Over 14 months they studied every patient admitted to two inpatient units of a municipal hospital who showed one or more of eight catatonic signs: mutism, stereotypy, posturing, catalepsy, automatic obedience, negativism, echolalia or echopraxia, and stupor. Each of the 55 patients they found was then diagnosed with explicit research criteria. Only four met their criteria for schizophrenia. More than two-thirds had a diagnosable affective disorder, usually mania. The motor signs were spread evenly across the diagnostic groups, so that no sign marked any particular illness, and the number and type of signs did not predict the short-term outcome; two-thirds of the whole sample were markedly improved or in remission by discharge.

The study had limits: a single sign was enough for entry, and the research criteria of the time defined schizophrenia narrowly. But its message, repeated by later work, was that catatonia is a syndrome that cuts across diagnoses and is especially common in mood disorders. DSM-5-TR reaches the same verdict: although up to 35 percent of people with schizophrenia show catatonia, most people with catatonia have a depressive or bipolar disorder.

The catatonic signs as phenomena, and where else they occur

Start with the pair that examiners ask about most. Echolalia is the automatic repetition of words or phrases that another person has just spoken; echopraxia is the automatic imitation of another person's movements. Both are echo phenomena: the person's own speech or action has been replaced by a copy of someone else's, without any apparent intention to mock, play or communicate. They are among the twelve catatonic signs, and in a question that asks which syndrome they are "most associated with", catatonia is the answer. Yet they also appear in at least five other settings, and a good clinician knows them all. In autism spectrum disorder, echolalia is part of the repetitive use of speech that DSM-5-TR describes as "the delayed or immediate parroting of heard words": immediate echolalia repeats what has just been said, delayed echolalia reproduces phrases heard hours or days earlier, such as a whole advertisement jingle or a line from a cartoon. In Tourette's disorder echolalia and echopraxia are complex tics, and DSM-5-TR notes that seeing a gesture or hearing a sound in another person may set off a similar tic. In dementia with severe language impairment, DSM-5-TR observes that echolalia and automatic speech typically come before mutism. In the transcortical aphasias, in which the ability to repeat is spared although spontaneous speech, comprehension or both are impaired, a patient may echo the examiner's sentences word for word (the aphasias themselves belong to the Physiological book). And in latah, a cultural syndrome reported from South-East Asia, a sudden startle sets off automatic obedience, echolalia and echopraxia. Echolalia is also a normal, passing stage in toddlers learning to talk, which is one reason it means little on its own in a young child.

Mutism is the absence, or near-absence, of speech in a person who is awake. It is taught as a disorder of speech in Section 2.4; as a catatonic sign it is the severe form of decreased engagement, and DSM-5-TR excludes it when a known aphasia explains it. Mutism also occurs in severe depression and depressive stupor, in dissociative states, as selective mutism (speech present in some settings and consistently absent in others, an anxiety disorder of childhood, Section 6.4.8), and in akinetic mutism, a neurological state of absent drive described in Section 2.2. The mute patient's eyes are the first clue: eyes that follow the examiner, resist being opened or fill with tears suggest preserved awareness.

Negativism is an apparently motiveless resistance to instructions or to being moved. In passive negativism the person simply fails to do what is asked: told to sit, he stands where he is; offered food, he keeps his mouth closed. In active negativism he does the opposite: asked to open his eyes, he screws them shut; asked to sit, he walks away. The word "motiveless" matters. A frightened patient who refuses tablets because he believes they are poison, or an angry teenager who will not answer his parents, is not negativistic in this sense, because the refusal has an understandable reason. The commonest clinical error runs in the other direction: real catatonic negativism is often misread as deliberate uncooperativeness, and the patient is labelled difficult while he quietly starves.

The signs of decreased movement have neighbours that sound alike. Catalepsy, the holding of a posture that someone else has placed, must not be confused with cataplexy, the sudden loss of muscle tone triggered by strong emotion (a burst of laughter, a surprise) without loss of consciousness, which is a symptom of narcolepsy (Chapter 8): one word holds a posture, the other drops it. Catalepsy can also be produced by suggestion in hypnosis. Posturing, a posture adopted and held by the patient himself, must be told apart from an acute dystonia, which is painful, involuntary and drug-induced. Grimacing has its own doubles: a facial grimace can be a simple motor tic, an orofacial movement of tardive dyskinesia or a catatonic sign, and only the setting and the rest of the picture tell them apart. Stereotypies, mannerisms and the other repeated acts are compared in Section 2.9.6.

Two further motor emergencies are recapped in a line each. Malignant catatonia, the lethal catatonia that Karl Stauder described in 1934, is catatonia with high fever, autonomic instability and stupor, which can be fatal without prompt treatment, usually with ECT. Neuroleptic malignant syndrome is a reaction to antipsychotic drugs with fever, severe rigidity, autonomic instability and abnormal blood tests, and it is widely seen as malignant catatonia provoked by the drug (Section 4.10.5). Both explain why clinicians avoid starting or raising antipsychotics in a patient who is still catatonic.

Exam trap

Echolalia (repeating others' words) and echopraxia (imitating others' movements) are most associated with catatonia, not with major depressive disorder, panic disorder or specific phobia. Catatonia can complicate depression (major depressive disorder with catatonia), but the echo signs point to the catatonic syndrome, not to depression as such; panic and phobia have no motor echo signs at all. The same phenomena occur outside catatonia in autism (immediate and delayed echolalia), Tourette's disorder (as complex tics), dementia, transcortical aphasia and latah, so read the options. Do not confuse palilalia (repeating one's own words, Section 2.4) with echolalia (repeating someone else's), or catalepsy (holding a placed posture) with cataplexy (sudden loss of tone in narcolepsy).

The older signs: a ward round with Nirmala

Several catatonic signs that DSM-5-TR does not list survive in British and Indian texts and in rating scales, and examiners still ask about them. They are easiest to understand in a single patient. Nirmala, a 52-year-old woman from a village near Mandya, was admitted to the district hospital two months after her husband died. For six weeks she had been severely depressed, convinced that she had brought ruin on her family; for the last four days she had stopped speaking and eating. On the morning ward round she lay on her back staring at the ceiling, neck rigid, her head lifted clear of the pillow as if an invisible cushion supported it. The nurses had already noticed that she kept this posture for hours, a classic form of posturing known as the "psychological pillow".

When the doctor said, "Nirmala-amma, please sit up", she sat up at once; when he said, "Stand", she stood; when he said, almost to himself, "Turn around", she turned, and she kept obeying every instruction mechanically, however pointless. This is automatic obedience: carrying out every command literally, regardless of its sense or consequence. Next he told her clearly, "Do not let me move your arm", and pressed lightly on her elbow with one finger. The arm rose and followed his fingertip wherever it went, like an anglepoise lamp. This is Mitgehen ("going with"), movement in any direction in response to the slightest pressure, even against instructions; it is an extreme form of Mitmachen ("doing with"), in which the examiner can place the body in any position despite being told to resist. The junior doctor then tried to bend her wrist. She resisted, and the harder he pushed the harder she resisted, with a force that exactly matched his; when he stopped, she stopped. This is Gegenhalten ("counter-holding"), opposition to passive movement with a force equal to the force applied. Neurologists call the same phenomenon paratonia and see it in frontal lobe disease and dementia, which is a reminder that several catatonic signs have neurological relatives.

The nurse held out a steel tumbler of water. Nirmala's hand moved towards it, stopped halfway, drew back, moved forward again and stopped again, over and over for several minutes, without ever taking it. This is ambitendency, the motor expression of Bleuler's volitional ambivalence (Section 2.8.6): two opposed impulses, to act and not to act, each cancelling the other, so that the movement is begun and withdrawn without any goal-directed act ever being completed. When the doctor's fingers brushed her palm, she gripped them and could not let go, even when asked; this forced grasping is a primitive grasp reflex, normal in infants, which reappears in frontal lobe disease and is included in catatonia rating scales. When the nurse said, "Take your tablets", Nirmala whispered back, "take your tablets", and then shut her eyes tightly when asked to open them: echolalia and active negativism. Her stupor, mutism and refusal of food completed the picture.

The diagnosis was major depressive disorder with catatonia. The doctor gave lorazepam 2 mg intravenously. Within the hour Nirmala asked for water and drank two glasses, and by the evening she was speaking in short sentences about her husband. Regular lorazepam was continued, the depression was treated, and electroconvulsive therapy was kept ready in case the response faded, as it is when benzodiazepines are not enough. Severity is often followed with the Bush-Francis Catatonia Rating Scale, an instrument described in the Assessment book that includes several of these older signs. Nirmala's story also shows the clinical stakes: a patient who neither eats nor drinks for days is at risk of dehydration, malnutrition, blood clots in the legs and pressure sores, so catatonia is a medical emergency as well as a psychiatric one.

Exam trap

Know the older signs by what the limb does. Mitmachen: the body can be put in any position despite instructions to resist. Mitgehen: the limb follows the lightest touch in any direction (the anglepoise lamp), an extreme of Mitmachen. Gegenhalten: passive movement is opposed with equal force (paratonia). Waxy flexibility: slight, even resistance as the limb is moved. Catalepsy: a placed posture is then held. Automatic obedience: every command is obeyed literally. Ambitendency: an action is begun and withdrawn repeatedly. The "psychological pillow" is a form of posturing. Of the signs in this box, only waxy flexibility and catalepsy are named among the twelve DSM-5-TR signs (the psychological pillow counts under posturing, which is also one of the twelve).

Mannerisms, stereotypies, tics, compulsions and other repeated acts

A repeated movement is one of the commonest things a clinician is asked to explain, and five or six quite different phenomena can produce one. The first step is to notice whether the movement has a goal. A mannerism is an odd, stilted or exaggerated version of a normal, goal-directed act, what DSM-5-TR calls an "odd, circumstantial caricature of normal actions". The act still reaches its goal, but in a strange way: a man with chronic schizophrenia who walks to the dining table on his toes with his knees raised high, as if marching in a parade, or who brings every spoonful to his mouth in a slow arc over his head. The everyday sense of the word (a habitual gesture such as twirling a moustache) is not what the psychiatric term means. A stereotypy, by contrast, has no goal at all: it is a repetitive, often rhythmic movement that serves no obvious purpose, such as rocking the body, flapping the hands, banging the head or rubbing the same spot over and over. In catatonia and chronic schizophrenia stereotypies appear as part of the motor syndrome. In autism spectrum disorder and intellectual disability they are common, and when they are severe enough to interfere with life or cause self-injury, DSM-5-TR diagnoses stereotypic movement disorder, defined by "repetitive, seemingly driven, and apparently purposeless motor behavior" (Chapter 3).

A tic is a sudden, rapid, recurrent, non-rhythmic movement or vocalisation, in DSM-5-TR's words. Tics are motor (blinking, grimacing, shrugging) or vocal (sniffing, throat clearing, grunting), and simple or complex; complex tics last longer and can look purposeful, and they include echopraxia, obscene gestures, repeating one's own words (palilalia), repeating another's (echolalia) and, in a minority, uttering obscenities (coprolalia), which DSM-5-TR notes comes out as an abrupt bark or grunt without the melody of ordinary angry speech. What marks a tic most clearly is its relation to the will. Most people with tics feel a premonitory urge, an uncomfortable local sensation before the tic, and can hold the tic back for a while, at the price of mounting tension, followed by relief when it is released. Tics are therefore neither fully voluntary nor fully involuntary, and they wax and wane over time. DSM-5-TR separates them from stereotypies: stereotypies usually begin earlier (often before the age of three), last longer (seconds to minutes), are rhythmic and repetitive in form and place, lack a premonitory urge and stop with distraction, such as hearing one's name called. The tic disorders, including Tourette's disorder (both motor and vocal tics), are taught in Chapter 3.

A compulsion is a repeated act, either visible (washing, checking, ordering, touching) or silent (counting, praying, saying words in one's head), that the person feels compelled to carry out because of an obsession or because of strict rules of his own. Its aim is to lower distress or to stop some feared event, yet it has no realistic link with that event or is plainly excessive. That is the substance of DSM-5-TR's definition, and each part of it rules something out. A compulsion is not pleasurable in itself: it relieves distress for a while and then the cycle starts again. It is usually recognised as excessive and resisted, at least early on (the obsession behind it is ego-dystonic, Section 2.1). And it is driven from inside by a thought, not by a sensory urge, although the line can blur: some people with tics feel they must repeat a tic until it feels "just right", and DSM-5-TR concedes that complex tics and compulsions can be hard to tell apart. The disorder is taught in Section 6.5.2. A culturally normal ritual is not a compulsion. A grandmother who performs her morning puja in the same order every day, lighting the lamp, offering flowers and ringing the bell, does so willingly, as part of a shared and meaningful practice, in a time the family expects, and without the dread that something terrible will happen if one step is imperfect. The same sequence becomes a compulsion only when it is driven by an obsessional fear, repeated far beyond custom, distressing, and time-consuming (DSM-5-TR's example is more than one hour a day).

Two more kinds of repetition complete the list. Habits are automatic, learned routines with little thought behind them, and the body-focused repetitive behaviours (hair pulling, skin picking, nail biting) are habits that have become harmful, often preceded by tension or boredom and followed by a sense of gratification or relief (Section 6.6.5). Perseveration of action is the continuation of an act after its goal has been reached or after the task has changed: the patient asked to draw a circle and then a square goes on drawing circles, or keeps wiping a plate that is already clean. It suggests a damaged brain, most often frontal damage or dementia; perseveration of speech and thought is described in Section 2.4. Figure 2.14 turns these distinctions into a sorting tree, and Table 2.25 compares the repeated acts feature by feature.

Table 2.25 The repeated acts compared.
ActGoalUrge or driveControl and reliefTypical disorders
MannerismA normal goal, reached in an odd wayNone reportedLooks voluntary; no reliefSchizophrenia, catatonia
StereotypyNoneOften self-soothing or pleasurableStops with distractionAutism, intellectual disability, stereotypic movement disorder, catatonia
TicNone (complex tics may look purposeful)Premonitory sensory urgeSuppressible for a while; relief afterTic disorders, Tourette's disorder
CompulsionTo prevent harm or reduce distressAn obsession or rigid ruleResisted; relieves distress, gives no pleasureObsessive-compulsive disorder
Habit or body-focused repetitive behaviourGrooming or self-soothingTension or boredomOften automatic; gratification or reliefTrichotillomania, excoriation disorder
PerseverationThe goal is already overNoneCannot shift to the next actFrontal lesions, dementia

One last group of movements looks neurological but is not. Functional (conversion) motor symptoms, such as weakness, tremor, jerks or a bizarre, lurching gait that makes standing and walking look impossible even though the legs work normally in bed (the old term is astasia-abasia), are taught with functional neurological symptom disorder in Section 7.8.4. Their hallmark is inconsistency with known neurological disease, not the presence of a psychological cause.

Clinical link

Why does the label matter? Because each repeated act leads to a different treatment. Tics respond to habit reversal training and comprehensive behavioural intervention for tics and, when severe, to medication; compulsions respond to exposure and response prevention and to SSRIs (both psychological treatments are taught in Book 2); stereotypies in autism often need no treatment unless they cause injury; perseveration calls for a neurological workup; and a catatonic stereotypy may vanish with lorazepam. Asking "Do you feel an urge just before it? What would happen if you did not do it?" separates most tics from most compulsions in two questions.

Exam trap

A mannerism is a goal-directed act done oddly; a stereotypy has no goal at all. A tic is sudden, rapid, non-rhythmic, preceded by an urge and briefly suppressible; a stereotypy is rhythmic, starts earlier and stops with distraction. A compulsion answers an obsession or rigid rule and relieves distress without giving pleasure. Perseveration of action continues an act whose goal is over and points to frontal or organic disease. A culturally prescribed ritual such as a daily puja is not a compulsion unless it is driven by obsessional fear, excessive and distressing.

Disorders of will

Behind every movement described so far stands the will, and the will can fail in its own right. The commonest failure is avolition, which DSM-5-TR defines as a decrease in motivated, self-initiated, purposeful activities: such a person can spend hours in one chair, uninterested in work, friends or family life. It is one of the five negative symptoms of schizophrenia and the one most closely tied to disability (Section 4.3.2), and a lesser form is common in depression, where it lifts as the mood improves. The neurological counterpart is abulia (Greek a, "without", and boulē, "will"): a marked lack of will and initiative, with slowed responses and little spontaneous speech or movement. Jagdish, the stroke patient of Section 2.8.8, showed it after a further small stroke deep in the brain. He sat in his chair for most of the day, answered questions after a long pause in two or three words, and never started a conversation or an activity. Yet when his son said, "Papa, drink your tea", he drank it, and when he was walked to the garden, he enjoyed it. That responsiveness to prompting is typical: the machinery of action is intact, but the self-starter is broken. Abulia follows damage to the frontal lobes (especially their inner, medial surface) and to the basal ganglia, and DSM-5-TR lists it, with emotional lability, among the features commonly associated with vascular neurocognitive disorder. Its extreme is akinetic mutism, in which a wakeful person with eyes that follow the examiner neither moves nor speaks (Section 2.2).

Apathy, avolition and abulia overlap, and examiners like to see that you can separate them. Apathy is a lack of feeling, interest and concern, a disorder of emotion and motivation (Section 2.8.6). Avolition is a lack of self-initiated, goal-directed activity, the psychiatric term used above all for the negative syndrome of schizophrenia. Abulia is the neurological term for a severe loss of will and initiative with slowness and poverty of spontaneous action, lying on a continuum that ends in akinetic mutism. Families often call all three "laziness"; none of them is a choice.

Three disorders of will have already appeared among the catatonic signs. Ambitendency is the volitional form of ambivalence, the hand that reaches and withdraws. Negativism is the will turned automatically against every instruction. Automatic obedience is its mirror image, the will surrendered to every instruction.
