A fourth disorder is the opposite of avolition: an excess of drive. In mania the will seems supercharged, and DSM-5-TR lists an increase in goal-directed activity, socially, at work, at school or sexually, among the criteria, together with excessive involvement in activities with a high potential for painful consequences, such as unrestrained buying sprees, sexual indiscretions or foolish business investments (Section 5.4.1).

That last criterion leads to impulsivity, acting on a sudden urge without forethought, reflection or regard for the consequences, and to disinhibition, the loss of the social restraints that normally keep urges and remarks to oneself. Impulsivity is found in mania, ADHD, borderline and antisocial personality disorders, intoxication and the impulse-control disorders (Chapter 11); disinhibition is a hallmark of frontal lobe injury and of the behavioural variant of frontotemporal dementia, for which DSM-5-TR lists "behavioral disinhibition" as a criterion. An impulse and a compulsion are opposites, and the contrast is a favourite question. An impulse is acted on readily and is gratifying or pleasurable at the moment it is acted on, even if regretted later; a compulsion is resisted, gives no pleasure, and relieves the distress produced by an obsession. The shoplifter with kleptomania feels a rising tension and then pleasure or relief as he steals; the man with obsessive-compulsive disorder checks the gas tap for the twentieth time with mounting misery.

Key study: The patient who copied his doctor (Lhermitte and colleagues, 1986)

What happens to the will when the frontal lobes are damaged? The French neurologist François Lhermitte had noticed that some patients with frontal lesions picked up and used everyday objects placed within their reach without being asked, which he called utilisation behaviour. In 1986 he and two colleagues described a further behaviour, imitation behaviour: while the examiner simply sat opposite the patient and made gestures, the patient copied them, although no instruction to imitate had been given. Asked why, patients explained that they thought they had to. In a neuropsychological study of 75 patients (40 with imitation behaviour, 35 with utilisation behaviour) and 50 patients with other diseases as controls, one or both behaviours were present in 96 percent of the 29 patients with focal frontal lesions, and brain scans showed damage to the lower part of the front of one or both frontal lobes.

Lhermitte interpreted the behaviours as a release: without frontal inhibition, the brain systems that respond automatically to the physical and social environment take over. In a companion paper he followed two patients into a doctor's office, a lecture room, a car, a garden, an apartment and a gift shop, and found that they behaved as though the environment itself contained an order to respond, which he called the environmental dependency syndrome, a disorder of personal autonomy. The work matters for this section twice over. It shows that echopraxia-like imitation can arise from frontal damage outside catatonia, and it shows what the will normally does: it frees us from having to do whatever our surroundings suggest.

A final disorder of will is visible in a person's home rather than in his body. Self-neglect, the failure to wash, eat properly, take medicine or keep one's surroundings habitable, is a behavioural sign that can come from severe depression, avolition in schizophrenia, dementia, alcohol dependence or frontal damage, and in its extreme form in older people living alone it forms the Diogenes syndrome of Section 2.6. Table 2.26 collects the disorders of will.

Table 2.26 The disorders of will, with what each one is and where it is found.
TermWhat it isWhere it is found
AvolitionA decrease in motivated, self-initiated, purposeful activitySchizophrenia (negative symptom), depression
AbuliaLack of will and initiative, slowed responses, little spontaneous speech or action; acts when promptedFrontal and basal ganglia lesions, stroke, vascular neurocognitive disorder
Akinetic mutismThe extreme of abulia: no movement or speech, eyes followingFrontal and deep midline lesions (Section 2.2)
ApathyLack of feeling, interest and concern; loss of motivationDementia, Parkinson's disease, depression (Section 2.8.6)
AmbitendencyOpposite impulses: an action begun and withdrawn, over and overCatatonia, schizophrenia
NegativismMotiveless opposition to instructions, passive or activeCatatonia
Automatic obedienceEvery instruction carried out literally, whatever its senseCatatonia, latah
Environmental dependencyActions dictated by the objects and people aroundFrontal lobe lesions
Increased driveExcessive goal-directed and risky activityMania, hypomania
ImpulsivityActing at once on an urge, without forethoughtMania, ADHD, borderline and antisocial personality, impulse-control disorders
DisinhibitionLoss of social restraintFrontal injury, frontotemporal dementia, mania, intoxication
Self-neglectFailure to care for oneself and one's surroundingsSevere depression, schizophrenia, dementia, Diogenes syndrome (Section 2.6)
Exam trap

Avolition (DSM-5-TR: a decrease in motivated, self-initiated, purposeful activities) is a negative symptom of schizophrenia; abulia is lack of will or initiative after frontal or basal ganglia damage, typically in stroke and vascular dementia; apathy is lack of feeling and concern. Ambitendency is ambivalence of the will. An impulse is gratifying and acted on readily; a compulsion is resisted and relieves distress. Imitation of the examiner's gestures can come from catatonia (echopraxia), Tourette's disorder (a complex tic) or frontal lobe damage (Lhermitte's imitation behaviour).

  2. From mildest to most severe, elevated mood runs euphoria, elation, , ecstasy.
  3. The inability to experience pleasure or interest in activities once enjoyed, named by Ribot in 1896, is .
  4. Having no words for one's own feelings, a condition named by Peter Sifneos, is called .
  5. Of quality, range, reactivity and persistence, the one that describes mood rather than affect is .

2.10 The Self, the Body, Insight and JudgementLower yield · 1 Q

Here is a question that sounds too silly to ask. How do you know that the thoughts you are thinking as you read this sentence are yours? You did not check. Nobody told you. You simply know, with a certainty so complete that it has never occurred to you to doubt it. The same goes for a handful of other certainties: that you are the one moving your hand when you turn the page, that you are one person and not two, that you are the same person who went to sleep last night, that your thoughts stay inside your own head, that the body in the chair is yours, and that if something went wrong with your mind you would be able to tell. None of these is a perception, a belief or a memory of the kind the earlier sections described. They are the background against which perceiving, believing and remembering take place. In psychopathology, every one of them can fail.

This last section of the chapter is about those background certainties. It begins with the self as the person experiences it, using the four characteristics that Karl Jaspers described (Section 2.1 introduced Jaspers and his method), and then turns to the commonest way in which the self and the world lose their felt reality, depersonalisation and derealisation. It moves next to the body as the person experiences it, which can be wrongly felt, wrongly believed about, or simply not noticed. It ends with the two headings that close every mental status examination: insight, the person's knowledge of his own illness, and judgement, his ability to weigh a situation and decide well, which in Indian law leads straight to the question of capacity.

The four formal characteristics of self-awareness

In General Psychopathology Jaspers distinguished two directions in which awareness can point. Most of the time it points outwards, at objects: the tree I see, the voice I hear, the problem I am working on. Jaspers called this object-consciousness. But every such experience also carries an awareness of the one who is having it, and this he called ego-consciousness, which in plainer English is self-awareness. He described it by four formal characteristics, "formal" in exactly the sense of Section 2.1: they concern the structure of self-experience, not its content. Whether I think of myself as clever or dull, devout or sceptical, a success or a failure, is content. That I experience myself as active, as one, as continuous and as separate from the world is form. Fish's Clinical Psychopathology, the British text on which much Indian teaching is based, sets out the four as follows.

  • Awareness of activity: the awareness that I exist and that I am the one who is thinking, perceiving, feeling and acting. My thoughts arrive with the sense that I am thinking them; my movements arrive with the sense that I am making them.
  • Awareness of unity: the awareness that at any given moment I am one person, not two or more.
  • Awareness of identity: the awareness that I am the same person over time, continuous with the child I was and with the person who went to sleep last night.
  • Awareness of boundaries: the awareness that I am distinct from the outside world and from other people, so that my thoughts and feelings are inside me and theirs are inside them.

Healthy people never notice these four, any more than they notice the floor under their feet. That is exactly why the disorders that break them are so hard for patients to put into words and so easy for clinicians to misread. The value of Jaspers' scheme is practical. When a person says something strange about himself, the first question to ask is which of the four has given way, because experiences that sound alike can turn out to belong to different characteristics and therefore to different disorders. Figure 2.15 shows the four with the main experiences that break them, and Table 2.27 adds the settings in which each occurs.

Activity. The awareness of activity is close to what researchers now call the sense of agency, a link the Heidelberg psychiatrist Thomas Fuchs has drawn explicitly. It can fail in two very different ways. In the first, the person still knows that he is the one acting but no longer feels it: he moves, speaks and thinks as if on automatic pilot, watching himself from a distance. This is depersonalisation, and Fish files it under exactly this heading; it is taught in full in the next subsection. In the second, the person is convinced that the activity is not his at all. These are the passivity experiences of Section 2.4: thought insertion (thoughts placed in the mind by an outside agency), thought withdrawal (thoughts taken out of it), thought broadcasting (thoughts passing out of the mind so that others know them), and the made feelings, made impulses, made actions and somatic passivity, in which emotions, urges, movements or bodily sensations are imposed from outside. It was on Jaspers' account of ego-consciousness that Kurt Schneider later built the German term Ich-Störungen, ego disorders, for these experiences; ICD-10 and DSM-IV treated the same experiences as bizarre delusions of control or passivity. The difference between the two failures is the difference between "it is as if I were a puppet" and "I am a puppet, and I know who pulls the strings". Hold on to it: it is one of the most important lines in the whole chapter.

Unity. Disturbances of unity are rarer and harder to pin down. The clearest appears in depersonalisation itself, where, as DSM-5-TR describes, the self can seem split, one part observing and the other taking part; at its extreme this becomes an out-of-body experience, in which the person seems to watch his own body from a point outside it. In schizophrenia some patients describe a doubleness of another kind, living at the same time in the shared everyday world and in a private delusional one. One patient told the Danish psychiatrist Josef Parnas and his colleagues, in a paper published in 2020, "I really feel that I live in two worlds." Eugen Bleuler's name for this was double bookkeeping (Section 2.2 mentions it). Whether it is a true division of the self, or a single consciousness holding two incompatible realities at once, is still argued, and Parnas himself takes the second view. Seeing one's own double, autoscopy, is a perceptual experience and belongs with the special hallucinations of Section 2.3.

Identity. The awareness of being the same person over time can be shaken in several ways. In the early phase of schizophrenia some people say that they have changed in some fundamental and indescribable way, that the person they used to be has gone, or that they have been altered from within. In dissociative identity disorder (Section 7.6.1) the break in continuity is more visible: two or more distinct personality states take turns, and the person loses stretches of time that another state lived through. DSM-5-TR describes the disorder in terms of discontinuities in both the sense of self and the sense of agency, so it disturbs activity as well as identity. In possession states (Section 7.6.7) the person's ordinary identity is displaced by that of a deity, a spirit, an ancestor or a demon, who speaks and acts through him. Here culture decides almost everything. A woman through whom the goddess speaks at a village festival, who enters the state at the expected moment, is recognised by her community and returns to herself afterwards, is taking part in a religious practice, and DSM-5-TR excludes from the disorder any possession that is a normal part of a broadly accepted cultural or religious practice. The same experience arriving unbidden, frightening her and disrupting her life, is another matter.

Boundaries. The fourth characteristic is the sense of where I end and the world begins. Schneider wrote of the permeability of the ego boundary in schizophrenia, and Figure 4.3 shows how naturally the first-rank symptoms can be read as one boundary giving way at many points: thoughts, feelings and impulses come in from outside, thoughts are pulled out or leak out, and one's own thinking is heard aloud as voices. A subtler boundary disturbance is transitivism, in which the person confuses his own thoughts, feelings or experiences with those of the person he is with, as though they were shared or had changed hands. Loosening of the boundary also has a normal counterpart. The French writer Romain Rolland (1866 to 1944) described to Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939) a religious sense of oneness with the universe, an "oceanic feeling", which Freud discussed at the opening of Civilization and Its Discontents (1930), and meditators and mystics in many traditions, Indian ones prominent among them, seek such states on purpose. As with every phenomenon in this chapter, the experience alone does not make a diagnosis: its form, its setting and what it does to the person's life do.

Jaspers' scheme has had a vigorous second life. Since the early 2000s a group of phenomenological psychiatrists led by Parnas in Copenhagen, together with the American psychologist Louis Sass, has argued that schizophrenia rests on a subtle, often long-standing disturbance of the most basic sense of being a self, which they call self-disorder or ipseity disturbance (from the Latin ipse, self). The person may feel that his thoughts have become oddly anonymous, that he is not quite present in his own experience, or that the line between himself and other people is uncertain, sometimes well before any voice or delusion appears. To study these experiences Parnas and his colleagues published the Examination of Anomalous Self-Experience (EASE) in 2005, and its five domains read like a modern expansion of Jaspers' list: cognition and the stream of consciousness, self-awareness and presence, bodily experiences, demarcation and transitivism, and existential reorientation. Reviews have found self-disorders to be characteristic of the schizophrenia spectrum, although similar experiences are reported in bipolar disorder and in depersonalisation disorder, so they are a pointer rather than a test. The interview itself belongs to the Assessment book.

Table 2.27 Jaspers' four formal characteristics of self-awareness and how they break.
CharacteristicThe healthy, unnoticed certaintyWhen it breaksUsual settings
Activity (agency)My thoughts, feelings and actions are mine, and I am doing themFeeling like an automaton while knowing one acts (depersonalisation); thoughts, feelings, impulses or acts made by an outside agency (passivity)Depersonalisation: exhaustion, panic, depression, drugs; passivity: schizophrenia
UnityI am one person at this momentA split self, one part watching and one acting; living in two worlds at onceDepersonalisation; schizophrenia
IdentityI am the same person I was yesterday and years ago"I am no longer who I was"; identities taking turns; a deity or spirit in place of the selfSchizophrenia; dissociative identity disorder; possession and trance states
BoundariesI am separate from the world and from other peopleThoughts inserted, withdrawn or broadcast; one's own experience confused with another's (transitivism)Schizophrenia (first-rank symptoms); normal counterpart in mystical and meditative states
Exam trap

Jaspers' four formal characteristics of self-awareness are activity, unity, identity (continuity over time) and boundaries (separateness from the world). Depersonalisation and the passivity experiences both disturb activity, but in depersonalisation the person knows he is acting and only feels as if he is not ("as if a robot"), while in passivity he is convinced that an outside agency is acting through him ("they move me like a robot"). Schneider's ego disorders (Ich-Störungen) are the passivity experiences, not depersonalisation. Dissociative identity disorder and possession break identity; the first-rank symptoms are failures of the boundary. And these are disorders of form: "I am a great man" is a delusion of content, not a disorder of self-awareness.

Depersonalisation and derealisation: the self and the world grown strange

Rahul is 21 and in the final year of a commerce degree in Jaipur. On the afternoon of Holi, after two glasses of bhang thandai at a friend's terrace party, the world changed. "It was as though I was operating my body by remote control, like a drone. My legs were walking and I was watching them do it. My friends were laughing and I laughed too, but the laugh was only on my face. Then I looked down at the street and it had lost its depth. The people and the shops looked cut out of cardboard and pasted one behind the other, and even the gulal on my kurta looked printed on." The intoxication had worn off by the next morning; the strangeness lasted most of a week. Since then it has come back three times, each time for a few hours, twice in a crowded examination hall. "Doctor, I can tell you exactly where I am, who I am and what day it is. I know it is all real. That is what frightens me. I know it, and I cannot feel it."

Rahul is describing the two experiences of this subsection. The first, depersonalisation, is a change in the awareness of oneself, so that the self, or some part of it, feels unreal, detached, changed or not one's own; in the phrase that the textbooks repeat, the person feels that he is no longer his "normal, natural self". The second, derealisation, is the same change turned outwards: the surroundings feel unreal, distant, lifeless or strange. The word depersonalisation was introduced in 1898 by the French philosopher and psychologist Ludovic Dugas, who took it from the private journal of the Swiss writer Henri-Frédéric Amiel. The experience itself had been described before him, notably by the Hungarian-born physician Maurice Krishaber in the early 1870s, who included it in what he called a cerebro-cardiac neuropathy and traced it to a disturbance of the senses.

What exactly has changed? Patients find this the hardest question in the interview, because ordinary language has no words for an experience that alters not what is perceived but how real it feels. They fall back on comparisons, and almost always on the words "as if". From many such accounts a consistent picture emerges. The person may feel estranged from the self as a whole ("I am nobody"), from his feelings, from his thoughts, from his body or parts of it, or from his own actions. The emotional side is so prominent that DSM-5-TR gives it a name, hypoemotionality: the person knows that he loves his family, or that the news is sad, and simply does not feel it, which is itself acutely distressing. Memories can lose their personal colour, so that the past seems to have happened to somebody else, and time can seem to rush or to stand still. In derealisation the world looks flat, two-dimensional, foggy, too sharp or artificial; objects can seem too near or too far, too large or too small (the micropsia and macropsia of Section 2.3); sounds can seem muffled or too loud.

Is this one experience or several? In 2005 Mauricio Sierra and his colleagues at the Institute of Psychiatry in London put the question to the test. They gave the Cambridge Depersonalisation Scale to 138 consecutive patients with depersonalisation disorder and examined which of its items went together. Four factors emerged, which between them accounted for about 73 percent of the variation in the scores: anomalous body experience (the body or its parts feel unreal or not one's own), emotional numbing, anomalous subjective recall (memories and imagination feel unreal or impersonal, with distortions of time), and alienation from surroundings, which is derealisation. Their conclusion was that depersonalisation is a syndrome made of distinct but related symptoms, and DSM-5-TR now describes its symptom factors in much the same terms. Rahul shows three of the four: his body moves "by remote control", his laughter has no feeling behind it, and the street has gone flat.

The single most important fact about depersonalisation and derealisation is what does not change. Throughout the experience the person knows that he exists and that the world is real; it only feels otherwise. DSM-5-TR calls this intact reality testing, and it is what the "as if" in the patient's account signals. It explains why depersonalised people so often fear that they are going mad: they are observing their own experience with full critical awareness and cannot make sense of it. It is also the line that separates depersonalisation from psychosis, and the line is easiest to see where the content is most extreme. Compare two people who both use the word "dead".

  • "I feel as if I have died inside. I go about like a ghost, although of course I know I am alive." This is depersonalisation: the "as if" is kept and reality testing is intact.
  • "I am dead. My blood has stopped and my stomach has rotted away; there is no point feeding a corpse." This is a nihilistic delusion, the heart of the Cotard syndrome of severe psychotic depression (Section 2.6; Table 7.14 sets out the look-alikes side by side).

The content moves only a short distance between the two statements; the form moves all the way from an altered feeling to a false belief held with conviction. That is Jaspers' distinction between form and content once more, and it is why a careful clinician asks not only what the person feels but what he believes about the feeling.

Depersonalisation must also be kept apart from the passivity experiences met under the awareness of activity. Rahul says that he moved "by remote control", and a man with schizophrenia may say that his arm is moved by a remote control in the hands of his neighbours. The image is the same; the form is opposite. Rahul knows that he is the one walking and only feels detached from it; the second man is certain that somebody else is doing it. One is an altered feeling with reality testing intact, the other a delusion of control and a first-rank symptom. Depersonalisation is not a first-rank symptom, however bizarre it sounds.

Key study: Freud on the Acropolis (1904, published 1936)

In 1904 Sigmund Freud and his younger brother Alexander were on holiday in Trieste when an acquaintance advised them to sail to Athens instead of following their original plan. Both felt oddly gloomy and hesitant about the change, but they went. Standing on the Acropolis, Freud was struck by a surprising thought, which in English translation runs: "So it really does exist, just as we learnt at school!" He had never doubted that the Acropolis existed; the strangeness lay in the feeling that what he was looking at could not quite be real. More than thirty years later, in 1936, he analysed the episode in an open letter for Romain Rolland's seventieth birthday, "A Disturbance of Memory on the Acropolis". He classed the experience as a derealisation and traced it to guilt at having gone further in life than his father, a man without secondary education for whom Athens could have meant little, as though to excel one's father were still forbidden.

The case matters for two reasons. It is a careful first-person account of derealisation in a healthy and highly articulate man, with reality testing fully intact. And it shows the difference between the descriptive and the dynamic questions of Section 2.1: the description (what the experience was like) is not in dispute, while the explanation (what it meant) is Freud's own interpretation, which you may or may not accept.

Who has these experiences? Almost everyone, briefly. DSM-5-TR estimates that about half of all adults have had at least one episode, and surveys give lifetime figures anywhere from about a quarter to three-quarters of people, depending on how the question is asked. In healthy people the usual triggers are exhaustion, lack of sleep, intense stress and sudden danger; people who have survived a road accident or a fall sometimes say that they watched it as though it were happening to somebody else. In clinical settings depersonalisation is one of the commonest and least specific of all symptoms. It is one of the thirteen listed symptoms of a panic attack (Section 6.2.1). It is frequent in depression and in the anxiety disorders; after trauma, persistent depersonalisation or derealisation defines the dissociative subtype of posttraumatic stress disorder; and it runs through the dissociative disorders. It occurs in the aura and the seizures of temporal lobe epilepsy. It is brought on by cannabis, as in Rahul's case, by hallucinogens and by ketamine. And it can be part of the early, prodromal phase of schizophrenia, before the illness declares itself.

Why should one experience turn up in so many conditions? In 1935 the German psychiatrist Wilhelm Mayer-Gross (1889 to 1961), by then working in Britain, proposed in his paper "On depersonalisation" that it is a "preformed functional response" of the brain: a stock reaction that the nervous system can produce in many different states, much as fever is a stock reaction of the body to many different infections. The idea has aged well. It explains why depersonalisation tells you so little about the diagnosis by itself, and why its meaning depends on everything else in the picture: the panic that came first, the low mood around it, the drug, the seizure, or the strange new conviction that follows it.

When depersonalisation or derealisation becomes persistent or recurrent, causes distress or impairment, is not the product of a drug or of a medical condition such as epilepsy, and is not better explained by another mental disorder, the diagnosis is depersonalisation/derealisation disorder. That disorder belongs to Chapter 7: Section 7.5.3 gives its criteria and Table 7.14, and Section 7.5.4 its prevalence (about 1 to 2 percent over a month in British surveys), its onset (on average at 16), its causes and the disappointing results of drug treatment. Book 2, Section 2.5.8 shows how to ask about both experiences in a mental status examination. Whether Rahul's recurrences add up to the disorder depends on how distressing and disabling they become; what he needs first is an explanation (the experience has a name, it is common after cannabis, it is not madness and it does not damage the brain) and the advice to keep away from the drug.

Exam trap

Depersonalisation is the self felt as unreal, detached or changed, no longer one's normal, natural self (papers phrase it as a change in the awareness of one's own identity); derealisation is the surroundings felt as unreal, flat or dreamlike. "Stress" and "depression" are distractors: they can set off both experiences but name neither. In both, reality testing is intact (the "as if"); "I am dead" held as a fact is a nihilistic delusion (Cotard syndrome, Section 2.6). Depersonalisation is not a first-rank symptom and not a passivity experience: "as if a robot" is depersonalisation, "they work me like a robot" is a made action. Déjà vu and jamais vu are disorders of recognition memory, taught in Section 2.7, not forms of derealisation. The word was introduced by Ludovic Dugas in 1898.

The body as experienced: body schema, body image and their disorders

Your body is the one part of the world that is also part of you, and you experience it from both sides at once: as an object you can see in a mirror and as the place from which you see. Disorders of body experience are correspondingly varied. Some are failures of perception, some are failures of awareness caused by damage to the brain, and some are beliefs and attitudes about the body that range from an anxious worry to a delusion. Two technical terms keep them apart, and the difference between them organises everything in this subsection.

The first is the body schema. In 1911 the London neurologists Henry Head (1861 to 1940) and Gordon Morgan Holmes (1876 to 1965), describing patients with damage to the parietal lobe, proposed that the brain keeps a constantly updated model of the posture and position of the body, against which each new movement and sensation is measured; they called it the postural schema. The schema works below awareness. You do not consciously consult it when you reach for a glass in the dark or duck under a low doorway, yet without it you could do neither. The second term is the body image, given its classic form by the Austrian psychiatrist Paul Ferdinand Schilder (1886 to 1940) in The Image and Appearance of the Human Body (1935): the picture of one's own body that one forms in the mind, the way the body appears to oneself. Schilder, who spent his last years at Bellevue Hospital in New York, insisted that this picture is not only perceptual. It is built from sensation and posture, but also from feeling, attitude, desire and the way other people respond to us, so that a body image can be bent by emotion and by culture as well as by a lesion. Modern research keeps the distinction: the schema is a largely unconscious sensorimotor system that serves action, the image a conscious set of perceptions, attitudes and beliefs about one's body.

The most dramatic evidence that the body schema lives in the brain rather than in the limbs is the phantom limb, the vivid sensation that an amputated limb is still present. The French military surgeon Ambroise Paré (about 1510 to 1590) described soldiers who complained of pain in limbs he had removed, and the American physician Silas Weir Mitchell (1829 to 1914), who treated amputees of the American Civil War, gave the experience its name in a magazine article of 1871 and in his book on nerve injuries the following year (some sources date the name to the book). Most amputees go on experiencing the missing limb, and Pinel and Barnes report that about half of all amputees have severe, chronic pain in it; remarkably, about one in five people born without a limb report a phantom too. The phantom usually feels entirely real, so much so that a man may try to step out of bed onto a leg that is not there. It may feel stuck in an awkward or painful position, and over the years it may seem to shrink up towards the stump, a change called telescoping. Notice the form: phantom limb is a perceptual experience, not a belief, because the person knows perfectly well that the limb is gone. Its neural basis, the reorganisation of the somatosensory cortex demonstrated by Vilayanur Ramachandran, and his mirror-box treatment belong to the Physiological book.

Sarita is 68, a retired Hindi teacher in Lucknow, admitted to a medical ward with a stroke in the right hemisphere of her brain that has paralysed the left side of her body. On the third day the ward team asks for a psychiatric opinion because, in the resident's words, "she has started talking nonsense". She is alert, oriented and pleasant, and she remembers the doctor's name from the day before. Asked whether anything is wrong with her, she says she has come in "for a little blood pressure". Asked to lift her left arm, she says, "There, I have lifted it," while it lies motionless on the sheet. When the doctor raises the limp left hand into her line of sight and asks whose it is, she glances at it and says, "That is my husband's. He sits here at night and leaves it behind." The nurses add that she eats only from the right half of her thali, and that she has to be turned to see visitors who stand on her left.

Sarita is not psychotic in the sense of Chapter 4. She is showing, in one patient, the family of body disorders that can follow damage to the right parietal region. Her unawareness of the paralysis is anosognosia, the word coined in 1914 by the French neurologist Joseph Babinski (1857 to 1932) for exactly this failure to recognise a deficit (Book 2, Section 2.6.9). Her neglect of the left half of her plate and of the left side of space is unilateral neglect, which the Physiological book teaches. Her failure to recognise the left arm as part of her own body is asomatognosia, which Pinel and Barnes describe as usually one-sided and commonly a part of neglect; when it affects a whole half of the body it is also called hemisomatognosia. And her explanation that the arm is her husband's goes one step further. Denying ownership of one's own limb and attributing it to somebody else, with an explanation that resists every correction, is somatoparaphrenia, a term introduced by the Austrian-born neurologist Josef Gerstmann (1887 to 1969) in 1942. It most often concerns the left arm, usually comes with paralysis of that side and often, though not always, with anosognosia. In form it is a true delusion, a fixed false belief, but one built on a neurological deficit. These states often fade over the weeks after a stroke. The psychiatrist's task is to recognise them for what they are, so that Sarita is not given a diagnosis of psychotic illness or an antipsychotic she does not need.

Two neighbouring disorders follow damage to the left parietal lobe instead. In autotopagnosia, first described in 1908 by the Prague neuropsychiatrist Arnold Pick (1851 to 1924), the person cannot localise or point to parts of his own body on request ("show me your knee", "touch your left ear"), although he can use them perfectly well. The narrower inability to name, identify or select one's own fingers, finger agnosia, was described by Gerstmann in 1924 and is one of the four elements of Gerstmann syndrome. Their neurology, with the agnosias in general, belongs to the Physiological book; what matters here is that they too are disorders of body experience that a psychiatric interview can stumble upon.

The psychiatric disorders of body image are different in kind. The brain's maps of the body are intact; what is disturbed is the body image in Schilder's wider sense, the attitudes, beliefs and feelings about the body. The best studied example is anorexia nervosa (Chapter 8). The German-born American psychiatrist Hilde Bruch (1904 to 1984), writing in 1962 about her own patients with anorexia nervosa, described a disturbance of the body image of delusional proportions and regarded it as an important sign for both diagnosis and outcome. Her observation entered the diagnostic criteria. DSM-5-TR's Criterion C for anorexia nervosa still offers three ways in which the person's relation to her body may be disturbed: in the way body weight or shape is experienced, in the undue influence of weight or shape on her judgement of herself, or in a persistent failure to recognise how serious the low weight is. Some patients feel fat all over; others accept that they are thin but insist that the abdomen, buttocks or thighs are "too fat", and check them constantly by weighing, measuring and looking in mirrors.

Key study: does she see herself as fat, or judge herself as fat?

If body image has a perceptual part and an attitudinal part, which is disturbed in anorexia nervosa? In 1973 Peter Slade and Gerald Russell asked patients with anorexia nervosa to estimate the widths of parts of their own bodies, and reported that the patients overestimated them, which seemed to show a perceptual distortion: they literally saw themselves as larger than they were. Over the next two decades dozens of studies used size-estimation tasks of this kind, with inconsistent results. In 1997 Thomas F. Cash and his co-author E. A. Deagle combined 66 such studies, published between 1974 and 1993, in a meta-analysis. Attitudinal body dissatisfaction produced substantially larger differences between patients and controls than perceptual inaccuracy did, and only the attitudinal measures distinguished patients with anorexia nervosa from those with bulimia nervosa. They concluded that the size distortion was unlikely to reflect a general sensory or perceptual deficit.

The lesson for descriptive psychopathology is that the "distorted body image" of anorexia nervosa is mainly a matter of attitude and evaluation, an overvalued idea about shape and weight (Section 2.5) that is sometimes held with delusional intensity, rather than a hallucination of fatness. It also helps to explain why such patients so often have poor insight into how ill they are.

The remaining psychiatric disturbances of body image fit into the families this chapter has already built. In body dysmorphic disorder (Section 6.6.2) the person is preoccupied with one or more perceived defects in appearance that are slight or invisible to others, most often the skin, the hair or the nose. Its variant muscle dysmorphia, the conviction that one's build is too small or insufficiently muscular, occurs almost exclusively in men, according to DSM-5-TR. In koro, which DSM-5-TR describes as a culturally related disorder that usually occurs in epidemics in South-East Asia, the person fears that the penis (in women, the labia, nipples or breasts) is shrinking or retracting into the abdomen, often believing that death will follow; India has had its own outbreaks, among them one in Assam and West Bengal in 1982. Dhat syndrome, the anxious conviction that semen is being lost, often in the urine, and that the loss is draining the body's strength, is the South Asian cultural concept of distress that DSM-5-TR names as a relative of koro; both are taught in Chapter 1. Hypochondriacal concern, in which ordinary bodily sensations are read as signs of serious disease, is the territory of illness anxiety disorder (Section 7.7.3). At the psychotic end lie the somatic delusions of Section 2.5 ("my intestines have turned to stone") and the somatic passivity of Section 2.4 ("they heat my liver with rays"); at the non-psychotic end, the changed, numb or unreal body of depersonalisation. Finally, ICD-11 includes body integrity dysphoria, a persistent desire to have a healthy limb amputated because the body's actual shape feels wrong, which DSM-5 does not include. Table 2.28 brings all of these together, and Figure 2.16 places them on the body.

Table 2.28 The disorders of body experience, with the kind of disturbance each involves.
DisorderWhat the person experiencesKind of disturbanceWhere it occurs
Phantom limbAn amputated limb still felt, often painfulPerceptual (body schema)After amputation, in most amputees
AnosognosiaUnaware of a paralysis or other deficitNeurological unawarenessRight-hemisphere stroke; dementia
Asomatognosia (hemisomatognosia)Fails to recognise a limb or half the body as one's ownNeurological unawarenessRight parietal damage, with neglect
SomatoparaphreniaSays one's own limb belongs to someone elseDelusion on a neurological basisRight-hemisphere stroke, usually the left arm
Autotopagnosia; finger agnosiaCannot point to or name one's own body parts or fingersNeurological (knowledge of the body's layout)Left parietal damage; Gerstmann syndrome
Anorexia nervosaA thin body experienced and judged as too fatMainly attitudinal: an overvalued ideaAnorexia nervosa (Chapter 8)
Body dysmorphic disorder; muscle dysmorphiaA slight or imagined flaw; a build felt to be too smallOvervalued idea, sometimes delusionalBody dysmorphic disorder
KoroGenitals retracting into the body, with fear of deathCulturally shaped fear and beliefSouth-East Asia; outbreaks in India

Exam trap

The body schema (Head and Holmes, 1911) is the unconscious postural model of the body that serves movement; the body image (Schilder, 1935) is the conscious picture of one's body, with its attitudes and feelings. Phantom limb is a perception, not a belief: the amputee knows the limb is gone. Anosognosia (Babinski, 1914) is unawareness of a deficit; somatoparaphrenia (Gerstmann, 1942) is a delusional disowning of one's own limb; both usually follow right-hemisphere damage and concern the left side. Autotopagnosia (Pick, 1908) and finger agnosia follow left parietal damage. In anorexia nervosa the disturbance is mainly attitudinal, an overvalued idea, more than a perceptual error. Koro is the fear of genital retraction with fear of death and dhat the fear of semen loss; neither is body dysmorphic disorder.

Insight: knowing that one is ill

Tushar is 38 and runs a garment shop in Surat. His elder brother brings him to the emergency department after a week in which Tushar has slept two or three hours a night, booked a new car, signed for a loan to open three showrooms "before Diwali" and spent an afternoon handing out sarees to strangers outside the shop. Asked why he has come, Tushar laughs. "Ask my brother. He is jealous because business has never been better. I have never felt better in my life." Ten days later, sleeping again and calmer, he says, "I got carried away. It was the heat, and the wedding season, and too much tea." By discharge he can explain to the junior doctor that he has bipolar disorder, that his tablets prevent relapse and that he will need them for years. Three months later he stops them, "because I was perfectly fine", and he is readmitted in the next wedding season. Two years on, it is Tushar himself who telephones the clinic when he notices that he is sleeping less and planning more, and he has agreed with his family that his brother keeps the chequebook during the busy months.

Tushar's story is a journey through the grades of insight, the heading that closes the mental status examination. In the MSE, insight means the person's awareness that he is ill, his understanding of the nature and cause of the illness, and his acceptance that he needs treatment. Book 2, Section 2.6.9 teaches it in detail, so only its essentials are recalled here. Aubrey Lewis (1900 to 1975) defined it in 1934 as "a correct attitude to a morbid change in oneself". The British psychiatrist Anthony David proposed in 1990 that it has three overlapping dimensions: recognising that one has a mental illness, accepting treatment, and being able to relabel unusual experiences such as voices and delusions as abnormal. In the WHO International Pilot Study of Schizophrenia, lack of insight was the most frequent symptom of all, found in about 97 percent of patients. Gaining insight in schizophrenia can bring depression with it, the insight paradox. And an explanation of illness shared with one's family and culture is not by itself poor insight. Section 4.2.7 adds the view, which DSM-5-TR takes, that the poor insight of psychosis is a symptom of the illness comparable to anosognosia rather than a way of coping. The six grades taught in most Indian MSE formats are summarised in Table 2.29 and drawn as a ladder in Figure 2.17.

Table 2.29 The six grades of insight in brief (Book 2, Table 2.17 gives a patient's words for each).
GradeNameWhat the person recognises
1Complete denialNothing; he is not ill
2Slight awareness, still denyingThat he may need some help, while denying illness in the same breath
3Awareness, cause placed outsideThat he is ill, but because of other people, events or physical factors
4Awareness, cause unknown in oneselfThat something in himself has gone wrong, though he does not know what
5Intellectual insightThat he is ill and his symptoms come from his own disturbance, without applying the knowledge to his conduct
6True emotional insightThe same, felt as well as known, so that it changes his behaviour

Put Tushar on the ladder. On the day of admission he was on grade 1, complete denial. Ten days later, blaming the heat, the wedding season and the tea, he was on grade 3: he accepted that something had happened to him but placed its cause outside himself. At discharge he was on grade 5, intellectual insight: he could describe his illness correctly, but the knowledge did not survive the first months of feeling well. Two years later, telephoning the clinic at the first sign and handing over the chequebook, he had reached grade 6, in which knowledge has become a change in conduct. Notice that the step from 5 to 6 is not a step in what he knew. It is a step in what he did with it.

Two older ideas sharpen the ladder. Jaspers separated a mere awareness of illness, the feeling of being ill or changed, which need not extend to all the symptoms or to the illness as a whole and involves no correct judgement of its kind or severity, from insight proper, which requires that correct judgement. A depressed man who says, "Something has gone terribly wrong with me; I think God is punishing me," has awareness of illness without insight. Later writers loosened the distinction: in 1992 Ivana Marková and Germán Berrios described insight as a continuum of thinking and feeling, affected by many internal and external variables, which is closer to the way the grades are used today. Lewis made a second point that is easy to forget: insight cannot by itself separate psychosis from neurosis, because it is found, and lost, in both.

What Book 2 does not do is follow insight across the disorders, and the pattern is worth learning because it shows what each disorder does to a person's knowledge of himself. Table 2.30 sets it out. Insight is usually absent in acute psychosis: hence the 97 percent of the pilot study. It is usually absent in mania too; DSM-5-TR observes that people in a manic episode often do not perceive that they are ill or in need of treatment and vehemently resist efforts to treat them, and it returns as the episode resolves, as Tushar's did. In depression the picture is mixed. Without psychotic features the depressed person usually knows that he is ill, but his insight is often distorted rather than absent: he may accept the illness and yet regard it as deserved, or as proof that he is beyond help, and the hopelessness is itself a symptom. With psychotic features, when guilt or nihilism has become a delusion, insight is lost.

The obsessive-compulsive and related disorders made insight part of the diagnosis itself. DSM-5-TR asks the clinician to specify, for obsessive-compulsive disorder, body dysmorphic disorder and hoarding disorder, whether the person has good or fair insight (recognises that the beliefs behind the symptoms are definitely or probably not true, or may or may not be true), poor insight (thinks they are probably true) or absent insight with delusional beliefs (is completely convinced that they are true). The levels differ between the disorders. In obsessive-compulsive disorder most people have good or fair insight and only a few, 4 percent or fewer, have absent insight; in body dysmorphic disorder insight is on average poor, and a third or more of patients are completely convinced that they are ugly; in hoarding disorder poor insight is common enough that the person may report no distress at all, while the impairment is obvious to everyone else. For obsessive-compulsive disorder DSM-5-TR links poorer insight to a worse long-term outcome, and in all three disorders a person with absent insight is not given an extra diagnosis of a psychotic disorder unless the delusional beliefs extend beyond the disorder's own themes (Section 6.5.5).

Insight is often poor in anorexia nervosa, where, in DSM-5-TR's words, individuals frequently either lack insight into or deny the problem, so that information from the family is essential; and it is often reduced in the neurocognitive disorders, where it shades into anosognosia. In the behavioural variant of frontotemporal neurocognitive disorder DSM-5-TR notes that insight is usually impaired, which delays medical consultation and means that the first referral is often to a psychiatrist. At the other end, insight is characteristically preserved in Charles Bonnet syndrome (Section 2.3), where the person knows the images are not real; in depersonalisation, where reality testing is intact by definition; and in most anxiety disorders. A person with a specific phobia usually recognises that his fear is excessive, although DSM-5-TR adds that he tends to overestimate the danger, so the judgement that the fear is out of proportion is finally the clinician's. Preserved insight was for a century the classic mark of the neuroses (Section 6.1.3), with Lewis's caveat that it is a tendency, not a rule.

Table 2.30 Insight across the disorders.
DisorderUsual insightNote
Schizophrenia and other acute psychosesUsually absentAbout 97 percent in the WHO pilot study; likened to anosognosia
ManiaUsually absent in the episodeDoes not perceive illness, resists treatment; returns with recovery
Depression without psychosisUsually present, often distortedAccepts illness but may see it as deserved or hopeless
Psychotic depressionLostGuilt or nihilism held as fact
Obsessive-compulsive disorderUsually good or fairSpecifier: good or fair, poor, absent; absent in 4 percent or fewer
Body dysmorphic disorderOn average poorA third or more fully convinced (delusional)
Hoarding disorderOften poorDistress may not be reported; others see the impairment
Anorexia nervosaOften poorLacks insight or denies the problem; informants needed
Neurocognitive disordersOften reducedShades into anosognosia; usually impaired in behavioural frontotemporal disorder
Panic disorder, phobias, most anxiety disordersUsually preservedFear recognised as excessive, though danger overestimated
Depersonalisation and derealisationPreservedReality testing intact by definition; fears "going mad"
Charles Bonnet syndromeUsually preservedKnows the images are not real
Personality disordersOften limitedTraits often ego-syntonic; informants help

Look down the second column of Table 2.30 and a pattern appears that links this subsection to the first section of the chapter. Insight is best preserved where the symptom is ego-dystonic, felt as intrusive, unwanted and alien to the self (Section 2.1): the obsession the person fights, the panic he dreads, the unreality he cannot bear. It is poorest where the symptom is ego-syntonic, at one with the person's values or with his sense of who he is: the anorexic pursuit of thinness, the personality trait that seems simply "the way I am" (DSM-5-TR notes that personality disorder traits are often ego-syntonic, which is why informants matter), and the elation that feels like the truth at last. Delusions are the extreme case. A belief held as a plain fact about the world is not experienced as a symptom at all, so there is nothing to have insight into until the belief begins to loosen. That is why ego-syntonic problems bring fewer people to treatment of their own accord, and why so many patients with poor insight arrive, as Tushar did, because somebody else brought them.

Indian context

The Mental Healthcare Act 2017 writes the cultural caution of Book 2, Section 2.6.9 into law. Section 3(3)(b) provides that mental illness shall not be determined on the basis of non-conformity with the moral, social, cultural, work or political values or religious beliefs prevailing in the person's community. For insight this means that a woman who explains her illness, as her family does, by the evil eye, a difficult planetary period or a goddess's displeasure has not thereby shown poor insight. What is graded is whether she recognises a morbid change in herself and will accept help for it. The rule cuts both ways: a belief does not become healthy because its content is religious, if its form is delusional (Section 2.5).

Exam trap

In the MSE, insight is the client's awareness and understanding of his own illness and of the need for treatment. It is not orientation to time, place and person (Section 2.2), not the ability to do serial subtraction, which tests attention and concentration (Section 2.2), and not the presence of hallucinations, which is a finding under perception (Section 2.3). Know the six grades: 1 complete denial; 2 slight awareness while still denying; 3 aware but blames others, external events or physical factors; 4 aware that the cause is something unknown in oneself; 5 intellectual insight, which leaves behaviour unchanged; 6 true emotional insight, which changes it. Insight is usually preserved in Charles Bonnet syndrome, depersonalisation and most anxiety disorders, and usually lost in acute psychosis and mania. And MSE insight is not the insight learning of Wolfgang Köhler (1887 to 1967), the sudden solution his chimpanzees found to problems such as reaching fruit with stacked boxes (Book 1).

Judgement, and the capacity to decide

Insight is knowledge about oneself; judgement is the ability to weigh a situation, foresee the likely results of acting in different ways, and act sensibly. Book 2, Section 2.6.8 teaches the three kinds that the MSE records: personal judgement (about one's own plans and life), social judgement (about conduct with other people) and test judgement, the answers given to standard hypothetical problems such as a stamped, addressed envelope found on the road or smoke in a crowded cinema hall. Its central warning is that the three can come apart, and that intact test judgement does not mean intact judgement. Tushar shows why. On the day of his admission he was asked what he would do if a caller offered him a prize in return for his bank details, and he answered at once and correctly that he would hang up and tell the bank. The same week he had committed himself to a loan he could never repay. Knowing what a sensible person would do is not the same as doing it.

What Book 2 leaves for this chapter is the question of where judgement fails, and here the descriptive vocabulary of the earlier sections explains a great deal. In mania, DSM-5-TR describes how expansive mood, excessive optimism, grandiosity and poor judgement lead to spending sprees, giving away possessions, reckless driving, foolish business investments and sexual indiscretions that are unusual for the person, despite their likely catastrophic consequences, and it adds that the serious consequences of a manic episode, from involuntary admission to legal and financial ruin, often result from poor judgement, loss of insight and hyperactivity together. In acute psychosis the reasoning may be intact while the premise is false: the man who sells his flat at a loss to escape the persecutors who have bugged it is reasoning well from a delusion (Section 2.5). In dementia and frontal lobe disease the machinery of weighing and inhibiting is itself damaged; DSM-5-TR lists poor judgement among the common symptoms of behavioural-variant frontotemporal neurocognitive disorder, alongside disinhibition and socially inappropriate behaviour. In intoxication DSM-5-TR includes impaired judgement among the changes produced by alcohol and many other drugs. In intellectual disability the problem is a limited grasp of risk; DSM-5-TR notes that gullibility and a lack of awareness of risk may leave the person open to exploitation. In depression, finally, judgement is distorted rather than lost: decisions follow a hopeless logic, to give up a course, resign a job or end a marriage, that the person would not endorse once well, which is why clinicians advise depressed patients to delay major decisions until they recover.

Judgement and insight matter beyond the MSE because they bear on one of the hardest questions in clinical work: can this person decide for himself? In India the question is now framed by the Mental Healthcare Act 2017, which Book 2, Section 1.10.3 teaches. Its definition of mental illness in section 2(1)(s) is written in the vocabulary of this chapter: a substantial disorder of thinking, mood, perception, orientation or memory that grossly impairs judgement, behaviour, the capacity to recognise reality or the ability to meet the ordinary demands of life. Section 4(1) then states that every person, including a person with mental illness, shall be "deemed to have capacity" to make decisions about his mental healthcare or treatment if he is able to understand the information relevant to the decision, to appreciate any reasonably foreseeable consequence of a decision or of a lack of decision, or to communicate the decision by speech, expression, gesture or any other means. Section 4(2) requires the information to be given in simple language, sign language or visual aids that the person can understand, and section 4(3) adds that a decision others perceive as inappropriate or wrong does not by itself mean that the person lacks decision-making capacity.

Read those three abilities as a psychopathologist and you can see which disorders threaten each. Understanding depends on clear consciousness, sustained attention, working memory and language, so it is lost in delirium, advanced dementia and severe intellectual disability (Sections 2.2 and 2.7). Appreciating is where insight and delusion bite: a patient may repeat accurately that people with his illness relapse without treatment and still be unable to accept that this applies to him, because he believes the illness is not his or the medicine is poison. Communicating fails in catatonic stupor (Section 2.9) and in severe aphasia, although the Act's "any other means" matters here: a mute patient who can nod, point or write may still communicate a decision. Notice, too, what the three concepts are not. Insight, judgement and capacity overlap but are not the same thing, as Table 2.31 shows. Capacity under the Act is presumed, is judged for a particular decision at a particular time, and is not cancelled merely because the person's choice seems unwise. Where a person with a severe mental illness cannot make treatment decisions independently and needs very high support from his nominated representative, and certain risks are present, section 89 provides for supported admission for up to thirty days, with safeguards.

Table 2.31 Insight, judgement and decision-making capacity compared.
ConceptThe question it asksJudged fromHow it can come apart from the others
InsightDoes he know that he is ill and needs treatment?His account of his experiences and illness (six grades)At grade 5 he describes his illness correctly yet stops treatment
JudgementCan he weigh a situation and act sensibly?History, behaviour and informants; test questionsHe answers test questions perfectly yet spends recklessly
Decision-making capacity (MHCA 2017, section 4)Can he make this decision, now?Ability to understand, appreciate, communicateHe may refuse a treatment others think wise and still have capacity
Clinical link

When a clinical psychologist is asked whether a patient "has insight" or "has capacity", it helps to translate the question back into the descriptive terms of this chapter. Is consciousness clear, attention held, memory working and language intact, so that he can understand? Does a delusion or a manic certainty block the step from "people with this illness relapse without treatment" to "I will relapse", so that he cannot appreciate? Can he communicate a choice? And is the question about this treatment, now, or about something else? A report built on those questions is useful to the team, fair to the patient, and consistent with the Act's starting point that capacity is presumed.

Exam trap

The three kinds of judgement are personal (own plans and decisions), social (conduct with others) and test (hypothetical problems such as the found envelope or the fire in the cinema). Intact test judgement does not mean intact judgement. Judgement fails in mania, acute psychosis, dementia, frontal lobe disease, intoxication and intellectual disability, and is distorted rather than lost in depression. Judgement (deciding and acting well) is not insight (knowing one is ill), and neither is the same as capacity, which the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 presumes (section 4) and judges by the abilities to understand, appreciate and communicate a decision; a decision others think wrong does not by itself show incapacity (section 4(3)).

This chapter began with Jaspers' insistence that the clinician first describe exactly what the patient experiences, and it ends with the patient describing himself. Insight is, in a sense, the patient's own descriptive psychopathology: his account of which of his experiences are symptoms, what kind of change has taken place in him, and what should be done about it. Every earlier heading feeds into it. A clouded consciousness cannot survey itself; a hallucination taken as real, a delusion held as fact and an elated mood felt as the truth all close off the view; an obsession resisted, a depersonalisation feared and a phantom limb known to be absent leave it open. That is why the mental status examination ends where it does, and why the vocabulary of this chapter is not a list to be memorised but a set of distinctions to be used: perception or belief, feeling or conviction, "as if" or "it is", form or content.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. Jaspers' four formal characteristics of self-awareness are activity, unity, identity and .
  2. Schneider's term Ich-Störungen, or ego disorders, refers to the experiences, not to depersonalisation.
  3. The feeling of no longer being one's normal, natural self is ; the feeling that the surroundings are unreal is derealisation.
  4. In both experiences remains intact, which the patient's "as if" reveals.
  5. The word depersonalisation was introduced in 1898 by the French philosopher and psychologist .
  6. The unconscious postural model of the body that Head and Holmes described in 1911 is the body .
  7. Insisting that one's own paralysed left arm belongs to somebody else is .
  8. In the MSE, the client's awareness and understanding of his own illness is called .
  9. A patient who describes his illness correctly but stops treatment as soon as he feels well has grade 5, or , insight.
  10. Under section 4 of the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 a person is deemed to have capacity if able to understand, or communicate a decision.

Answers: (1) boundaries, (2) passivity, (3) depersonalisation, (4) reality testing, (5) Ludovic Dugas, (6) schema, (7) somatoparaphrenia, (8) insight, (9) intellectual, (10) appreciate.

The case of Madame M., revisited

Go back to the woman who walked into a Paris police station in June 1918 to report children imprisoned in cellars, and who, by the time Joseph Capgras presented her case in January 1923, held that her husband, her daughter and almost everyone around her had been replaced by doubles. Put the questions of this chapter to her, one function at a time. Was it a disorder of perception? No. She saw the visitor accurately and offered only trifles as proof (a little mark on an ear, a thinner face, a longer moustache), so it was neither an illusion, which misperceives a real stimulus, nor a hallucination, which has no stimulus at all (Section 2.3). Was it a disorder of memory? No: her memory of her husband's face worked, which is why Capgras and Jean Reboul-Lachaux, though they still used the word "illusion", added that it was not an illusion of the senses but "the conclusion of an emotional judgement". What remains is a disorder of belief: a false conviction, held with certainty, that no proof could shift, which is a delusion (Section 2.5.1). Its content was the double; its form was a delusion; and because the familiar was declared unfamiliar, it is the hypoidentification member of Christodoulou's delusional misidentification syndromes, the picture that Joseph Lévy-Valensi named Capgras syndrome in 1929 (Section 2.6.1).

The rest of the chapter fills in her picture. Her doubles multiplied, as Section 2.6.1 says impostors can, from her husband and daughter to the concierge, the tenants, the doctors and the nurses, and the title of the 1923 paper placed them inside a chronic systematised delusion, one connected system of belief (Section 2.5.4). She acted on the belief, asking the courts for a divorce from "this double", which is why the safety of the misidentified person is part of every assessment. And where would she stand on the ladder of insight (Section 2.10.4)? She took her trouble to the police and the courts, not to a doctor. What Capgras could not explain, Hadyn Ellis and Andrew Young proposed in 1990: the conscious route that recognises a face had survived and the affective route that makes it feel familiar had failed, so that a face that looked right and felt wrong could only belong to an impostor (Section 2.6.3), and the two-factor theory adds a second fault, in the checking of beliefs, that let the explanation stand. But the explanation came nearly seventy years after the description, and it could come only because Capgras had first written down, in her own words, exactly what she experienced. Describe first, explain afterwards: that is Jaspers' method, and it is the method of every section of this chapter.

Chapter summary

2.1 What Descriptive Psychopathology Is: Jaspers, Form and Content. Descriptive psychopathology is the precise description and classification of abnormal experiences, beliefs and behaviour as the person lives them, without a theory of their cause. It grew out of Karl Jaspers' General Psychopathology (1913), written when psychiatry spoke the languages of course (Kraepelin), of the brain (Griesinger's successors) and of hidden meaning (Freud). Jaspers' phenomenology uses neutral, empathic questioning to grasp what an experience is like (static understanding) and how it grows from what went before (genetic understanding); what cannot be understood points to a process. Its central distinction is form, the kind of experience, versus content, what it is about: culture shapes content, form carries the diagnosis. The section also sorts the senses of primary and secondary, sign and symptom, syndrome, pathognomonic, organic and functional, ego-syntonic and ego-dystonic, and descriptive versus dynamic psychopathology.

2.2 Disorders of Consciousness, Attention and Orientation. Consciousness has a level (arousal) and a content. Above normal lies hypervigilance; below it come clouding, the mildest lowering and the core of delirium, then drowsiness, obtundation, stupor and coma, and every other finding is read in the light of the level. Delirium is a disturbance of attention and awareness with an acute onset, a fluctuating course often worse at night, a further cognitive change and a physical cause; its hypoactive form is mistaken for depression. The qualitative changes include the twilight state of epilepsy and Mayer-Gross's oneiroid state. Psychiatric stupor often preserves consciousness, unlike organic stupor, and must be told from akinetic mutism and the locked-in syndrome. Distractibility marks mania and inattention delirium. Orientation is lost for time first, then place, then person; marked disorientation means an organic state, while lost identity with intact time and place points to dissociation.

2.3 Disorders of Perception: Distortions, Illusions and Hallucinations. Sensory distortions change a real perception (hyperacusis, the xanthopsia of digoxin, micropsia); sensory deceptions are new perceptions. An illusion misperceives a real stimulus (completion, affect and pareidolic illusions). A hallucination, which Esquirol first separated from illusion, is a percept without a stimulus, as forceful as a real one and involuntary (Slade and Bentall) and, Jaspers added, located in outer space: a disorder of perception, not of thinking, judgement or intelligence. The pseudohallucination has two meanings. Each sense points somewhere: third-person voices and running commentary to schizophrenia, visual hallucinations to organic states and Lewy body dementia, smells and tastes to temporal lobe epilepsy, formication to cocaine. Special types include functional, reflex, extracampine, autoscopic and Lilliputian hallucinations. Hypnagogic hallucinations are commonest in normal people (37 percent in Ohayon's survey), and Charles Bonnet syndrome is complex visual hallucinations with visual loss and preserved insight.

2.4 Disorders of Thought and Speech: Stream, Form and Possession. Thought is known only through speech, as Andreasen stressed, and the British headings ask four questions: stream (how fast and continuous), form (how ideas are linked), content (what is thought) and possession (whose the thoughts feel). Flight of ideas, a rapid shift between ideas linked by chance, rhyme and clang, marks mania; retardation and poverty of thought mark depression and negative states; thought block is an observed sudden stop; perseveration points to frontal disease. Bleuler's loosening of associations, knight's move thinking and derailment belong to schizophrenia; tangentiality never reaches the goal, while circumstantiality finally does. Word salad is the most severe formal thought disorder. Cameron described overinclusive thinking and Goldstein concrete thinking. Speech disorders, from dysarthria to Ganser's approximate answers, are excluded first. Thought insertion, withdrawal and broadcasting are disorders of possession and, with made experiences, Schneider's first-rank symptoms.

2.5 Delusions and the Other Disorders of Thought Content. A delusion is a false belief, firmly held, not amenable to reason or contrary evidence and out of keeping with the person's background; of Jaspers' marks (certainty, incorrigibility, impossibility of content) falsity is the weakest. Primary delusions have no understandable origin: delusional mood, delusional perception (first-rank), the sudden delusional idea and delusional memory. Secondary delusions grow out of a mood, a hallucination, a passivity experience, another delusion or an impairment. Delusions are described as systematised or fragmentary, mood-congruent or incongruent, bizarre or non-bizarre, and by what they make the person do. Persecutory delusions are the commonest, above all in schizophrenia and delusional disorder; grandiose delusions mark mania, and guilt, poverty and nihilism severe depression. Wernicke's overvalued idea is understandable and less fixed, the obsession is recognised as one's own and resisted, and phobias, preoccupations and magical thinking complete thought content.

2.6 The Named Syndromes: Misidentification, Cotard, Othello and the Other Eponyms. The eponyms are descriptive syndromes found inside many disorders. Christodoulou grouped the delusional misidentification syndromes: Capgras syndrome (1923, named by Lévy-Valensi in 1929), a familiar person replaced by an identical impostor; Fregoli syndrome (Courbon and Fail, 1927), strangers as one familiar persecutor in disguise; intermetamorphosis; subjective doubles; and Pick's reduplicative paramnesia (1903) for places. Ellis and Young (1990) explained Capgras as the mirror image of prosopagnosia, recognition intact but the affective response lost; Coltheart's two-factor theory adds faulty belief evaluation. Cotard (1880) described nihilistic delusions of being dead or without organs, usually in severe psychotic depression. Othello syndrome (Todd and Dewhurst, 1955) is delusional jealousy, linked to alcohol dependence and dangerous to the partner. De Clérambault described erotomania (1921) and Ekbom delusional parasitosis (1938). Couvade and Diogenes syndromes are not delusions.

2.7 Disorders of Memory: Amnesia and the Paramnesias. Amnesia is a pathological loss of memory: anterograde or retrograde (by Ribot's law, recent before remote), lacunar, post-traumatic or transient global, the organic amnesia of Korsakoff syndrome (1887), and dissociative amnesia, in which identity can be lost first. Hypermnesia is memory too vivid. Paramnesias distort recall or recognition. Confabulation, in Berlyne's definition a falsification of memory in clear consciousness with an organic amnesia, fills gaps without intent to deceive, above all in Korsakoff syndrome; Kopelman's provoked and spontaneous forms renamed Bonhoeffer's two. Pseudologia fantastica (Delbrück, 1891) is lying the teller half believes, retrospective falsification bends the past to the present, and cryptomnesia returns a memory as an original idea. Déjà vu, the illusion of recognition, is common in health and pathological in temporal lobe epilepsy; déjà entendu is the illusion of having heard before, and jamais vu the familiar felt as new.

2.8 Disorders of Emotion: Mood, Affect and Their Vocabulary. Mood is the sustained, reported emotional climate; affect is the immediate, observed weather, so the emotion shown at this moment is affect. Mood is described by quality (depressed, elevated, expansive, irritable, anxious, dysphoric, euthymic), reactivity and diurnal variation, morning worsening marking melancholia. Elevated mood rises from euphoria through elation and exaltation to ecstasy. Anhedonia (Ribot, 1896), the loss of pleasure or interest, is a cardinal symptom of depression and a negative symptom of schizophrenia. Apathy (Marin), alexithymia (Sifneos, 1973) and Bleuler's ambivalence are different things. Affect has the dimensions of quality, range, intensity, reactivity, appropriateness, stability and communicability, while persistence describes mood; restricted, blunted and flat affect form a ladder. Lability, emotional incontinence and pseudobulbar affect, sudden involuntary laughing or crying out of keeping with mood after stroke or in multiple sclerosis, ALS, brain injury and dementia, complete the section.

2.9 Disorders of Movement and Will. Volition turns intention into action; a drug cause is excluded before any movement is called psychiatric. Psychomotor retardation and agitation count in DSM-5-TR only when observable by others, and agitation is not akathisia. Kahlbaum named catatonia in 1874; DSM-5-TR requires three of twelve signs, and Abrams and Taylor (1976) found it commoner in mood disorders than in schizophrenia. Echolalia repeats others' words and echopraxia imitates their movements; Nirmala showed the older signs (automatic obedience, Mitgehen, Gegenhalten, ambitendency) and their response to lorazepam, and catalepsy is not cataplexy. A mannerism is a goal-directed act done oddly, a stereotypy has no goal, a tic is sudden, non-rhythmic and preceded by an urge, and a compulsion answers an obsession. Avolition is a negative symptom, abulia a frontal or basal ganglia loss of will, and Lhermitte's imitation behaviour shows the will released by frontal damage.

2.10 The Self, the Body, Insight and Judgement. Jaspers described four formal characteristics of self-awareness: activity, unity, identity and boundaries. Depersonalisation (the self felt unreal, a word Dugas introduced in 1898) and derealisation (the world felt unreal) keep reality testing intact, and the patient's "as if" separates them from passivity and from Cotard's delusion. The body schema (Head and Holmes, 1911) and body image (Schilder, 1935) frame the phantom limb, anosognosia (Babinski, 1914), somatoparaphrenia (Gerstmann, 1942) and autotopagnosia, while in anorexia nervosa it is mainly attitudinal. Insight in the mental status examination is the person's awareness and understanding of his own illness and need for treatment (Aubrey Lewis, 1934), graded from complete denial to true emotional insight; it is kept where symptoms are ego-dystonic and lost in acute psychosis and mania. Judgement is personal, social or test, and under the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 both feed into decision-making capacity.
