From the past papers

  1. Symptoms where a person believes that other people are out to get them and that he or she is being attacked by rays from outer space. These patients are experiencing: (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) Depression (b) Paranoia (c) Dissociative disorder (d) Anxiety disorder

    Answer: (b) Paranoia. The belief that others are plotting against one and attacking one is a persecutory delusion, the hallmark of paranoia and paranoid states (Section 2.5.5). Depression, dissociative disorder and anxiety disorder do not, as such, produce persecutory delusions; when persecution appears in a psychotic depression it is often felt to be deserved.

  2. "Hallucination" is a disorder of: (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) Judgement (b) Perception (c) Intelligence (d) Thinking

    Answer: (b) Perception. A hallucination is a perception without an external stimulus, so it is a disorder of perception (Section 2.3.3). "Thinking" is the bait: the disorder of belief is the delusion, and judgement and intelligence can be intact, as in the old man with failing sight who knows that the children on his bed are not real.

  3. 'Nihilistic delusions' are commonly seen in: (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) Depression (b) Schizophrenia (c) Obsessive Compulsive Disorder (d) Somatoform disorder

    Answer: (a) Depression. Nihilistic delusions (that oneself, one's organs or the world do not exist or are dead) belong above all to severe psychotic depression, and Cotard syndrome is their extreme (Sections 2.5.5 and 2.6.4). They can occur in schizophrenia, but depression is the keyed and best answer; obsessions are resisted rather than believed, and somatoform disorder has bodily preoccupation without delusional conviction.

  4. A patient presents with a fixed delusional belief that their spouse is being unfaithful, despite having no evidence. This specific form of delusional jealousy is known as: (Real PYQ 2025)

    (a) Capgras syndrome (b) Othello syndrome (c) Fregoli syndrome (d) De Clérambault's syndrome

    Answer: (b) Othello syndrome. Othello syndrome (Todd and Dewhurst, 1955) is the delusional form of morbid jealousy (Section 2.6.5). Capgras is a familiar person replaced by an impostor, Fregoli is strangers believed to be one familiar persecutor in disguise, and de Clérambault's is erotomania, the delusion of being loved.

  5. Which of the following phenomena is characterized by the "illusion of recognition," where a person feels a new situation has been experienced before? (Real PYQ 2025)

    (a) Jamais vu (b) Pseudologia fantastica (c) Déjà vu (d) Confabulation

    Answer: (c) Déjà vu. Déjà vu is the illusion of recognition, a distortion of the feeling of familiarity (Section 2.7.7). Jamais vu is its opposite, the familiar felt as unfamiliar, while pseudologia fantastica (Delbrück, 1891) and confabulation are distortions of recall, not of recognition.

  6. Cotard syndrome, a rare neuropsychiatric condition, is primarily characterized by which type of delusional belief? (Real PYQ 2025)

    (a) Grandiose delusions of having special powers or abilities (b) Persecutory delusions of being followed or monitored (c) Nihilistic delusions of being dead or not existing (d) Somatic delusions of having a serious medical illness

    Answer: (c) Nihilistic delusions of being dead or not existing. Jules Cotard (1880) described the délire des négations: the belief that one is dead, does not exist, or has lost one's organs or blood (Section 2.6.4). The somatic option is the near miss, because the Cotard patient believes the organs are gone or dead, not diseased; special powers are grandiose and being followed is persecutory.

  7. Delusion of infidelity on part of the sexual partner is known as: (RML 2024)

    (a) Ekbom's syndrome (b) Othello syndrome (c) de Clérambault's syndrome (d) Couvade syndrome

    Answer: (b) Othello syndrome. Delusional jealousy is Othello syndrome, classically associated with alcohol dependence (Section 2.6.5). Ekbom syndrome is delusional parasitosis (and, separately, restless legs), de Clérambault's is erotomania, and Couvade syndrome is pregnancy-like symptoms in an expectant father, which is not a delusion at all.

  8. Hypnagogic hallucinations most commonly occur in: (RML 2024)

    (a) Schizophrenia (b) Delirium (c) Drug dependence (d) Normal

    Answer: (d) Normal. Hallucinations while falling asleep are commonest in healthy people: Ohayon's survey (1996) found them in 37 percent, and DSM-5-TR counts them as normal (Section 2.3.6). Narcolepsy is the disorder that features them; schizophrenia, delirium and drug dependence are wrong because the stem asks where they are most common, not where they can occur.

  9. Our current mood and feelings are best described by the term: (RML 2024)

    (a) Emotion (b) Mood (c) Affect (d) Feeling

    Answer: (c) Affect. Affect is the immediate, observed expression of emotion here and now, the weather, while mood is the sustained state over days or weeks, the climate (Section 2.8.1). "Mood" is the bait because the word is in the stem; an emotion is a brief reaction to a particular event, and a feeling is the experienced side of an emotion.

  10. False belief which cannot be shaken by reason is termed as: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Illusion (b) Delusion (c) Hallucination (d) False memory

    Answer: (b) Delusion. A delusion is a false belief, firmly held, not amenable to reason or contrary evidence and out of keeping with the person's background: a disorder of thought content (Section 2.5.1). An illusion and a hallucination are disorders of perception, and a false memory is an error of memory.

  11. Amnesia is a clinical condition of: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Pathological loss of memory (b) Behavioral disturbance (c) Depressed affect (d) Impaired perception

    Answer: (a) Pathological loss of memory. Amnesia is defined by what is missing from memory (Section 2.7.1). A person with amnesia may also be agitated, low in mood or misperceiving, which is why the distractors tempt, but those belong to behaviour, emotion and perception.

  12. Delusional beliefs of an individual that he is being deliberately interfered with, discriminated against, plotted against, threatened, or otherwise mistreated is called delusion of: (IHBAS 2024)

    (a) Reference (b) Persecution (c) Control (d) Grandiosity

    Answer: (b) Persecution. Being harmed, plotted against or mistreated is persecution, the commonest of all delusions (Section 2.5.5). Reference is the belief that unrelated events refer to oneself, control the belief that an outside force directs one's acts, and grandiosity the belief in one's exceptional abilities, wealth or fame.

  13. Choose the correct arrangement of these stages of elevated mood based upon severity: i) Elation, ii) Exaltation, iii) Euphoria, iv) Ecstasy. (Amity 2023)

    (a) i, ii, iii and iv (b) iii, i, ii and iv (c) iv, iii, ii and i (d) None of the above

    Answer: (b) iii, i, ii and iv. From mildest to most severe the ladder is euphoria, elation (which adds activity), exaltation (which adds grandiosity) and ecstasy (which adds rapture) (Section 2.8.3). Option (a) starts with elation and (c) reverses the order; ecstasy, not euphoria, is the extreme.

  14. Illusions are: (Amity 2023)

    (a) Distortions of existing stimuli (b) The same as hallucinations (c) The result of innate mechanisms (d) Not based on external reality

    Answer: (a) Distortions of existing stimuli. An illusion is a misperception of a real external stimulus (Section 2.3.2). "Not based on external reality" describes a hallucination, so (b) and (d) are wrong by definition, and (c) is not a definition at all: illusions arise from normal perceptual processes that learning shapes.

  15. A patient with Korsakoff's psychosis who fabricates detailed and believable stories about their past to fill memory gaps, without the intention to deceive, is exhibiting: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Confabulation (b) Confusion (c) Delirium (d) Malingering

    Answer: (a) Confabulation. Confabulation is a falsification of memory in clear consciousness with an organic amnesia (Berlyne, 1972), given without intent to deceive (Section 2.7.5). Confusion and delirium are disturbances of consciousness, which that definition excludes, and malingering is the deliberate faking of symptoms for an external gain.

  16. Déjà entendu refers to: (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) Illusion of having seen something before (b) Memory of past events with clarity (c) Illusion of having heard something previously (d) Repetitive involuntary speech

    Answer: (c) Illusion of having heard something previously. Entendu means heard (Section 2.7.7). Having seen something before is déjà vu, memory of past events with unusual clarity is hypermnesia, and repetitive involuntary speech is a speech sign such as palilalia or verbigeration.

  17. Delusion of persecution is most commonly seen in: (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) OCD (b) Bipolar disorder (c) Schizophrenia (d) Panic disorder

    Answer: (c) Schizophrenia. Persecutory delusions are found above all in schizophrenia, where they are the most frequent delusion, and in delusional disorder (Section 2.5.5). They also occur in mania, which makes bipolar disorder tempting, but "most commonly" decides it; they are not features of obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD) or panic disorder.

  18. Circumstantiality is a disorder of: (Recall set)

    (a) Possession of thought (b) Content of thought (c) Form of thought (thought process) (d) Perception

    Answer: (c) Form of thought (thought process). Circumstantiality is the gentlest disorder of form: a long, over-detailed route that finally reaches the goal, unlike tangentiality, which never does (Section 2.4.4). It says nothing about what is believed (content), the thoughts feel entirely the person's own (possession), and nothing is misperceived.

High-yield sheet

The facts this chapter's questions ask most, most-asked first. Last read before the exam.

  1. Capgras syndrome is the delusion that a familiar person, usually the spouse, has been replaced by an identical impostor (Capgras and Reboul-Lachaux, 1923, "l'illusion des sosies"); its inverse, Fregoli syndrome (Courbon and Fail, 1927), is the belief that strangers are one familiar persecutor in disguise. asked 6 times
  2. Persecutory delusions, the belief that one is being deliberately interfered with, plotted against, threatened or mistreated, are the commonest delusions, the hallmark of paranoia, and are seen most often in schizophrenia and delusional disorder. asked 3 times
  3. Othello syndrome (Todd and Dewhurst, 1955) is delusional jealousy, the fixed belief without evidence that the partner is unfaithful, classically with alcohol dependence; de Clérambault syndrome is erotomania and Ekbom syndrome delusional parasitosis. asked 3 times
  4. Cotard syndrome (Jules Cotard, 1880; délire des négations, 1882) is the nihilistic delusion that one is dead, does not exist, or has lost one's organs or blood. asked 3 times
  5. Déjà vu is the illusion of recognition (a new situation feels already experienced); déjà entendu is the illusion of having heard something before; jamais vu is the familiar felt as unfamiliar. asked 2 times
  6. Anhedonia, the word Ribot coined in 1896, is the inability to experience pleasure or interest in activities once enjoyed, a cardinal symptom of major depression. asked 3 times
  7. Confabulation is the falsification of memory in clear consciousness with an organic amnesia, filling gaps with detailed, believable accounts without intent to deceive, above all in Korsakoff syndrome; it is not confusion, delirium or malingering. asked 2 times
  8. Tangentiality, derailment, loosening of associations and circumstantiality are disorders of the form (process) of thought, not of its content; circumstantiality finally reaches the goal, tangentiality never does. asked 2 times
  9. Affect is the immediate, observed expression of emotion (the weather) and mood the sustained, reported state (the climate); quality, range and reactivity describe affect, while persistence describes mood. asked 2 times
  10. Word salad (incoherence), in which even the grammar fails, is the most severe formal thought disorder; it is not a negative symptom. asked 2 times
  11. Charles Bonnet syndrome is complex visual hallucinations in a cognitively intact person with visual loss and preserved insight (Bonnet, 1760; named by de Morsier, 1967). asked 2 times
  12. Pseudobulbar affect is sudden, brief, involuntary laughing or crying out of proportion to or incongruent with the mood, in stroke, multiple sclerosis, ALS, brain injury and dementia. asked 2 times
  13. A hallucination is a perception without an external stimulus, so it is a disorder of perception, not of thinking, judgement or intelligence. asked once
  14. Hypnagogic hallucinations (while falling asleep) occur most commonly in normal people, 37 percent of adults in Ohayon's 1996 survey; hypnopompic ones occur on waking. asked once
  15. A delusion is a false belief, firmly held, not amenable to reason or contrary evidence and out of keeping with the person's educational, cultural and social background. asked once
  16. Amnesia is a pathological loss of memory, not a disturbance of behaviour, affect or perception. asked once
  17. Elevated mood graded from mildest to most severe: euphoria, elation, exaltation, ecstasy. asked once
  18. Illusions are misperceptions of real, existing stimuli; a hallucination has no stimulus at all. asked once
  19. Nihilistic delusions are seen most characteristically in severe psychotic depression, especially in older people. asked once
  20. Loosening of associations (Bleuler) and knight's move thinking are formal thought disorder, characteristic of schizophrenia. asked once
  21. Insight in the mental status examination is the person's awareness and understanding of his own illness and need for treatment; it is not orientation, serial subtraction or the presence of hallucinations. asked once
  22. Ego-dystonic symptoms, obsessions above all, are experienced as intrusive, unwanted and inconsistent with one's self-image; ego-syntonic ones feel consistent with it. asked once
  23. Flight of ideas is a rapid shift from one idea to another before any is completed, with links (chance associations, rhymes, puns, clangs) that can still be followed; it marks mania. asked once
  24. Thought broadcasting is the experience that one's thoughts pass out of the mind so that others know them; in thought withdrawal they are taken out, in thought insertion alien thoughts are put in. asked once
  25. Echolalia (repeating others' words) and echopraxia (imitating others' movements) are signs of catatonia. asked once
